<a id='question-101'></a>

### Question 101: Genomic Imprinting: Prader-Willi Syndrome
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Genomic imprinting is an epigenetic phenomenon where certain genes are expressed in a parent-of-origin-specific manner, leading to monoallelic expression. Deletions or mutations affecting imprinted genes on the paternal chromosome 15q11-q13 region can cause Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS), characterized by neonatal hypotonia, feeding difficulties, developmental delay, hyperphagia, obesity, and hypogonadism. The maternal allele for these genes is normally silenced via methylation.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy is brought to the pediatrician by his parents due to concerns about his development and eating habits. He was born full-term but exhibited significant hypotonia and poor suckling reflex in the neonatal period, requiring feeding support via nasogastric tube for the first 6 months. His motor milestones have been delayed; he is not yet walking independently. His parents report that over the past year, he has developed an insatiable appetite (hyperphagia) and is constantly seeking food, leading to rapid weight gain and obesity. He also has characteristic facial features, including almond-shaped eyes and a narrow forehead. His older sister is developmentally normal. Karyotyping reveals a normal 46,XY chromosome complement. Fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH) analysis using a probe specific for the SNRPN gene locus on chromosome 15q11-q13 shows that the paternal allele is absent in the patient's cells, while the maternal allele is present and appears normal. Methylation analysis of the 15q11-q13 region reveals hypermethylation on the maternal allele and hypomethylation on the paternal allele (where present).

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) A mutation in the maternal copy of the *UBE3A* gene, leading to loss of function of the maternal allele, which is normally expressed in the brain.
(B) A deletion of the paternal chromosome 15q11-q13 region, encompassing several imprinted genes, including *SNRPN*, *NDN*, and *MAGP1*, which are normally expressed only from the paternal allele in this region.
(C) A translocation involving chromosome 21, leading to trisomy 21 (Down syndrome), which can sometimes present with hypotonia and developmental delay.
(D) A mutation in the *FMR1* gene, causing fragile X syndrome, characterized by intellectual disability, characteristic facial features, and macroorchidism.
(E) A uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 21, where the individual inherits two copies of chromosome 21 from the mother and none from the father, leading to an increased risk of Alzheimer's disease later in life.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with neonatal hypotonia, poor suckling, developmental delay, hyperphagia, obesity, and characteristic facial features. This constellation of symptoms is highly suggestive of Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS).
- **Family History:** Older sister is normal, suggesting a *de novo* event or a condition not typically inherited in a simple Mendelian pattern (like imprinting disorders or UPD).
- **Karyotype:** Normal 46,XY rules out aneuploidies like Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) or Turner syndrome.
- **FISH Analysis:** Absence of the paternal allele for the *SNRPN* gene (located in the 15q11-q13 region) indicates a loss of genetic material from the paternal chromosome 15. The presence of the maternal allele confirms that the maternal chromosome 15 is intact.
- **Methylation Analysis:** Hypermethylation on the maternal allele and hypomethylation on the paternal allele (where present) is the hallmark epigenetic modification characteristic of the 15q11-q13 imprinted region. Normally, the maternal allele is heavily methylated, leading to its transcriptional silencing, while the paternal allele is unmethylated and actively transcribed. The finding of hypomethylation on the paternal allele (where present) indicates that the paternal copy is not silenced as it should be, but the FISH result shows it's absent. This confirms the loss of the *paternally derived* genetic material in this region.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Genomic Imprinting:** This is an epigenetic process where gene expression depends on the parent of origin. Specific regions of DNA are marked (usually by methylation) during gametogenesis, leading to differential expression of alleles inherited from the mother versus the father.
- **Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS) Locus:** The critical region for PWS is 15q11-q13. Several genes within this region are subject to imprinting. Key genes include *SNRPN* (Small Nuclear Ribonucleoprotein N), *NDN* (Neuropeptide N), *MAGP1* (Meiotic Associated Gamete Protein 1), *MKBPBP* (Myelin Basic Protein Binding Protein), and *IPW* (Imprinting Protein W).
- **Paternal Requirement:** For PWS to occur, the *paternally derived* alleles of the imprinted genes in the 15q11-q13 region must be non-functional or absent. This can happen through several mechanisms:
    1.  **Deletion:** Loss of the paternal chromosome 15q11-q13 region (most common cause, ~70% of cases).
    2.  **Maternal Uniparental Disomy (mUPD):** Inheritance of two copies of chromosome 15 from the mother and none from the father (~25% of cases). In this case, both copies are maternally derived and silenced by methylation.
    3.  **Imprinting Defect:** Failure of methylation on the paternal allele, leading to silencing of both paternal and maternal alleles (~5% of cases).
- **Molecular Mechanism in this Case:** The FISH analysis specifically shows the absence of the paternal *SNRPN* allele, indicating a deletion of the paternal 15q11-q13 region. The methylation pattern confirms the normal imprinting status (maternal allele hypermethylated/silenced, paternal allele hypomethylated/active *if present*). The combination of clinical features, FISH showing paternal deletion, and methylation analysis points definitively to a deletion of the paternally inherited 15q11-q13 region. The genes within this region (*SNRPN*, *NDN*, *MAGP1*, etc.) are normally expressed *only* from the paternal allele due to imprinting, and their absence leads to PWS.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** This describes Angelman syndrome (AS). *UBE3A* is located in the 15q11-q13 region, but it is subject to *maternal* imprinting. In AS, the *maternal* allele is either deleted, mutated, or silenced (e.g., by mUPD or imprinting defect), leading to loss of function of the maternal *UBE3A*. The clinical features of AS (severe developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, happy demeanor) are distinct from PWS. The vignette describes loss of the *paternal* allele, not the maternal *UBE3A* allele.
- (C): **Incorrect.** This describes Down syndrome (Trisomy 21). While Down syndrome can present with hypotonia and developmental delay, the characteristic features (e.g., upslanting palpebral fissures, single palmar crease, intellectual disability profile) and the specific hyperphagia/obesity pattern are different from PWS. The karyotype was normal (46,XY), ruling out Trisomy 21.
- (D): **Incorrect.** This describes Fragile X syndrome. It is caused by a CGG repeat expansion in the *FMR1* gene on the X chromosome. Clinical features include intellectual disability, characteristic facial features (long face, large ears, prominent jaw), macroorchidism (post-pubertal), and behavioral issues. The genetic mechanism and clinical presentation are distinct from PWS. The patient is male (46,XY), but the vignette points specifically to a chromosome 15 defect.
- (E): **Incorrect.** This describes a potential consequence of uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 21, specifically paternal UPD. While UPD can cause imprinting disorders (like PWS if it's mUPD 15 or AS if it's pUPD 15), UPD of chromosome 21 itself is rare and primarily associated with an increased risk of Alzheimer's disease later in life due to the inheritance of two copies of the *APP* gene (Amyloid Precursor Protein) from the same parent, potentially leading to overexpression. The vignette describes a deletion of the paternal chromosome 15, not UPD of chromosome 21.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Understand the concept of genomic imprinting and its role in specific syndromes like Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS) and Angelman syndrome (AS), both located in the 15q11-q13 region. Recognize that PWS results from loss of function of *paternally expressed* genes in this region, while AS results from loss of function of the *maternally expressed* *UBE3A* gene in the same region.
- **Differentials:** Key differentials for PWS include Angelman syndrome (though clinical features are distinct), other causes of neonatal hypotonia and developmental delay, and conditions causing hyperphagia and obesity. Understanding the specific genetic and epigenetic mechanisms is crucial for diagnosis.

<a id='question-102'></a>

### Question 102: Angelman Syndrome vs. Prader-Willi Syndrome
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Genomic imprinting in the 15q11-q13 region, specifically the differential methylation patterns and gene expression of *UBE3A*, *SNRPN*, and *MAGEL2* leading to distinct clinical syndromes (Angelman syndrome vs. Prader-Willi syndrome).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old girl presents with severe developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, and frequent episodes of inappropriate laughter (gelastic seizures). She has minimal speech and exhibits microcephaly. Her parents report that she was hypotonic at birth. Her father has a history of obesity, hyperphagia, and mild intellectual disability, diagnosed in childhood as Prader-Willi syndrome. Karyotyping of the patient reveals a normal female karyotype (46,XX). Methylation analysis of the 15q11-q13 region is performed. The results show that the maternal allele for *UBE3A* is methylated, while the paternal allele is unmethylated. Conversely, the maternal alleles for *SNRPN* and *MAGEL2* are unmethylated, while the paternal alleles are methylated. Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Deletion of the paternal 15q11-q13 region, leading to loss of the unmethylated *UBE3A* allele and loss of the methylated *SNRPN* and *MAGEL2* alleles.
(B) Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15, resulting in two copies of the methylated *UBE3A* allele and two copies of the unmethylated *SNRPN* and *MAGEL2* alleles.
(C) A mutation in the *UBE3A* gene on the paternal chromosome, leading to a non-functional protein despite being unmethylated.
(D) A translocation involving chromosome 15, disrupting the *SNRPN* gene on the paternal chromosome and leading to its silencing.
(E) A mutation in the *SNRPN* gene on the maternal chromosome, leading to a non-functional protein despite being unmethylated.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old girl with severe developmental delay, ataxia, seizures (including gelastic seizures), inappropriate laughter, minimal speech, microcephaly, and neonatal hypotonia. This constellation of symptoms strongly suggests Angelman syndrome.
- **Family History:** Father diagnosed with Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS), characterized by neonatal hypotonia, hyperphagia, obesity, and intellectual disability. This points towards a genetic defect in the 15q11-q13 region, which is associated with both syndromes.
- **Karyotype:** Normal 46,XX, ruling out large chromosomal abnormalities like deletions or translocations that would be visible cytogenetically.
- **Methylation Analysis:** This is the key piece of information.
    - *UBE3A*: Maternal allele methylated, paternal allele unmethylated. This pattern is characteristic of Angelman syndrome (AS). *UBE3A* is normally expressed *only* from the paternal allele in the brain due to imprinting. Methylation silences the maternal *UBE3A* allele. In AS, if the paternal *UBE3A* is missing or non-functional, the methylated maternal allele cannot be expressed, leading to a complete loss of UBE3A protein in the brain.
    - *SNRPN* and *MAGEL2*: Maternal alleles unmethylated, paternal alleles methylated. This pattern is characteristic of PWS. *SNRPN* and *MAGEL2* are normally expressed *only* from the paternal allele in the brain due to imprinting. Methylation silences the maternal *SNRPN* and *MAGEL2* alleles. In PWS, if the paternal *SNRPN* and *MAGEL2* are missing or non-functional, the unmethylated maternal alleles cannot compensate, leading to a loss of function.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the *patient's* clinical presentation (Angelman syndrome).

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Genomic Imprinting:** A phenomenon where certain genes are expressed in a parent-of-origin-specific manner. This occurs due to epigenetic modifications, primarily DNA methylation, established during gametogenesis.
- **15q11-q13 Region:** This region on chromosome 15 contains several imprinted genes, including *UBE3A* (involved in protein degradation via the ubiquitin-proteasome system), *SNRPN* (involved in splicing), and *MAGEL2* (function less well-defined, but involved in development).
- **Normal Expression:** In the brain, *UBE3A* is expressed *only* from the paternal allele because the maternal allele is heavily methylated and silenced. *SNRPN* and *MAGEL2* are expressed *only* from the paternal allele because the maternal alleles are unmethylated and active, while the paternal alleles are methylated and silenced.
- **Angelman Syndrome (AS):** Caused by the loss of function of the *paternally* inherited *UBE3A* allele. This can occur through several mechanisms:
    1.  Deletion of the paternal 15q11-q13 region (most common, ~70%).
    2.  Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15 (mUPD15) (~3-7%). The patient inherits two copies of chromosome 15 from the mother, both carrying the methylated *UBE3A* allele.
    3.  Imprinting defect (ID) (~3-7%). The paternal *UBE3A* allele is methylated, silencing it, despite the normal methylation pattern of *SNRPN* and *MAGEL2*.
    4.  Mutation in the *UBE3A* gene on the paternal chromosome (~10-15%).
- **Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS):** Caused by the loss of function of the *paternally* inherited genes in the 15q11-q13 region, primarily *SNRPN* and *MAGEL2*. This can occur through:
    1.  Deletion of the paternal 15q11-q13 region (most common, ~70%).
    2.  Maternal uniparental disomy (mUPD15) (~25%). The patient inherits two copies of chromosome 15 from the mother, both carrying the unmethylated *SNRPN* and *MAGEL2* alleles, and the methylated *UBE3A* allele.
    3.  Imprinting defect (ID) (~1-2%). The paternal alleles of *SNRPN* and *MAGEL2* are methylated, silencing them, despite the normal methylation pattern of *UBE3A*.
- **Patient's Case:** The patient presents with AS symptoms. The methylation analysis confirms the typical AS pattern: maternal *UBE3A* methylated, paternal *UBE3A* unmethylated; maternal *SNRPN/MAGEL2* unmethylated, paternal *SNRPN/MAGEL2* methylated. This pattern is consistent with maternal UPD (mUPD15), where the patient inherited two copies of chromosome 15 from her mother. Both maternal copies would have the methylated *UBE3A* allele (silenced) and the unmethylated *SNRPN/MAGEL2* alleles (active). The paternal chromosome 15 is missing, so there is no unmethylated paternal *UBE3A* allele to compensate, leading to AS. The father's PWS is consistent with a paternal deletion or mUPD15.

##### 3. Connecting Vignette to Correct Answer (B)
The patient's clinical presentation (AS) and the specific methylation pattern described (maternal *UBE3A* methylated, paternal *UBE3A* unmethylated; maternal *SNRPN/MAGEL2* unmethylated, paternal *SNRPN/MAGEL2* methylated) are diagnostic of maternal uniparental disomy for chromosome 15 (mUPD15). This mechanism leads to the loss of the normally expressed paternal *UBE3A* allele (due to the maternal methylated allele being inherited twice) and the retention of the normally silenced maternal *SNRPN/MAGEL2* alleles (due to the maternal unmethylated alleles being inherited twice).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Deletion of the paternal 15q11-q13 region would cause PWS, not AS. The methylation pattern would also be different: maternal *UBE3A* methylated, paternal *UBE3A* deleted (or methylated if an imprinting defect); maternal *SNRPN/MAGEL2* unmethylated, paternal *SNRPN/MAGEL2* deleted (or methylated if an imprinting defect). This contradicts the patient's AS phenotype and methylation results.
- (C): A mutation in the *UBE3A* gene on the paternal chromosome would also cause AS. However, the methylation pattern described (maternal *UBE3A* methylated, paternal *UBE3A* unmethylated) is *not* typical for a paternal *UBE3A* mutation. A paternal *UBE3A* mutation would typically have a normal methylation pattern (maternal methylated, paternal unmethylated) but would result in a non-functional protein. The described methylation pattern is characteristic of mUPD15 or an imprinting defect. While a paternal mutation *could* occur, the provided methylation data points specifically to mUPD15 as the underlying cause matching the pattern.
- (D): A translocation disrupting the *SNRPN* gene on the paternal chromosome would cause PWS. The methylation pattern would be consistent with PWS (maternal *UBE3A* methylated, paternal *UBE3A* unmethylated; maternal *SNRPN/MAGEL2* unmethylated, paternal *SNRPN/MAGEL2* methylated or disrupted). This contradicts the patient's AS phenotype.
- (E): A mutation in the *SNRPN* gene on the maternal chromosome would cause PWS if the paternal allele was also deleted or UPD. The methylation pattern would be consistent with PWS (maternal *UBE3A* methylated, paternal *UBE3A* unmethylated; maternal *SNRPN/MAGEL2* unmethylated, paternal *SNRPN/MAGEL2* methylated or disrupted). This contradicts the patient's AS phenotype.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Genomic imprinting in the 15q11-q13 region leads to distinct syndromes based on the parent-of-origin expression of *UBE3A*, *SNRPN*, and *MAGEL2*. Angelman syndrome results from loss of paternal *UBE3A* expression, while Prader-Willi syndrome results from loss of paternal *SNRPN* and *MAGEL2* expression. Methylation analysis is crucial for diagnosis, differentiating between deletion, UPD, and imprinting defects. Key differentials include other causes of developmental delay, ataxia, and seizures (e.g., Rett syndrome, other genetic syndromes).

<a id='question-103'></a>

### Question 103: Prader-Willi Syndrome Mechanisms
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the genetic mechanisms underlying Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS), specifically genomic imprinting, paternal deletion, maternal uniparental disomy (UPD), and imprinting center defects. (理解Prader-Willi综合征 (PWS) 的遗传机制，特别是基因组印记、父本缺失、母本单亲二体 (UPD) 和印记中心缺陷。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy is brought to the pediatrician by his parents due to concerns about developmental delay, hypotonia, and excessive eating. The child was born full-term but exhibited poor suckling and feeding difficulties in infancy, requiring specialized formula and feeding support. He has significant hypotonia, particularly in the trunk, leading to delayed motor milestones like sitting and walking. His parents report that he has an insatiable appetite ("hyperphagia") and is constantly seeking food, leading to rapid weight gain and obesity. Physical examination reveals short stature, small hands and feet, characteristic facial features (narrow forehead, almond-shaped eyes, thin upper lip), and hypogonadism (small penis, undescended testes). Developmental assessment shows moderate intellectual disability. Karyotyping reveals a normal male chromosome complement (46,XY). Fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH) analysis using a probe specific for the SNRPN gene locus on chromosome 15q11-q13 shows that the paternal allele is absent in the patient's cells, while the maternal allele is present.

Which of the following genetic mechanisms is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15q11-q13.
(B) A translocation involving chromosome 15q11-q13, disrupting the paternal allele.
(C) A mutation in the SNRPN gene on the maternal allele.
(D) A deletion of the imprinting control region (ICR) on the paternal chromosome 15q11-q13.
(E) A paternal deletion encompassing the 15q11-q13 region, including the SNRPN and IPW genes.

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with developmental delay, hypotonia, hyperphagia, obesity, short stature, small hands/feet, characteristic facial features, hypogonadism, and moderate intellectual disability. This constellation of symptoms is highly suggestive of Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS).
- **Infancy History:** Poor suckling, feeding difficulties, hypotonia. These are classic early signs of PWS.
- **Karyotype:** Normal 46,XY rules out aneuploidy (e.g., Down syndrome) or large chromosomal abnormalities detectable by standard karyotyping.
- **FISH Analysis:** Shows absence of the paternal allele for the SNRPN gene locus (15q11-q13) and presence of the maternal allele. This is the crucial piece of information. PWS is caused by the lack of expression of paternally inherited genes in the 15q11-q13 region. The SNRPN gene is a key gene in this region that is normally expressed only from the paternal allele due to genomic imprinting.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most likely* genetic mechanism responsible for the condition, given the clinical picture and FISH results.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS):** A complex genetic disorder caused by the loss of function of specific genes located in the 15q11-q13 region on chromosome 15.
- **Genomic Imprinting:** This region is subject to genomic imprinting, meaning that certain genes are expressed in a parent-of-origin-specific manner. In this region, the paternal allele is normally active, while the maternal allele is silenced (imprinted).
- **Key Genes:** Several genes in this region are implicated in PWS, including SNRPN (Small Nuclear Ribonucleoprotein N), IPW (Imprinting Protein, Weight-related), MKBPBP (Myelin Basic Protein Binding Protein), and others. These genes are normally expressed only from the paternal chromosome.
- **Molecular Mechanisms of PWS:**
    - **Paternal Deletion (approx. 70%):** The most common cause is a deletion of the paternal 15q11-q13 region. This removes the active paternal copies of the critical genes.
    - **Maternal Uniparental Disomy (UPD) (approx. 25%):** The individual inherits two copies of chromosome 15 from the mother and none from the father. Since the maternal copies of the genes in the 15q11-q13 region are imprinted (silenced), there is no functional paternal copy, leading to PWS.
    - **Imprinting Center Defect (approx. 5%):** A defect in the imprinting control region (ICR) on the paternal chromosome 15q11-q13 can lead to inappropriate silencing (imprinting) of the paternal copies of the critical genes, even though they are physically present.
- **FISH Result Interpretation:** The FISH result showing the absence of the paternal SNRPN allele and the presence of the maternal allele directly points to a mechanism where the paternal contribution is missing or silenced.
- **Evaluating the Options:**
    - (E) Paternal deletion: This directly explains the absence of the paternal allele detected by FISH. It is the most common cause of PWS and fits the clinical picture and FISH findings perfectly.
    - (A) Maternal UPD: This would also result in the absence of a functional paternal allele. However, FISH would show two maternal signals (if the probe detects the maternal allele) and no paternal signal. The vignette states the maternal allele *is present*, implying a single maternal allele, which is consistent with a deletion of the paternal allele. While UPD is a cause of PWS, the specific FISH result described (paternal allele absent, maternal allele present) is most characteristic of a paternal deletion.
    - (B) Translocation: A translocation *could* disrupt the paternal allele, but it's less common than deletion. FISH would likely show the presence of the paternal sequence, but it might be rearranged or part of a derivative chromosome, which isn't described. The simple absence of the paternal signal points more strongly to a deletion.
    - (C) Maternal SNRPN mutation: A mutation on the maternal allele would not cause PWS because the paternal allele is normally active. PWS results from the lack of *paternal* gene expression.
    - (D) Imprinting center defect: This would lead to silencing of the paternal allele, similar to maternal UPD. FISH might show both paternal and maternal alleles present, but the paternal genes would not be expressed. The vignette states the paternal allele is *absent* on FISH, making an imprinting center defect less likely than a deletion.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15q11-q13: This is a known cause of PWS (approx. 25%). In UPD, the individual inherits two copies of chromosome 15 from the mother and none from the father. Since the maternal copies of the genes in the 15q11-q13 region are imprinted (silenced), there is no functional paternal copy. However, the FISH result described (paternal allele absent, maternal allele present) is most typical of a deletion. In UPD, FISH might show two maternal signals (if the probe detects the maternal allele) and no paternal signal, or it might show a single maternal signal if the probe only detects one allele. The description "maternal allele is present" is more consistent with the remaining maternal chromosome in the context of a paternal deletion. Students might choose this because it's a common mechanism for PWS.
- (B) A translocation involving chromosome 15q11-q13, disrupting the paternal allele: Translocations can cause PWS if they disrupt the paternal 15q11-q13 region or lead to its loss. However, this is a less common mechanism than deletion or UPD. Furthermore, FISH in a translocation might show the paternal sequence present but rearranged, or part of a derivative chromosome, rather than a complete absence of the paternal signal as described. This option is less likely given the specific FISH result.
- (C) A mutation in the SNRPN gene on the maternal allele: PWS is caused by the lack of expression of *paternally* inherited genes in the 15q11-q13 region due to imprinting. A mutation on the maternal allele would not cause PWS because the paternal allele is normally expressed. This option fundamentally misunderstands the concept of genomic imprinting in PWS.
- (D) A deletion of the imprinting control region (ICR) on the paternal chromosome 15q11-q13: Defects in the ICR can cause PWS by leading to inappropriate silencing (imprinting) of the paternal alleles. This results in the absence of expression from the paternal genes. However, FISH analysis in this case would typically show both paternal and maternal alleles present, although the paternal genes would be non-functional due to silencing. The vignette specifically states the paternal allele is *absent* on FISH, which is inconsistent with an ICR defect where the paternal DNA sequence is still present.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Prader-Willi Syndrome is caused by the lack of expression of paternally inherited genes in the 15q11-q13 region due to genomic imprinting. The three main mechanisms are paternal deletion (most common), maternal uniparental disomy (UPD), and imprinting center defect. Understanding the specific molecular consequences of each mechanism, especially as revealed by FISH analysis (e.g., absence of paternal signal vs. presence of both paternal and maternal signals), is crucial. Differentiating PWS from Angelman Syndrome (caused by loss of maternal function in the same region) is also important.

<a id='question-104'></a>

### Question 104: Prader-Willi Syndrome
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS) is a genetic disorder caused by the loss of function of specific genes on chromosome 15, typically inherited paternally. The clinical features include neonatal hypotonia and feeding difficulties, followed by hyperphagia and obesity in childhood, hypogonadism, and developmental delay. The underlying mechanism involves genomic imprinting and deletion or uniparental disomy of the paternal chromosome 15q11.2-q13 region. (Prader-Willi 综合征 (PWS) 是一种遗传失调，由 15 号染色体上特定基因的功能丧失引起，通常是父系遗传的。临床特征包括新生儿低张力肌和喂养困难，随后是儿童时期贪吃和肥胖、性腺功能减退和发育迟缓。潜在机制涉及 15q11.2-q13 区域的基因组印记和父系染色体的缺失或单亲二体。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old male infant presents to the pediatrician with failure to thrive. His parents report significant feeding difficulties since birth, requiring nasogastric tube feeding for the first 3 months. He exhibited marked hypotonia (muscle tone) and lethargy as a neonate. Physical examination reveals developmental delay, short stature, and small hands and feet. Laboratory investigations show hypogonadotropic hypogonadism (low LH, FSH, and testosterone). Genetic testing reveals a deletion of the paternal allele in the 15q11.2-q13 region. The infant's parents are concerned about his current poor weight gain despite increased caloric intake attempts, but they are also worried about the potential for future weight problems given his family history of obesity. Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the clinical presentation of this infant?

(A) Loss of function mutation in the *MC4R* gene, leading to decreased satiety signaling in the hypothalamus.
(B) Loss of function mutation in the *FMR1* gene, resulting in fragile X syndrome characterized by intellectual disability and specific physical features.
(C) Loss of genomic imprinting leading to the expression of only the maternal allele of genes in the 15q11.2-q13 region, including *SNRPN*.
(D) Gain-of-function mutation in the *GH1* gene, causing gigantism due to excessive growth hormone secretion.
(E) Loss of function mutation in the *POMC* gene, leading to impaired production of melanocyte-stimulating hormone (MSH) and ACTH, resulting in red hair, fair skin, and adrenal insufficiency.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Age/Sex:** 6-month-old male infant.
- **Presenting Complaint:** Failure to thrive, feeding difficulties since birth (requiring NG tube), neonatal hypotonia, lethargy.
- **Physical Exam:** Developmental delay, short stature, small hands/feet.
- **Labs:** Hypogonadotropic hypogonadism (low LH, FSH, testosterone).
- **Genetics:** Deletion of the paternal allele in the 15q11.2-q13 region.
- **Question:** Molecular mechanism explaining the clinical presentation.

The combination of neonatal hypotonia, feeding difficulties, developmental delay, hypogonadism, and a deletion of the paternal 15q11.2-q13 region strongly points towards Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS). The key to understanding PWS lies in genomic imprinting. This region contains several genes, some of which are normally expressed only from the paternal allele (e.g., *SNRPN*, *IPW*, *MAGI3*), while others are expressed only from the maternal allele (e.g., *UBE3A* - associated with Angelman Syndrome when maternally deleted/mutated). The paternal deletion means the normally expressed paternal copies of these genes are missing. Because of imprinting, the maternal copies of the paternally expressed genes (*SNRPN*, *IPW*, *MAGI3*) are silenced, leading to a functional loss of these genes. This loss of function explains the characteristic PWS phenotype. The later development of hyperphagia and obesity is also a hallmark feature, although not explicitly described in detail in this vignette, the concern about future weight problems hints at it.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS) is a complex genetic disorder primarily caused by the loss of function of paternally expressed genes located in the 15q11.2-q13 region. This loss of function occurs through several mechanisms:
1.  **Paternal Deletion (most common, ~70%):** Deletion of the critical region on the paternal chromosome 15. This is what the vignette describes.
2.  **Maternal Uniparental Disomy (UPD) (10-15%):** The individual inherits two copies of chromosome 15 from the mother and none from the father.
3.  **Imprinting Defect (10-15%):** An error in the imprinting process leads to silencing of the paternal allele, similar to the maternal allele.
4.  **Rare mutations:** Point mutations or small deletions within specific paternally expressed genes in the region.

The critical genes involved, normally expressed from the paternal allele, include *SNRPN* (Small Nuclear Ribonucleoprotein N), *IPW* (Imprinting Protein W), and *MAGI3* (Membrane-Associated Guanine Nucleotide Exchange Factor with IQ Motif 3). *SNRPN* is thought to be a key player, involved in RNA splicing. *IPW* is a non-coding RNA. *MAGI3* is involved in neuronal development and function.

Genomic imprinting is an epigenetic phenomenon where certain genes are expressed in a parent-of-origin-specific manner. In the 15q11.2-q13 region, the paternal allele is normally active, while the maternal allele is silenced through DNA methylation and histone modifications. When the paternal allele is lost (e.g., by deletion, UPD, or imprinting defect), the individual only has the silenced maternal allele, resulting in a functional absence of the paternally expressed genes. This loss of function leads to the characteristic PWS phenotype, including hypotonia, feeding difficulties in infancy, hyperphagia and obesity later in life, hypogonadism, short stature, developmental delay, and characteristic facial features.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Loss of function mutation in the *MC4R* gene is associated with monogenic obesity. *MC4R* (Melanocortin 4 Receptor) is located in the hypothalamus and plays a crucial role in regulating appetite and energy homeostasis. Mutations lead to impaired satiety signaling, resulting in hyperphagia and obesity. While hyperphagia and obesity are features of PWS, the underlying cause in PWS is the loss of paternally expressed genes in 15q11.2-q13 due to imprinting, not a mutation in *MC4R*. The vignette specifically mentions a deletion in the 15q11.2-q13 region, pointing away from *MC4R*.
- (B): Loss of function mutation in the *FMR1* gene causes Fragile X syndrome. This is the most common inherited cause of intellectual disability. It is characterized by intellectual disability, developmental delay, specific physical features (long face, large ears, macroorchidism), and behavioral problems. It is caused by a CGG repeat expansion in the *FMR1* gene on the X chromosome, leading to silencing of the gene. This is unrelated to the 15q11.2-q13 deletion and the PWS phenotype described.
- (D): Gain-of-function mutation in the *GH1* gene (Growth Hormone 1) causes gigantism (if occurring before epiphyseal closure) or acromegaly (if occurring after). This leads to excessive growth hormone production, resulting in overgrowth. This is the opposite of the short stature seen in PWS and involves a completely different gene and mechanism.
- (E): Loss of function mutation in the *POMC* gene (Pro-opiomelanocortin) leads to deficiencies in ACTH, MSH, and β-endorphin. This results in adrenal insufficiency, red hair, and fair skin. It is unrelated to the PWS phenotype and involves a different gene (*POMC* on chromosome 2) and pathway (peptide hormone processing).

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Prader-Willi Syndrome is caused by the loss of function of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11.2-q13 region due to genomic imprinting defects (deletion, UPD, imprinting defect). Key features include neonatal hypotonia, feeding difficulties, later hyperphagia/obesity, hypogonadism, and developmental delay. Differentiate PWS from Angelman Syndrome (loss of maternal *UBE3A* in the same region, causing severe developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, happy demeanor), Bardet-Biedl Syndrome (ciliary dysfunction, obesity, retinitis pigmentosa, polydactyly), and other causes of obesity and developmental delay.

<a id='question-105'></a>

### Question 105: Angelman Syndrome Mechanisms
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the genetic and molecular mechanisms underlying Angelman syndrome, specifically genomic imprinting and its role in disease pathogenesis (安吉尔曼综合征的遗传和分子机制，特别是基因组印记在疾病发病中的作用).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old girl presents to the pediatrician with developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, and frequent episodes of inappropriate laughter. Her speech is minimal, and she exhibits microcephaly and characteristic jerky movements. Her parents report that she was born full-term without complications. Her father has a history of epilepsy, but her mother is healthy. A karyotype analysis reveals a normal 46,XX chromosome complement. Further investigation includes methylation analysis of the 15q11-q13 region, which shows a paternally inherited pattern of methylation across the UBE3A gene locus. Fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH) analysis using a probe specific for the 15q11-q13 region shows no deletion or duplication. Sequencing of the UBE3A gene reveals no pathogenic variants. A uniparental disomy (UPD) analysis is performed.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most likely responsible for this patient's Angelman syndrome?

(A) Maternal deletion of the 15q11-q13 region encompassing the UBE3A gene.
(B) Paternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 15.
(C) Imprinting defect leading to biallelic expression of the UBE3A gene.
(D) Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 15.
(E) A pathogenic point mutation within the UBE3A gene that disrupts protein function.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- The patient presents with classic features of Angelman syndrome (AS): developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, inappropriate laughter ("happy puppet syndrome"), minimal speech, microcephaly, and jerky movements.
- Karyotype is normal (46,XX), ruling out large chromosomal abnormalities like trisomy 21 or other aneuploidies.
- Methylation analysis shows a *paternally inherited* pattern of methylation across the UBE3A gene locus. In normal individuals, the maternal allele of UBE3A is unmethylated and expressed, while the paternal allele is methylated and silenced due to genomic imprinting. A paternally inherited methylation pattern across the *entire* UBE3A locus indicates that the maternal allele is also methylated and silenced, leading to a lack of functional UBE3A protein expression.
- FISH analysis rules out deletions or duplications in the 15q11-q13 region, which are common causes of AS (specifically, maternal deletion).
- Sequencing of the UBE3A gene reveals no pathogenic variants, ruling out point mutations or other sequence alterations within the gene itself.
- The combination of clinical features, paternally inherited UBE3A methylation pattern, normal FISH, and normal UBE3A sequencing strongly points towards a disruption of imprinting where the maternal allele is inappropriately silenced.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Angelman syndrome is primarily caused by the loss of function of the maternally inherited UBE3A gene located in the 15q11-q13 region.
- UBE3A encodes an E3 ubiquitin ligase involved in protein degradation.
- The 15q11-q13 region is subject to genomic imprinting, meaning that gene expression depends on the parent of origin.
- Normally, the maternal UBE3A allele is unmethylated and actively transcribed in the brain, while the paternal UBE3A allele is heavily methylated in the brain (and other tissues) and silenced. This differential methylation is established during gametogenesis.
- The clinical vignette describes a patient with AS features. The key finding is the *paternally inherited methylation pattern* across the UBE3A locus. This means the maternal allele, which should be unmethylated and expressed, is instead methylated and silenced.
- The vignette explicitly states that FISH analysis is normal, ruling out deletions (most common cause, ~70%) or duplications.
- The vignette also states that UBE3A gene sequencing is normal, ruling out point mutations or other sequence variants (~10%).
- Therefore, the maternal allele is silenced despite being unmethylated in normal individuals. This silencing is due to the *paternal* methylation pattern being inherited.
- How can a patient inherit a paternally inherited methylation pattern for the maternal UBE3A allele? This occurs when the patient inherits both copies of chromosome 15 from the father (paternal UPD) and no copy from the mother (maternal UPD). In this scenario, the patient only has the methylated paternal UBE3A allele, leading to a complete loss of UBE3A function in the brain.
- Paternal UPD of chromosome 15 accounts for approximately 3-7% of AS cases.
- Option (A) is incorrect because FISH was normal, ruling out deletion.
- Option (C) is incorrect because the methylation pattern is *paternally inherited*, indicating the maternal allele is silenced, not biallelic expression. Biallelic expression would imply the maternal allele is expressed despite being methylated, which is not the case here.
- Option (E) is incorrect because UBE3A sequencing was normal, ruling out point mutations.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Maternal deletion of the 15q11-q13 region is the most common cause of AS (~70%). However, the FISH analysis in the vignette was normal, explicitly ruling out this possibility. Students might choose this option because it's the most frequent cause, but they must carefully read the negative findings.
- (C): Imprinting defects can cause AS, but typically they result in the maternal allele being inappropriately methylated (like in this case) or, less commonly, the paternal allele being unmethylated. Biallelic expression of UBE3A would mean both alleles are transcribed, which is inconsistent with the clinical presentation of AS (loss of function) and the finding of a paternally inherited methylation pattern (implying maternal silencing). This option confuses the mechanism.
- (D): Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 15 would mean the patient inherited both copies of chromosome 15 from the mother. In this case, the maternal UBE3A allele would be unmethylated and expressed, while the paternal allele (inherited from the father, but not present) would be methylated and silenced. This would lead to normal UBE3A expression, not AS. This is the opposite of the correct mechanism.
- (E): Pathogenic point mutations within the UBE3A gene account for about 10% of AS cases. However, the vignette explicitly states that sequencing of the UBE3A gene revealed no pathogenic variants, ruling out this mechanism. Students might choose this option if they overlook the negative sequencing result.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Angelman syndrome results from the loss of function of the maternally expressed UBE3A gene. The most common cause is a maternal deletion of 15q11-q13. Other causes include paternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 15, imprinting defects (leading to methylation of the maternal UBE3A allele), and pathogenic mutations in the UBE3A gene. The key to diagnosing the specific mechanism often lies in detailed molecular testing, including methylation analysis, FISH, and sequencing. Paternal UPD (Option B) results in inheriting two methylated paternal copies of UBE3A, leading to loss of maternal UBE3A expression. This contrasts with maternal UPD (Option D), which would result in normal UBE3A expression.

<a id='question-106'></a>

### Question 106: Angelman Syndrome
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Angelman syndrome is a neurodevelopmental disorder caused by the loss of function of the maternally inherited *UBE3A* gene on chromosome 15q11-q13, typically due to deletion, uniparental disomy, imprinting defect, or mutation. This leads to characteristic clinical features including severe intellectual disability, absent speech, ataxia, seizures, and a happy demeanor with frequent laughter. (安吉尔曼综合征是一种神经发育障碍，由15号染色体q11-q13上母系遗传的*UBE3A*基因功能丧失引起，通常是由于缺失、单亲二体、印迹缺陷或突变。这导致了特征性临床表现，包括严重的智力障碍、失语、共济失调、癫痫发作和快乐的天性，伴有频繁的笑声。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old girl is brought to the pediatrician by her concerned parents. Since infancy, she has exhibited significant developmental delays, failing to meet milestones such as sitting, crawling, or walking. She has never uttered a single word. Her parents describe her as having a perpetually happy disposition, often erupting into fits of inappropriate, high-pitched laughter, especially when startled or frustrated. Her gait is described as jerky and unsteady, resembling that of a puppet. Neurological examination reveals severe intellectual disability, microcephaly, and frequent seizures. A family history reveals no similar symptoms in other relatives. Chromosomal microarray analysis shows a deletion encompassing the 15q11.2-q13 region on the maternal chromosome 15. Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the pathogenesis of this patient's condition?

(A) Loss-of-function mutation in the *SNRPN* gene located on the paternal chromosome 15, leading to impaired RNA splicing.
(B) Loss-of-function mutation in the *UBE3A* gene located on the paternal chromosome 15, leading to impaired protein ubiquitination.
(C) Loss-of-function mutation in the *UBE3A* gene located on the maternal chromosome 15, leading to impaired protein ubiquitination.
(D) Gain-of-function mutation in the *UBE3A* gene located on the maternal chromosome 15, leading to constitutive protein ubiquitination.
(E) Loss-of-function mutation in the *GABRP* gene located on the maternal chromosome 15, leading to impaired GABAergic neurotransmission.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old girl with severe developmental delay (no speech, delayed motor milestones), happy demeanor with inappropriate laughter, ataxic gait ("puppet-like"), microcephaly, seizures. These are classic features of Angelman syndrome.
- **Family History:** Negative family history suggests a *de novo* event or a condition not typically inherited in a Mendelian fashion.
- **Genetic Testing:** Chromosomal microarray shows a deletion in the 15q11.2-q13 region on the *maternal* chromosome 15. This region contains the *UBE3A* gene.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the pathogenesis. The key is linking the clinical phenotype, the specific genetic finding (maternal deletion of 15q11.2-q13), and the function of the gene involved.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Angelman syndrome (AS) is a neurodevelopmental disorder characterized by severe intellectual disability, developmental delay, speech impairment, ataxia, seizures, and a happy demeanor.
- The critical region for AS is 15q11.2-q13. This region is subject to genomic imprinting, meaning gene expression depends on the parent of origin.
- The *UBE3A* gene, encoding an E3 ubiquitin ligase, is located within this region. *UBE3A* is normally expressed *only* from the maternal allele in the brain. The paternal allele is silenced by imprinting.
- The patient has a deletion of the maternal 15q11.2-q13 region. This deletion removes the functional maternal copy of the *UBE3A* gene.
- Since the paternal *UBE3A* allele is normally silenced in the brain due to imprinting, the loss of the maternal copy results in a complete absence of functional UBE3A protein in the brain.
- UBE3A is an E3 ubiquitin ligase involved in protein degradation. Its loss leads to the accumulation of specific target proteins, disrupting neuronal function and development, causing the AS phenotype.
- Therefore, the pathogenesis is due to the loss-of-function of the *UBE3A* gene specifically on the *maternal* chromosome 15.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Loss-of-function mutation in the *SNRPN* gene located on the paternal chromosome 15, leading to impaired RNA splicing. *SNRPN* is located in the 15q11.2-q13 region, but it is normally expressed from the *paternal* allele in the brain. Loss-of-function mutations in the paternal *SNRPN* cause Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS), characterized by hypotonia, feeding difficulties in infancy, hyperphagia and obesity later, short stature, hypogonadism, and mild to moderate intellectual disability. This is incorrect because the patient has a maternal deletion, and the phenotype is AS, not PWS.
- (B) Loss-of-function mutation in the *UBE3A* gene located on the paternal chromosome 15, leading to impaired protein ubiquitination. While *UBE3A* loss-of-function causes AS, the critical point is that the functional copy in the brain is maternal. The paternal copy is normally silenced. A loss-of-function mutation in the paternal *UBE3A* would not cause AS because the maternal copy would still be expressed. This is incorrect because the patient has a maternal deletion, eliminating the functional maternal copy.
- (D) Gain-of-function mutation in the *UBE3A* gene located on the maternal chromosome 15, leading to constitutive protein ubiquitination. Angelman syndrome is caused by the *loss* of function of the maternal *UBE3A* allele, not a gain-of-function. Gain-of-function mutations in *UBE3A* are not associated with AS. This is incorrect because the mechanism is loss-of-function, not gain-of-function.
- (E) Loss-of-function mutation in the *GABRP* gene located on the maternal chromosome 15, leading to impaired GABAergic neurotransmission. *GABRP* (gamma-aminobutyric acid receptor, rho) is located in the 15q11.2-q13 region and is also subject to imprinting (maternally expressed). Loss-of-function mutations in *GABRP* can cause AS, but the most common cause is deletion of the *UBE3A* gene (about 70% of cases). Given the specific finding of a deletion in 15q11.2-q13, and *UBE3A* being the primary gene responsible for the classic AS phenotype within that region, loss of *UBE3A* function is the most direct and common explanation. While *GABRP* mutations can cause AS, *UBE3A* deletion is the most likely mechanism given the vignette. Furthermore, the question asks for the *best* explanation based on the provided information (deletion in the region containing *UBE3A*).

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Angelman syndrome is caused by the loss of function of the maternally inherited *UBE3A* gene due to various mechanisms (deletion, UPD, imprinting defect, mutation). Prader-Willi syndrome is caused by the loss of function of paternally inherited genes (e.g., *SNRPN*, *GNAS*) in the same chromosomal region (15q11.2-q13) due to different mechanisms (paternal deletion, maternal UPD, imprinting defect, mutation). Both are imprinting disorders. Key clinical differences: AS features severe ID, ataxia, happy demeanor, no speech; PWS features hypotonia, hyperphagia/obesity, short stature, hypogonadism, moderate ID.

<a id='question-107'></a>

### Question 107: Prader-Willi Syndrome and Uniparental Disomy
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the genetic basis of Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS), specifically the role of genomic imprinting and uniparental disomy (UPD) in its pathogenesis. (理解Prader-Willi综合征的遗传基础，特别是基因组印记和单亲二体畸形(UPD)在其中的发病机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy is brought to the pediatrician by his parents due to developmental delay, hypotonia since infancy, and excessive eating leading to obesity. The child has characteristic facial features including almond-shaped eyes, a narrow forehead, and small hands and feet. His parents report that he has difficulty feeding as an infant and exhibits behavioral problems such as temper tantrums. His older sister is phenotypically normal. The parents are of average height and weight with no significant medical history. Karyotyping reveals a normal 46,XY chromosome complement. However, methylation analysis of the SNRPN gene region on chromosome 15q11-q13 shows that all copies of the SNRPN gene are unmethylated. Further investigation using microsatellite markers reveals that the child inherited both copies of chromosome 15 from his mother and no copies from his father.

(A) A deletion of the paternal 15q11-q13 region.
(B) A deletion of the maternal 15q11-q13 region.
(C) A translocation involving chromosome 15, disrupting the paternal copy of the SNRPN gene.
(D) A point mutation in the maternal copy of the SNRPN gene.
(E) A point mutation in the paternal copy of the SNRPN gene.

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Clinical Presentation:** The 3-year-old boy presents with classic features of Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS): developmental delay, infantile hypotonia, hyperphagia leading to obesity, characteristic facial features (almond eyes, narrow forehead, small hands/feet), and behavioral issues.
*   **Family History:** Older sister is normal, parents are unaffected, suggesting a *de novo* event or a specific inheritance pattern related to PWS.
*   **Karyotype:** Normal 46,XY rules out large chromosomal abnormalities like aneuploidy or large deletions/translocations detectable by standard karyotyping.
*   **Methylation Analysis:** Unmethylated SNRPN gene copies. In normal individuals, the paternal copy of genes in the 15q11-q13 region (including SNRPN) is methylated, while the maternal copy is unmethylated. In PWS, the *paternal* contribution is silenced (methylated), and the *maternal* contribution is active (unmethylated). The finding that *all* copies are unmethylated indicates a lack of the paternal contribution or a failure of paternal imprinting.
*   **Microsatellite Markers:** The child inherited both chromosome 15s from his mother and none from his father. This is the definition of paternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS):** PWS is a neurodevelopmental disorder caused by the loss of function of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11-q13 region. The critical gene is SNRPN (Small Nuclear Ribonucleoprotein N), which encodes a component of the spliceosome. Other genes in this region, like MKBPBP (formerly BKNP), are also paternally expressed and contribute to the phenotype.
*   **Genomic Imprinting:** This is an epigenetic phenomenon where certain genes are expressed in a parent-of-origin-specific manner. In the 15q11-q13 region, the paternal allele is normally silenced by methylation, while the maternal allele is active.
*   **Mechanisms of PWS:**
    *   **Deletion (70%):** Deletion of the paternal 15q11-q13 region. This is the most common cause.
    *   **Maternal Uniparental Disomy (UPD) (25%):** The child inherits two copies of chromosome 15 from the mother and none from the father. Since the maternal copies are normally unmethylated (active) but the paternal copies are required for function, this leads to PWS.
    *   **Imprinting Defect (ID) (3-5%):** A defect in the methylation machinery leads to methylation of the maternal allele and/or failure of methylation of the paternal allele. This results in silencing of both copies, leading to PWS.
    *   **Translocation (rare):** A translocation involving chromosome 15 disrupts the paternal copy of the critical region.
*   **Case Analysis:** The patient has PWS symptoms. Karyotype is normal, ruling out a simple deletion detectable by standard methods. Methylation analysis shows *all* copies of SNRPN are unmethylated. This means the paternal contribution, which should be methylated, is either absent or has failed to imprint correctly. The microsatellite analysis confirms that the child has paternal UPD15 (inherited both chromosome 15s from the mother). In paternal UPD15, the child has two maternal copies of the 15q11-q13 region. Since the maternal copies are normally unmethylated and active, but the paternally expressed genes are required, the child develops PWS.
*   **Why Option A is Correct:** Paternal UPD15 means the child received both chromosome 15s from the mother. This is equivalent to having two maternal copies and zero paternal copies in the critical region. Since the paternal contribution is necessary for normal function (and is normally methylated/silenced), the absence of the paternal contribution leads to PWS. Option A describes the underlying genetic event: the child effectively lacks the paternal 15q11-q13 region due to inheriting both copies from the mother.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (B) A deletion of the maternal 15q11-q13 region. This would lead to Angelman syndrome (AS), characterized by severe developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, and a happy demeanor. AS results from the loss of the *maternal* contribution of genes in this region, particularly UBE3A, which is maternally expressed and paternally imprinted (methylated/silenced). The patient's symptoms and methylation pattern (all unmethylated) are inconsistent with AS.
- (C) A translocation involving chromosome 15, disrupting the paternal copy of the SNRPN gene. While a translocation *can* cause PWS by disrupting the paternal copy, the vignette states the karyotype is normal (46,XY). Standard karyotyping typically detects translocations involving large segments of chromosomes. Although a subtle translocation might be missed, the microsatellite analysis definitively points to UPD15. Furthermore, the methylation pattern (all unmethylated) is most consistent with the complete absence of the paternal contribution, as seen in UPD15 or maternal deletion, rather than a disruption of the paternal copy while the maternal copy is present (which would likely show mixed methylation patterns depending on the specific break points and imprinting status).
- (D) A point mutation in the maternal copy of the SNRPN gene. A point mutation in the maternal copy would likely lead to a loss-of-function allele on the maternal side. Since the maternal copy is normally active in this region, a loss-of-function mutation would be expected to cause Angelman syndrome (AS), not PWS. The patient's methylation pattern (all unmethylated) also contradicts this, as it indicates a lack of paternal contribution or imprinting.
- (E) A point mutation in the paternal copy of the SNRPN gene. A point mutation in the paternal copy could potentially cause PWS if it leads to a loss-of-function allele. However, the methylation analysis showing *all* copies unmethylated strongly suggests the paternal contribution is either absent (as in UPD15 or deletion) or has failed to imprint correctly (imprinting defect). A simple point mutation wouldn't explain the global unmethylation pattern observed. The microsatellite data definitively confirms UPD15.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Prader-Willi syndrome results from the loss of function of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11-q13 region due to genomic imprinting. The most common cause is a paternal deletion, but maternal UPD15, imprinting defects, and translocations can also cause PWS. Angelman syndrome results from the loss of function of the *maternal* UBE3A gene in the same region, typically due to maternal deletion or paternal UPD15. Understanding the concepts of genomic imprinting, uniparental disomy, and the distinct clinical features and molecular mechanisms of PWS and AS is crucial.

<a id='question-108'></a>

### Question 108: Trisomy 21 (Down Syndrome) - Heterodisomy vs. Isodisomy
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the molecular mechanisms leading to trisomy, specifically differentiating between heterodisomy (resulting from nondisjunction in meiosis I) and isodisomy (resulting from nondisjunction in meiosis II), and how these mechanisms affect the origin of the extra chromosome. (理解导致三体畸的分子机制，特别是区分异染色单体 (Meiosis I 不分离) 和同染色单体 (Meiosis II 不分离)，以及这些机制如何影响额外染色体的来源。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 38-year-old woman (G2P1) delivers a full-term infant with characteristic features of Down syndrome, including upslanting palpebral fissures, epicanthal folds, a flattened facial profile, and hypotonia. Prenatal screening indicated an increased risk for trisomy 21. Karyotyping of the infant reveals 47,XY,+21. Molecular analysis using single nucleotide polymorphism (SNP) arrays is performed on DNA extracted from the infant's peripheral blood leukocytes. The SNP array data show that all three copies of chromosome 21 are derived from the maternal genome. Further analysis reveals that one copy of chromosome 21 is identical to the maternal chromosome 21, while the other two copies are different from each other and from the maternal chromosome 21. The mother's karyotype is normal (46,XX). The father's karyotype is also normal (46,XY). The mother reports no history of previous pregnancies affected by trisomy 21.

Which of the following mechanisms is the MOST likely cause of the infant's trisomy 21, given the molecular findings?

(A) Meiotic nondisjunction in the father during spermatogenesis, resulting in a gamete with two copies of chromosome 21.
(B) Meiotic nondisjunction in the mother during oogenesis, resulting in an egg with two copies of chromosome 21.
(C) Mitotic nondisjunction in the zygote after fertilization, resulting in a cell line with trisomy 21.
(D) Postzygotic paternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 21, resulting in the loss of the maternal chromosome 21 in some cells.
(E) Postzygotic maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 21, resulting in the loss of the paternal chromosome 21 in some cells.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** Infant with Down syndrome (Trisomy 21). Confirmed by karyotype (47,XY,+21).
*   **Molecular Findings (SNP Array):** All three copies of chromosome 21 are maternal in origin. One copy is identical to the maternal chromosome 21, and the other two copies are different from each other and the maternal copy.
*   **Parental Karyotypes:** Both parents are chromosomally normal (46,XX and 46,XY).
*   **Question:** Determine the mechanism causing the trisomy 21, specifically differentiating between heterodisomy and isodisomy.

The key finding is that all three copies of chromosome 21 are maternal. This indicates that the extra chromosome 21 originated from the mother. The SNP array further distinguishes between heterodisomy and isodisomy. Heterodisomy implies that the extra chromosome came from a gamete with two *different* copies of the chromosome (e.g., from Meiosis I nondisjunction). Isodisomy implies that the extra chromosome came from a gamete with two *identical* copies of the chromosome (e.g., from Meiosis II nondisjunction).

In this case, there are three copies of chromosome 21, all maternal. One copy is identical to the maternal chromosome, and the other two are different from each other and the maternal copy. This pattern is characteristic of heterodisomy. Heterodisomy for chromosome 21 results from nondisjunction during maternal meiosis I. The mother produces an egg with two different copies of chromosome 21 (one maternal chromosome 21 and one maternal chromosome 21 from the homologous pair). Fertilization by a normal sperm (carrying one paternal chromosome 21) results in a zygote with three copies of chromosome 21, all derived from the mother (two different maternal copies and one identical maternal copy).

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Trisomy 21 Pathogenesis:** Most commonly caused by meiotic nondisjunction, leading to a gamete with an extra chromosome 21 (n+1). Fertilization with a normal gamete (n) results in a trisomic zygote (2n+1).
*   **Meiosis I Nondisjunction:** Homologous chromosomes fail to separate during anaphase I. This results in a gamete with two copies of the chromosome (one from each homolog). If this occurs in the mother (oogenesis), the egg will have two maternal copies of chromosome 21. Fertilization by a normal sperm (with one paternal copy) leads to a zygote with three copies of chromosome 21, all maternal in origin. The SNP array will show two *different* maternal copies and one identical maternal copy. This is **heterodisomy**.
*   **Meiosis II Nondisjunction:** Sister chromatids fail to separate during anaphase II. This results in a gamete with two *identical* copies of the chromosome. If this occurs in the mother (oogenesis), the egg will have two identical copies of chromosome 21. Fertilization by a normal sperm (with one paternal copy) leads to a zygote with three copies of chromosome 21, two of which are identical maternal copies. The SNP array will show two *identical* maternal copies and one different maternal copy. This is **isodisomy**.
*   **Mitotic Nondisjunction:** Occurs after fertilization in the zygote. Results in mosaicism, where some cells are trisomic and others are disomic. This would not explain the finding that *all* three copies are maternal in origin in the tested cells (peripheral blood leukocytes).
*   **Uniparental Disomy (UPD):** Both copies of a chromosome (or part of a chromosome) are inherited from a single parent, with no copy from the other parent. In this case, all three copies are maternal, meaning the paternal contribution is missing. This is not UPD; it's trisomy due to an extra maternal chromosome. UPD would typically result in a diploid state (46 chromosomes) where both copies come from one parent, often associated with imprinting disorders if it involves imprinted genes.

In this specific case, the SNP array shows two *different* maternal copies and one identical maternal copy. This pattern is consistent with heterodisomy resulting from maternal meiosis I nondisjunction.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Meiotic nondisjunction in the father during spermatogenesis, resulting in a gamete with two copies of chromosome 21.** If this occurred, the resulting zygote would have three copies of chromosome 21, but two would be paternal and one maternal. The SNP array would show two *paternal* copies and one *maternal* copy. This contradicts the finding that all three copies are maternal.
- (C): **Mitotic nondisjunction in the zygote after fertilization, resulting in a cell line with trisomy 21.** Mitotic nondisjunction leads to mosaicism (e.g., 46,XY,+21/46,XY). While it can cause trisomy, it doesn't explain why *all* three copies of chromosome 21 in the analyzed cells (blood leukocytes) are maternal. Mosaicism would involve a mixture of cell lines.
- (D): **Postzygotic paternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 21, resulting in the loss of the maternal chromosome 21 in some cells.** Paternal UPD for chromosome 21 means the individual has two paternal copies and no maternal copy. This would result in a diploid karyotype (46 chromosomes) with two paternal copies of chromosome 21. It does not explain the presence of three copies, all maternal. Also, UPD typically leads to mosaicism if it occurs postzygotically.
- (E): **Postzygotic maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 21, resulting in the loss of the paternal chromosome 21 in some cells.** Maternal UPD for chromosome 21 means the individual has two maternal copies and no paternal copy. This would result in a diploid karyotype (46 chromosomes) with two maternal copies of chromosome 21. It does not explain the presence of three copies, all maternal. Again, postzygotic UPD leads to mosaicism.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Understanding the difference between heterodisomy (Meiosis I nondisjunction) and isodisomy (Meiosis II nondisjunction) is crucial for interpreting molecular genetic data in trisomy cases. SNP arrays help determine the origin (maternal vs. paternal) and identity (identical vs. different) of the extra chromosome copies. This question highlights the importance of integrating karyotype findings with molecular data for precise diagnosis and genetic counseling. Key differentials include mosaicism (mitotic nondisjunction) and uniparental disomy.

<a id='question-109'></a>

### Question 109: Trinucleotide Repeat Expansion: Dynamic Unstable Repeat Expansions Amplifying in Meiosis Causing Disease Exacerbation
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Trinucleotide repeat expansions are unstable DNA sequences prone to expansion during DNA replication, particularly in meiosis, leading to anticipation (earlier onset and increased severity) in subsequent generations. This phenomenon is linked to specific neurological disorders. (三核苷酸重复序列扩增是不稳定的DNA序列，在DNA复制过程中容易扩增，尤其是在减数分裂中，导致后代出现预期现象（更早发病和更严重），这与特定的神经系统疾病有关。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male presents to a neurology clinic complaining of progressive gait ataxia, dysarthria, and cognitive decline over the past 5 years. His symptoms began subtly but have worsened significantly. His father developed similar symptoms at age 50, and his paternal grandfather exhibited signs of clumsiness and slurred speech starting in his late 50s. Family history reveals no other significant neurological disorders. Neurological examination reveals cerebellar ataxia, nystagmus, and mild pyramidal signs. MRI of the brain shows cerebellar atrophy. Genetic testing reveals an expanded CAG repeat in the *ATXN1* gene. The patient's father underwent genetic testing 10 years prior, which showed 32 CAG repeats. The patient's current genetic testing reveals 45 CAG repeats.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the earlier onset and increased severity of symptoms in the patient compared to his father and grandfather?

(A) Somatic mosaicism arising from post-zygotic expansion of the CAG repeat in the patient's neurons.
(B) Increased expression of the *ATXN1* gene due to epigenetic modifications in the patient's brain tissue.
(C) Anticipation resulting from the dynamic instability of the CAG trinucleotide repeat expansion during meiosis in the patient's father's germline.
(D) Reduced protein degradation of the mutant ATXN1 protein due to impaired ubiquitin-proteasome system function in the patient.
(E) Increased DNA repair efficiency in the patient's germline leading to fewer errors during replication of the *ATXN1* gene.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 45-year-old male with progressive neurological symptoms (ataxia, dysarthria, cognitive decline) over 5 years. This suggests a neurodegenerative disorder.
- **Family History:** Father developed symptoms at 50, paternal grandfather at late 50s. This pattern of symptoms appearing earlier in successive generations is characteristic of anticipation.
- **Neurological Exam & MRI:** Findings (cerebellar ataxia, nystagmus, pyramidal signs, cerebellar atrophy) are consistent with spinocerebellar ataxia (SCA).
- **Genetic Testing:** Expanded CAG repeat in the *ATXN1* gene confirms a diagnosis of SCA type 1 (SCA1). The *ATXN1* gene is known to be associated with trinucleotide repeat expansion.
- **Repeat Count Comparison:** Father had 32 CAG repeats, patient has 45 CAG repeats. This increase in repeat number between generations is a key feature of dynamic instability.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism explaining the earlier onset and increased severity (anticipation) in the patient compared to his father and grandfather.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Trinucleotide Repeat Expansion:** SCA1 is caused by an expansion of a CAG trinucleotide repeat in the *ATXN1* gene. Normal individuals have <36 repeats. Intermediate alleles (36-39 repeats) are typically asymptomatic. Affected individuals usually have >40 repeats.
- **Dynamic Instability:** Trinucleotide repeats, particularly CAG repeats, are prone to expansion during DNA replication. This instability is especially pronounced in meiosis (germline cells). The mechanism involves slippage of the DNA polymerase during replication, leading to the insertion or deletion of repeat units.
- **Anticipation:** The phenomenon where a genetic disorder manifests earlier and/or more severely in successive generations is called anticipation. This is a hallmark of trinucleotide repeat disorders. It occurs because the repeat expansion tends to increase in size as it is transmitted from parent to offspring, particularly through the paternal germline for CAG repeats (as seen in Huntington's disease and SCA1).
- **Mechanism in this Case:** The patient's father had 32 repeats (likely asymptomatic or mildly symptomatic, though the vignette states symptoms started at 50, which is borderline). During meiosis in his germline, the CAG repeat expanded to 45 repeats in the allele transmitted to the patient. This larger repeat size correlates with earlier onset (45 years old vs. 50 years old in father) and potentially increased severity of SCA1 symptoms. The expansion is due to the inherent instability of the repeat sequence during DNA replication in the germline.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Somatic mosaicism:** While somatic expansion can occur in some trinucleotide repeat disorders (e.g., FRAXA syndrome), it typically leads to variable expression within an individual, not anticipation across generations. The key feature here is the transmission of an expanded allele from father to son, explaining the earlier onset in the son. Somatic mosaicism doesn't explain the generational shift.
- (B) **Increased gene expression:** While altered gene expression can contribute to disease severity, it doesn't directly explain the *increase* in repeat number and the *earlier onset* across generations (anticipation). The primary driver of anticipation in trinucleotide repeat disorders is the expansion of the repeat itself.
- (D) **Reduced protein degradation:** Impaired protein degradation (e.g., via the ubiquitin-proteasome system) can worsen the phenotype by increasing the levels of the toxic mutant protein. However, this mechanism doesn't explain the *increase* in the CAG repeat number between generations or the phenomenon of anticipation itself. It might contribute to severity, but not the core mechanism of anticipation.
- (E) **Increased DNA repair efficiency:** Increased DNA repair efficiency would *decrease* the likelihood of errors during replication, including repeat expansions. This is the opposite of what is observed in trinucleotide repeat disorders, where the repeat is inherently unstable and prone to expansion due to inefficient repair or polymerase slippage.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Trinucleotide repeat expansions are unstable sequences that can increase in size during DNA replication, especially in meiosis, leading to anticipation (earlier onset and increased severity in subsequent generations). Key disorders include Huntington's disease (HTT gene, CAG), Fragile X syndrome (FMR1 gene, CGG), Myotonic dystrophy (DMPK gene, CTG), and several spinocerebellar ataxias (e.g., SCA1, SCA3, SCA6, SCA7, SCA17). Remember that anticipation is a hallmark of these disorders. Differential diagnoses for progressive ataxia include Friedreich's ataxia (GAA repeat, but not typically anticipation), Wilson's disease (copper metabolism), and various other neurodegenerative conditions.
