<a id='question-110'></a>

### Question 110: Genetic Anticipation in Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Genetic anticipation is a phenomenon where a genetic disorder manifests at an earlier age and/or with increased severity in successive generations. This is typically caused by the expansion of unstable nucleotide repeats (e.g., trinucleotide repeats) during transmission from parent to offspring. (遗传性预期，即后代发病年龄提前和/或疾病严重程度增加的现象。这通常是由亲代到子代的遗传过程中不稳定的核苷酸重复序列（例如三核苷酸重复序列）的扩增引起的。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to his physician complaining of progressive muscle weakness, particularly in his facial and distal limb muscles, and difficulty relaxing his grip after shaking hands (myotonia). He also reports cataracts and cardiac conduction abnormalities. His medical history is significant for insulin-resistant diabetes mellitus. His father, who is now deceased, had similar symptoms that began in his 40s. The patient's 35-year-old son also presents with similar symptoms, including muscle weakness, myotonia, and cataracts, but his symptoms began in his early 20s, significantly earlier than his father or grandfather. Neurological examination of the son reveals ptosis, facial weakness, and distal muscle atrophy. Genetic testing on the patient reveals an expansion of a CTG trinucleotide repeat in the *DMPK* gene. The patient's allele size is 1500 repeats, his father's allele size was 1000 repeats, and his son's allele size is 2000 repeats.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the earlier age of onset and increased severity of myotonic dystrophy type 1 in the patient's son compared to his father and grandfather?

(A) Genomic imprinting, where the expression of the *DMPK* gene is silenced on the paternal chromosome, leading to a more severe phenotype in the son who inherited the expanded allele from his father.
(B) Uniparental disomy, where the son inherited two copies of the chromosome containing the *DMPK* gene from his father, resulting in a higher dosage of the expanded allele.
(C) Expansion of the CTG trinucleotide repeat in the *DMPK* gene during spermatogenesis, leading to a larger repeat size in the son's allele compared to the father's allele.
(D) A point mutation in the *DMPK* gene that occurred spontaneously in the son, leading to a non-functional protein and a more severe phenotype.
(E) Epigenetic modifications, such as DNA methylation, that are stably inherited and cause increased expression of the *DMPK* gene in the son.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with myotonia, muscle weakness (facial, distal), cataracts, cardiac issues, insulin resistance. Symptoms started later in life.
- **Family History:** Father had similar symptoms starting in his 40s. Son (35 years old) has similar symptoms starting in his early 20s. This demonstrates earlier onset in successive generations.
- **Genetic Testing:** Expansion of CTG trinucleotide repeat in the *DMPK* gene. Patient (1500 repeats), Father (1000 repeats), Son (2000 repeats). The repeat size increased from father to son.
- **Diagnosis:** The clinical picture (myotonia, muscle weakness, cataracts, cardiac issues, insulin resistance) combined with the CTG repeat expansion in the *DMPK* gene is characteristic of Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1 (DM1).
- **Key Observation:** The age of onset is progressively earlier (Grandfather -> Father -> Son), and the repeat size is progressively larger (Father -> Son). This pattern is the definition of genetic anticipation.
- **Question:** What molecular mechanism explains this genetic anticipation?

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1 (DM1) is caused by an expansion of a CTG trinucleotide repeat in the 3' untranslated region (UTR) of the *DMPK* (Dystrophia Myotonica Protein Kinase) gene on chromosome 19q13.3.
- The normal *DMPK* gene has fewer than 37 CTG repeats. In DM1, the number of repeats is expanded, typically >50 repeats, but often much larger (hundreds to thousands).
- The expanded CTG repeat in the 3' UTR interferes with the splicing of *DMPK* mRNA, leading to the production of abnormal mRNA transcripts containing microRNAs (specifically, miR-124-5p) that sequester RNA-binding proteins, such as Muscleblind-like 1 (MBNL1).
- MBNL1 is crucial for regulating the splicing of many other mRNAs, particularly those involved in muscle function. Its sequestration leads to widespread splicing abnormalities, contributing to the muscle weakness, myotonia, and other systemic features of DM1.
- **Genetic Anticipation:** A hallmark of DM1 is genetic anticipation, meaning that the disease tends to appear at an earlier age and/or with greater severity in successive generations.
- **Mechanism of Anticipation:** This phenomenon is primarily due to the instability of the CTG repeat during meiosis (gamete formation). The repeat sequence is prone to expansion when passed from parent to offspring, particularly during transmission through the male germline (spermatogenesis), although expansion can also occur during oogenesis. The larger the repeat size in the parent, the greater the likelihood and magnitude of expansion in the offspring.
- In this case, the father had 1000 repeats, and the son inherited 2000 repeats. This increase in repeat size correlates directly with the earlier age of onset in the son compared to his father. The expansion of the repeat leads to increased sequestration of MBNL1 and more severe splicing defects, resulting in a more severe phenotype and earlier onset.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Genomic imprinting involves differential expression of genes depending on whether they are inherited from the mother or the father. While imprinting affects some genetic disorders (e.g., Prader-Willi/Angelman syndromes), DM1 is not typically considered an imprinted disorder. The *DMPK* gene is expressed from both alleles, and anticipation occurs regardless of the parent of origin, although expansion is often more pronounced through the paternal line. This option incorrectly attributes anticipation to imprinting.
- (B): Uniparental disomy (UPD) occurs when an individual inherits two copies of a chromosome (or part of a chromosome) from one parent and no copies from the other parent. While UPD can cause disease by leading to homozygosity for a recessive allele or hemizygosity for a gene on the X chromosome, it does not explain the progressive expansion of trinucleotide repeats seen in genetic anticipation. The patient inherited the *DMPK* gene from both parents, not exclusively from one.
- (D): A point mutation is a change in a single nucleotide base. While point mutations can cause genetic diseases, they do not explain the progressive expansion of trinucleotide repeats characteristic of DM1 and genetic anticipation. The genetic testing specifically identified a CTG repeat expansion, not a point mutation. Furthermore, a spontaneous point mutation in the son would not explain the correlation between repeat size and age of onset across three generations.
- (E): Epigenetic modifications, such as DNA methylation or histone modification, can alter gene expression without changing the underlying DNA sequence. While epigenetic changes can influence disease phenotypes and sometimes be inherited (e.g., imprinting), they are not the primary mechanism driving the expansion of trinucleotide repeats that causes genetic anticipation in DM1. The core mechanism is the inherent instability of the repeat sequence during DNA replication in germ cells.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Genetic anticipation is a key feature of trinucleotide repeat disorders, caused by the expansion of the repeat sequence during gametogenesis. Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1 (DM1) is a classic example, caused by CTG repeat expansion in the *DMPK* gene. Other examples include Huntington's disease (CAG repeat in *HTT* gene) and Fragile X syndrome (CGG repeat in *FMR1* gene). Understand that the mechanism is repeat expansion, often more pronounced in male germline transmission, leading to earlier onset and/or increased severity in subsequent generations. Differentiate anticipation from other genetic phenomena like imprinting, UPD, or point mutations.

---

<a id='question-111'></a>

### Question 111: Huntington Disease Repeat Mechanics: CAG Expansion (Polyglutamine) in Coding Exon, Paternal Transmission Leading to Larger Expansions
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Huntington disease (HD) is an autosomal dominant neurodegenerative disorder caused by a CAG trinucleotide repeat expansion in the *HTT* gene. The expansion occurs within a coding exon, leading to an elongated polyglutamine tract in the huntingtin protein. Anticipation, characterized by earlier onset and increased severity in successive generations, is particularly pronounced with paternal transmission due to instability during spermatogenesis. (亨廷顿病是一种常染色体显性神经退行性疾病，由 *HTT* 基因中 CAG 三核苷酸重复序列的扩增引起。扩增发生在编码外显子中，导致亨廷顿蛋白中多谷氨酰胺片段延长。期待现象，即后代发病年龄更早、病情更严重，在父系遗传中尤为明显，这是由于在精子发生过程中不稳定。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male presents to a neurologist with progressive chorea, cognitive decline, and psychiatric disturbances. His father was diagnosed with Huntington disease at age 45 and died at age 60. The patient's paternal grandfather also had HD, diagnosed at age 50. The patient underwent genetic testing, which revealed an *HTT* gene mutation with 45 CAG repeats. His daughter, age 25, is considering genetic testing. She is concerned because her father's symptoms began earlier than his father's and grandfather's. She asks the neurologist about the inheritance pattern and the likelihood of her developing the disease. The neurologist explains that the CAG repeat number tends to increase with each generation, especially through the paternal line. The patient's father had 40 CAG repeats, and his paternal grandfather had 35 CAG repeats. The daughter's genetic test reveals 50 CAG repeats.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the observed increase in CAG repeats from the paternal grandfather to the patient, and the further increase from the patient to his daughter?
(A) Slippage of DNA polymerase during replication in somatic cells, leading to expansion in both males and females equally.
(B) Unequal crossing over during meiosis in somatic cells, resulting in expansion primarily in females.
(C) Slippage of DNA polymerase during replication in germline cells (specifically spermatogenesis), leading to expansion that is more pronounced with paternal transmission.
(D) Strand breaks and re-ligation during DNA repair in somatic cells, causing contraction of the repeat sequence.
(E) Increased transcription of the *HTT* gene in somatic cells, leading to increased protein aggregation and symptom severity.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 52-year-old male with classic HD symptoms (chorea, cognitive decline, psychiatric issues).
- **Family History:** Autosomal dominant inheritance pattern (father, paternal grandfather affected). Father diagnosed at 45, grandfather at 50, patient at 52 (suggests anticipation - earlier onset).
- **Genetic Testing:** Patient has 45 CAG repeats. Father had 40 repeats. Paternal grandfather had 35 repeats. Daughter has 50 repeats.
- **Key Observation:** CAG repeat number increased across generations (35 -> 40 -> 45) and further increased in the daughter (45 -> 50). The increase is more significant between the grandfather and father, and again between the father and daughter.
- **Neurologist's Explanation:** Anticipation is linked to increased repeat numbers with each generation, particularly through the paternal line.
- **Question:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the repeat expansion and its pattern (especially paternal transmission).

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Huntington Disease:** Caused by CAG repeat expansion in the *HTT* gene (chromosome 4p16.3).
- **Gene Structure:** The CAG repeats are located within exon 1 of the *HTT* gene.
- **Protein Product:** The expanded CAG repeats translate into an elongated polyglutamine (polyQ) tract in the N-terminus of the huntingtin protein. This abnormal protein misfolds and aggregates, leading to neuronal dysfunction and death, particularly in the striatum and cortex.
- **Mechanism of Expansion:** The expansion occurs primarily during DNA replication in germline cells (sperm and egg). The mechanism involves DNA polymerase slippage. During replication of the highly repetitive CAG sequence, the polymerase can "stutter" or slip, either adding extra CAG repeats (leading to expansion) or deleting repeats (leading to contraction).
- **Anticipation & Paternal Transmission:** Anticipation (earlier onset, increased severity) is observed because the repeat number tends to increase across generations. This instability is more pronounced during spermatogenesis (paternal transmission) than during oogenesis (maternal transmission). In males, the *HTT* gene is replicated late in meiosis, and the number of divisions is higher, providing more opportunities for polymerase slippage and expansion. Furthermore, the paternal allele is generally transmitted more frequently than the maternal allele, contributing to the observed pattern. The number of repeats generally correlates with the age of onset: <26 repeats = normal; 27-35 repeats = reduced penetrance; 36-39 repeats = variable penetrance/later onset; ≥40 repeats = high penetrance/earlier onset.
- **Relevance to Vignette:** The family history shows anticipation (earlier onset in successive generations). The repeat numbers confirm expansion (35 -> 40 -> 45 -> 50). The neurologist's explanation points to paternal transmission as a key factor. Therefore, the mechanism must involve germline replication slippage, more pronounced in males.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Slippage of DNA polymerase during replication *can* cause expansion, but it occurs primarily in *germline* cells, not somatic cells. Also, while slippage occurs in both males and females, the *effect* (expansion) is more pronounced during paternal transmission. This option incorrectly localizes the primary event to somatic cells and doesn't capture the sex-specific difference in expansion rate.
- (B): Unequal crossing over is a mechanism for repeat expansion in some disorders (e.g., Fragile X syndrome, Myotonic Dystrophy), but it's not the primary mechanism for HD. Furthermore, unequal crossing over occurs during meiosis, but the instability in HD is primarily attributed to polymerase slippage during DNA replication, and the effect is more pronounced paternally, not primarily in females.
- (D): Strand breaks and re-ligation during DNA repair can occur, but they typically lead to *contraction* or loss of repeats, not expansion, in trinucleotide repeat disorders. This is the opposite of what is observed in HD.
- (E): Increased transcription of the *HTT* gene in somatic cells does occur in HD, leading to increased levels of the mutant huntingtin protein. However, this explains the *pathogenesis* (protein aggregation, toxicity) and potentially symptom severity, but it does *not* explain the *mechanism of repeat expansion* across generations (anticipation). The expansion is a genetic event occurring during germline replication.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Huntington disease is caused by CAG repeat expansion in the *HTT* gene, leading to anticipation (earlier onset/increased severity in successive generations), particularly with paternal transmission, due to DNA polymerase slippage during spermatogenesis.
- **Differentials:** Other trinucleotide repeat disorders include Fragile X (CGG repeat, X-linked, unequal crossing over), Myotonic Dystrophy (CTG repeat, autosomal dominant, unequal crossing over), Spinocerebellar Ataxias (various repeats, autosomal dominant, polymerase slippage). Understand the specific gene, repeat type, location (coding vs. non-coding), and primary mechanism (slippage vs. crossing over) for each.

---

<a id='question-112'></a>

### Question 112: Fragile X Syndrome Inheritance and Molecular Mechanism
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Fragile X syndrome is caused by an expansion of a CGG trinucleotide repeat in the 5' untranslated region (UTR) of the *FMR1* gene, leading to methylation and silencing of the gene. This expansion is subject to anticipation, particularly through maternal transmission, due to instability during oogenesis. (脆性X综合征是由 *FMR1* 基因 5' 非编码区 CGG 三核苷酸重复序列的扩增引起的，导致基因甲基化和沉默。这种扩增具有预期性，尤其是在母系遗传中，因为在卵子形成过程中不稳定。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 4-year-old boy presents to the pediatrician with developmental delay, speech impairment, and autistic features. His physical examination reveals a long face, prominent jaw, large ears, and macroorchidism. His mother has a history of mild learning difficulties and anxiety, while his father is phenotypically normal. Family history reveals that the mother's father (the boy's maternal grandfather) had intellectual disability. Genetic testing on the boy shows a markedly expanded CGG repeat count of 850 in the *FMR1* gene. His mother's repeat count is 210, and his father's repeat count is 28. The boy's maternal grandfather had a repeat count of 100.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the transmission of the expanded *FMR1* CGG repeat from the maternal grandfather to the mother, and subsequently from the mother to the son, leading to the boy's phenotype?

(A) The CGG repeat expansion occurs primarily during spermatogenesis due to errors in DNA replication repair mechanisms, leading to paternal anticipation.
(B) The CGG repeat expansion occurs primarily during oogenesis due to errors in DNA replication repair mechanisms, leading to maternal anticipation.
(C) The CGG repeat expansion occurs primarily during mitosis in somatic cells, leading to mosaicism in the germline.
(D) The CGG repeat expansion occurs primarily due to a point mutation in the DNA polymerase enzyme responsible for replicating the *FMR1* gene.
(E) The CGG repeat expansion occurs primarily due to a deficiency in the mismatch repair system, leading to an equal likelihood of expansion during both oogenesis and spermatogenesis.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 4-year-old boy with developmental delay, speech impairment, autistic features, long face, prominent jaw, large ears, macroorchidism – classic features of Fragile X syndrome.
*   **Family History:** Mother has mild learning difficulties (possible premutation carrier), maternal grandfather had intellectual disability (likely full mutation carrier). Father is normal. This pattern suggests X-linked inheritance with potential anticipation.
*   **Genetic Testing:**
    *   Boy: CGG repeat count = 850 (Full mutation range: >200 repeats, typically >230, often >1000). This confirms Fragile X syndrome.
    *   Mother: CGG repeat count = 210 (Premutation range: 55-200 repeats). She is a premutation carrier.
    *   Father: CGG repeat count = 28 (Normal range: <55 repeats). He is unaffected and not a carrier.
    *   Maternal Grandfather: CGG repeat count = 100 (Intermediate allele range: 55-200 repeats, or potentially a low-end premutation). This suggests he may have had a premutation that expanded further in his daughter (the mother).
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the transmission and expansion of the CGG repeat, specifically linking it to the observed inheritance pattern (maternal transmission, anticipation).

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Fragile X Syndrome Genetics:** Caused by expansion of a CGG trinucleotide repeat in the 5' UTR of the *FMR1* gene on the X chromosome.
*   **Normal Allele:** <55 repeats.
*   **Intermediate Allele:** 55-200 repeats.
*   **Premutation Allele:** 55-200 repeats. Carriers may have Fragile X-associated tremor/ataxia syndrome (FXTAS) or Fragile X-associated primary ovarian insufficiency (FXPOI).
*   **Full Mutation Allele:** >200 repeats (often >230, typically >1000). Associated with Fragile X syndrome.
*   **Mechanism of Expansion:** The CGG repeat is prone to expansion during DNA replication. This instability is particularly pronounced during oogenesis (egg formation) compared to spermatogenesis (sperm formation).
*   **Anticipation:** The phenomenon where the severity of a genetic disorder increases, or the age of onset decreases, in successive generations. In Fragile X, anticipation is primarily due to the tendency for the CGG repeat to expand further during transmission through the female germline.
*   **Maternal Transmission:** The mother (with 210 repeats, a premutation) transmitted the allele to her son. The repeat expanded from 210 to 850 during oogenesis.
*   **Paternal Transmission:** The father (with 28 repeats, normal) did not transmit the allele. Full mutations are typically silenced by methylation, preventing transcription of the *FMR1* gene and often leading to a lack of FMRP protein. This silencing can also affect sperm development, potentially reducing the number of sperm carrying the full mutation allele. However, the primary driver of *expansion* is oogenesis.
*   **Molecular Basis of Instability:** The exact mechanism is complex but involves factors like DNA secondary structure formation (G-quadruplexes) at the repeat, slippage of the DNA polymerase during replication, and inefficient repair of the resulting loops. These processes are thought to be more error-prone in the oocyte nucleus compared to the spermatogonia.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) The CGG repeat expansion occurs primarily during spermatogenesis due to errors in DNA replication repair mechanisms, leading to paternal anticipation.** Incorrect. While expansion can occur during spermatogenesis, it is significantly less frequent and less pronounced than during oogenesis. Paternal transmission of a full mutation is possible but less common, and anticipation is primarily a maternal phenomenon in Fragile X. The father in this case had a normal repeat count, so paternal transmission of the expanded allele is not relevant here.
*   **(B) The CGG repeat expansion occurs primarily during oogenesis due to errors in DNA replication repair mechanisms, leading to maternal anticipation.** Correct. This accurately describes the known mechanism of CGG repeat expansion in Fragile X syndrome. The instability is highest during oogenesis, leading to significant expansion across generations through the maternal line (maternal anticipation). The mother's premutation allele expanded significantly during oogenesis to cause the full mutation in her son.
*   **(C) The CGG repeat expansion occurs primarily during mitosis in somatic cells, leading to mosaicism in the germline.** Incorrect. The expansion occurs during meiosis (specifically oogenesis) in the germline, not primarily during mitosis in somatic cells. While somatic mosaicism can occur in some genetic disorders, it is not the primary mechanism for the repeat expansion and anticipation seen in Fragile X.
*   **(D) The CGG repeat expansion occurs primarily due to a point mutation in the DNA polymerase enzyme responsible for replicating the *FMR1* gene.** Incorrect. The expansion is not caused by a point mutation in the polymerase itself. It's due to the inherent instability of the long repeat sequence during DNA replication, involving slippage and repair errors, exacerbated during oogenesis.
*   **(E) The CGG repeat expansion occurs primarily due to a deficiency in the mismatch repair system, leading to an equal likelihood of expansion during both oogenesis and spermatogenesis.** Incorrect. While mismatch repair plays a role in DNA replication fidelity, the instability of CGG repeats is not primarily attributed to a general deficiency in this system. Furthermore, the expansion is *not* equally likely during oogenesis and spermatogenesis; it is significantly more pronounced during oogenesis.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Fragile X syndrome is a classic example of trinucleotide repeat expansion disease exhibiting anticipation, primarily through maternal transmission due to instability during oogenesis. Remember that full mutations (>200 repeats) cause the syndrome, premutations (55-200 repeats) can cause other disorders (FXTAS, FXPOI), and normal alleles have <55 repeats. Key differentials for developmental delay include Down syndrome (trisomy 21), other chromosomal abnormalities, and single-gene disorders like Rett syndrome (MECP2 mutation). Key differentials for trinucleotide repeat disorders include Huntington's disease (CAG expansion in HTT), Myotonic dystrophy (CTG expansion in DMPK), and Kennedy's disease (CAG expansion in AR).

<a id='question-113'></a>

### Question 113: Fragile X Syndrome: FMR1 Promoter Methylation and FMRP Deficiency
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the molecular mechanism underlying Fragile X syndrome, specifically the role of FMR1 promoter hypermethylation leading to the silencing of the FMR1 gene and subsequent deficiency of the Fragile X Mental Retardation Protein (FMRP), which affects synaptic plasticity and translation.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy is brought to the pediatrician by his parents due to developmental delay, particularly in speech and language. He exhibits features such as a long face, prominent jaw, large ears, and macroorchidism. His older brother has a confirmed diagnosis of autism spectrum disorder. Family history reveals that the boy's maternal grandfather had intellectual disability. A genetic test is performed on the boy, revealing an expanded CGG repeat in the 5' untranslated region (UTR) of the FMR1 gene. Southern blot analysis shows a markedly increased size of the FMR1 allele compared to normal controls, and methylation-specific PCR confirms hypermethylation of the FMR1 promoter region. Western blot analysis of fibroblasts from the patient shows a complete absence of FMRP protein. Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most directly responsible for the absence of FMRP protein in this patient?

(A) Point mutation within the coding sequence of the FMR1 gene leading to a premature stop codon.
(B) Deletion of the entire FMR1 gene.
(C) Hypermethylation of the CGG repeat expansion in the FMR1 promoter region, leading to transcriptional silencing.
(D) Increased transcription of the FMR1 gene due to the CGG repeat expansion, resulting in mRNA instability and degradation.
(E) Reduced translation efficiency of the FMR1 mRNA due to sequestration by the expanded CGG repeat.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with developmental delay (speech/language), characteristic physical features (long face, prominent jaw, large ears, macroorchidism), family history suggestive of X-linked inheritance (maternal grandfather with intellectual disability, older brother with autism). These features strongly suggest Fragile X syndrome.
- **Genetic Test Findings:** Expanded CGG repeat in the 5' UTR of the FMR1 gene. This is the hallmark genetic finding in Fragile X syndrome.
- **Southern Blot:** Markedly increased size of the FMR1 allele. This confirms the presence of the expanded CGG repeat, which appears as a larger band on the Southern blot due to the increased size of the repeat region.
- **Methylation-Specific PCR:** Confirms hypermethylation of the FMR1 promoter region. This is the key epigenetic modification responsible for gene silencing in most cases of Fragile X syndrome.
- **Western Blot:** Complete absence of FMRP protein. This confirms the functional consequence of the genetic and epigenetic changes – the lack of the protein product.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most direct* molecular mechanism responsible for the absence of FMRP protein, given the constellation of findings.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Fragile X Syndrome Pathogenesis:** Fragile X syndrome is caused by mutations in the FMR1 gene located on the X chromosome (Xq27.3). The most common mutation is an expansion of a CGG trinucleotide repeat in the 5' UTR of the FMR1 gene.
- **Normal vs. Mutant FMR1:** In normal individuals, the CGG repeat number is typically between 5 and 44. In individuals with Fragile X syndrome, the repeat number is usually >200.
- **Mechanism of Silencing:** When the CGG repeat expands beyond a certain threshold (typically >200), the repeat region becomes heavily methylated. This hypermethylation occurs primarily at CpG dinucleotides within the repeat sequence.
- **Transcriptional Repression:** The hypermethylation of the FMR1 promoter region leads to the recruitment of methyl-binding domain (MBD) proteins and histone deacetylases (HDACs). This complex compacts the chromatin structure (heterochromatin formation), making the promoter inaccessible to transcription factors and RNA polymerase II, thereby silencing the FMR1 gene transcriptionally.
- **FMRP Function:** The FMR1 gene encodes the Fragile X Mental Retardation Protein (FMRP), an RNA-binding protein crucial for regulating the translation of numerous mRNAs, particularly those involved in synaptic plasticity and neuronal development. Deficiency of FMRP leads to the characteristic neurological and behavioral features of Fragile X syndrome.
- **Connecting the Clues:** The vignette provides strong evidence for Fragile X syndrome (clinical features, family history, expanded CGG repeat, hypermethylation, absent FMRP). The question asks for the direct cause of the absent protein. The expanded CGG repeat leads to promoter hypermethylation, which silences transcription, resulting in the absence of FMRP mRNA and consequently, the absence of FMRP protein.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Point mutation within the coding sequence leading to a premature stop codon.** While point mutations can cause genetic disorders, they are not the primary mechanism in the vast majority (>95%) of Fragile X syndrome cases. The vignette specifically points to an expanded CGG repeat and promoter hypermethylation, not a coding sequence mutation. This distractor tests the understanding that Fragile X is primarily a repeat expansion disorder with epigenetic silencing.
- (B): **Deletion of the entire FMR1 gene.** Gene deletions are a cause of some genetic disorders, but they are not the typical cause of Fragile X syndrome. The Southern blot shows an *increased* size of the allele due to the expanded repeat, not the absence of the allele. This distractor confuses Fragile X with other genetic disorders caused by deletions.
- (D): **Increased transcription of the FMR1 gene due to the CGG repeat expansion, resulting in mRNA instability and degradation.** The expanded CGG repeat does *not* lead to increased transcription; it leads to transcriptional silencing via hypermethylation. The mRNA is not inherently unstable due to the repeat itself, but rather its production is blocked. This distractor reverses the actual mechanism of gene expression regulation.
- (E): **Reduced translation efficiency of the FMR1 mRNA due to sequestration by the expanded CGG repeat.** While the expanded CGG repeat is located in the 5' UTR, it does not directly sequester the mRNA during translation. The primary problem is the lack of mRNA production due to transcriptional silencing. Although FMRP itself regulates translation, the *absence* of FMRP in this case is due to lack of its synthesis, not a problem with the translation of its own mRNA (which is absent). This distractor confuses the function of FMRP (regulating translation) with the mechanism of its own deficiency.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Fragile X syndrome is primarily caused by CGG repeat expansion in the FMR1 promoter leading to hypermethylation and transcriptional silencing, resulting in FMRP deficiency. Key features include intellectual disability, characteristic facial features, macroorchidism, and behavioral issues. Differentiate Fragile X from other causes of intellectual disability (e.g., Down syndrome - trisomy 21, point mutations, deletions) and other trinucleotide repeat disorders (e.g., Huntington's disease - CAG expansion in HTT, Myotonic dystrophy - CTG expansion in DMPK). The epigenetic mechanism (hypermethylation) is crucial for understanding Fragile X pathogenesis.

---

<a id='question-114'></a>

### Question 114: Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Myotonic dystrophy type 1 (DM1) is caused by a CTG trinucleotide repeat expansion in the 3' untranslated region (UTR) of the *DMPK* gene, leading to RNA toxicity and aberrant splicing. (肌强直营养不良 1 型 (DM1) 是由 *DMPK* 基因 3' 非翻译区 (UTR) 中 CTG 三核苷酸重复序列的扩增引起的，导致 RNA 毒性和异常剪接。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male presents to his primary care physician complaining of progressive muscle weakness, particularly in his facial and distal limb muscles, and myotonia (difficulty relaxing muscles after contraction). He also reports cataracts, cardiac conduction abnormalities, and cognitive impairment. His father had similar symptoms, and his mother was unaffected. Neurological examination reveals ptosis, temporal wasting, and a positive myotonic grip test. Electromyography (EMG) shows characteristic myotonic discharges. Genetic testing reveals an expansion of the CTG repeat in the 3' UTR of the *DMPK* gene, with 1500 repeats. A muscle biopsy shows type 1 fiber atrophy and central nuclei. Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the pathogenesis of this patient's condition?

(A) Gain-of-function mutation leading to increased activity of the DMPK protein, resulting in sequestration of RNA-binding proteins and impaired mRNA splicing.
(B) Loss-of-function mutation leading to decreased expression of the DMPK protein, causing mitochondrial dysfunction and oxidative stress.
(C) Formation of toxic aggregates of the expanded CTG repeat RNA within the nucleus, sequestering splicing factors and leading to aberrant splicing of multiple pre-mRNAs.
(D) Increased expression of the DMPK protein due to the expanded CTG repeat acting as an enhancer, leading to disruption of cytoskeletal structure and muscle fiber damage.
(E) Impaired protein degradation pathway (ubiquitin-proteasome system) due to the expanded CTG repeat RNA, leading to accumulation of misfolded proteins and cellular dysfunction.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 45-year-old male with progressive muscle weakness (facial, distal limbs), myotonia, cataracts, cardiac conduction abnormalities, cognitive impairment. These are classic features of Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1 (DM1).
- **Family History:** Father affected, mother unaffected, suggesting autosomal dominant inheritance with anticipation (severity increases in successive generations), common in trinucleotide repeat disorders.
- **Physical Exam:** Ptosis, temporal wasting, positive myotonic grip test are characteristic signs of DM1.
- **EMG:** Myotonic discharges confirm the presence of myotonia.
- **Genetic Testing:** Expansion of the CTG repeat in the 3' UTR of the *DMPK* gene with 1500 repeats confirms the diagnosis of DM1. Repeat numbers > 50 are premutation, > 200 are typically associated with adult-onset DM1, and > 1000 are associated with congenital DM1.
- **Muscle Biopsy:** Type 1 fiber atrophy and central nuclei are histological findings consistent with DM1.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the pathogenesis.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **DM1 Pathogenesis:** DM1 is caused by an expansion of a CTG trinucleotide repeat in the 3' UTR of the *DMPK* gene on chromosome 19.
- **Mechanism:** The expanded CTG repeat RNA transcript (DMPK mRNA) is abnormally long and forms stable secondary structures (hairpins). These structures sequester multiple RNA-binding proteins, most notably the hnRNPA1 (heterogeneous nuclear ribonucleoprotein A1) and other splicing factors.
- **RNA Toxicity:** Sequestration of these splicing factors leads to their depletion from the cytoplasm and nucleus, where they are needed for normal pre-mRNA splicing. This results in aberrant splicing of numerous target mRNAs (estimated > 1000s), leading to the production of non-functional or dysfunctional proteins in various tissues (muscle, heart, brain, lens, testes, endocrine glands, etc.). This widespread disruption of protein function explains the multi-systemic nature of DM1.
- **Key Points:** The expanded RNA itself is toxic, not the protein product (DMPK protein is normally expressed and its function is not fully understood, but it is not the primary cause of the disease). The toxicity stems from the sequestration of splicing factors and the resulting widespread aberrant splicing.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** While the expanded RNA does lead to impaired mRNA splicing, the primary mechanism is not increased *activity* of the DMPK protein. The DMPK protein itself is not considered the main pathogenic factor; rather, the expanded RNA transcript causes RNA toxicity. Sequestration of RNA-binding proteins is correct, but the "gain-of-function mutation leading to increased activity of the DMPK protein" part is wrong.
- (B): **Incorrect.** DM1 is not caused by a loss-of-function mutation leading to decreased DMPK protein expression. The DMPK protein is expressed, and the disease mechanism involves the expanded RNA transcript, not a lack of protein. Mitochondrial dysfunction and oxidative stress can occur secondary to the disease process but are not the primary initiating mechanism.
- (D): **Incorrect.** The expanded CTG repeat does not act as an enhancer to increase DMPK protein expression. While some studies suggest potential effects on transcription, the primary pathogenic mechanism is RNA toxicity due to sequestration of splicing factors, not increased protein expression disrupting the cytoskeleton.
- (E): **Incorrect.** While cellular dysfunction occurs, the primary mechanism is not impaired protein degradation. The main issue is aberrant splicing of numerous mRNAs due to the sequestration of splicing factors by the expanded CTG repeat RNA. Impaired protein degradation might be a secondary consequence in some contexts, but it's not the central pathogenic mechanism.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1 is a classic example of a trinucleotide repeat disorder caused by RNA toxicity. The expanded CTG repeat in the *DMPK* 3' UTR leads to sequestration of splicing factors, causing widespread aberrant splicing and multi-systemic disease.
- **Differentials:** Other trinucleotide repeat disorders include Huntington's disease (CAG expansion in *HTT*), Fragile X syndrome (CGG expansion in *FMR1*), and Friedreich's ataxia (GAA expansion in *FXN*). Each has a different gene, repeat sequence, and primary pathogenic mechanism (e.g., Huntington's involves a toxic gain-of-function protein, Fragile X involves methylation-mediated gene silencing). DM1 is unique in that the expanded RNA itself is the primary toxic agent causing aberrant splicing.

---

<a id='question-115'></a>

### Question 115: Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the genetic basis, molecular mechanism, and clinical manifestations of Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1 (DM1), specifically focusing on the expanded CTG repeat in the *DMPK* gene and its impact on RNA processing and protein function. (考察第一型肌强直性肌营养不良症 (DM1) 的遗传基础、分子机制和临床表现，特别是 *DMPK* 基因中 CTG 重复序列的扩增及其对 RNA 加工和蛋白质功能的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male presents to his primary care physician complaining of progressive muscle weakness, particularly in his distal limbs, and difficulty releasing his grip after shaking hands (myotonia). He also reports experiencing frontal balding and blurred vision, which he attributes to developing cataracts. His father exhibited similar symptoms, including muscle weakness and cataracts, starting in his 50s. On physical examination, the patient demonstrates delayed muscle relaxation after handgrip and has noticeable temporal balding. Slit-lamp examination reveals early cataracts. Genetic testing reveals an expansion of a CTG trinucleotide repeat in the 3' untranslated region (UTR) of the *DMPK* gene. The repeat count is 1500, significantly above the normal range (<37). Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the pathogenesis of this patient's condition?

(A) Gain-of-function mutation leading to constitutive activation of a tyrosine kinase receptor, resulting in uncontrolled cell proliferation and muscle hypertrophy.
(B) Loss-of-function mutation causing deficient splicing of pre-mRNA due to mutations in spliceosome components, leading to accumulation of unspliced transcripts and impaired protein synthesis.
(C) Gain-of-function mutation involving the formation of toxic RNA aggregates (ribonuclear foci) due to the expanded repeat sequestering RNA-binding proteins, leading to aberrant pre-mRNA splicing and reduced expression of the *DMPK* transcript and other target mRNAs.
(D) Loss-of-function mutation resulting in a non-functional protein product due to a frameshift mutation caused by the expanded repeat, leading to complete absence of the protein's normal function.
(E) Gain-of-function mutation causing misfolding and aggregation of the DMPK protein in the endoplasmic reticulum, leading to ER stress and activation of the unfolded protein response (UPR).

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 45-year-old male with progressive distal muscle weakness, myotonia (delayed grip release), frontal balding, and cataracts. These are classic features of Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1 (DM1).
*   **Family History:** Father had similar symptoms, suggesting an autosomal dominant inheritance pattern, consistent with DM1.
*   **Physical Exam:** Confirms myotonia and frontal balding.
*   **Ophthalmologic Exam:** Confirms cataracts.
*   **Genetic Testing:** Expansion of CTG trinucleotide repeat (1500 copies) in the 3' UTR of the *DMPK* gene. This is the definitive diagnostic finding for DM1. Normal repeats are typically <37.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the pathogenesis.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **DM1 Genetics:** DM1 is caused by an expansion of a CTG trinucleotide repeat in the 3' UTR of the *DMPK* (dystrophia myotonica protein kinase) gene on chromosome 19q13.3.
*   **Molecular Mechanism:** The expanded CTG repeat in the *DMPK* mRNA forms a stable secondary structure (hairpin loop). This structure acts as a "molecular sponge," sequestering numerous RNA-binding proteins (RBPs), including splicing factors like hnRNPA1, hnRNPA2, and others.
*   **Pathophysiology:**
    *   **RNA Foci:** The sequestration of RBPs leads to the formation of cytoplasmic aggregates called ribonuclear foci (RNF).
    *   **Aberrant Splicing:** The depletion of splicing factors from the nucleus causes widespread aberrant splicing of numerous pre-mRNAs, affecting hundreds of different genes. This leads to the production of non-functional or altered proteins.
    *   **Reduced DMPK Expression:** The expanded repeat and RNF formation also lead to reduced levels of the normal DMPK protein. The exact function of DMPK is not fully understood, but it may be involved in regulating RNA metabolism or ion channel function.
    *   **Clinical Manifestations:** The combination of aberrant splicing affecting multiple tissues and reduced DMPK function contributes to the multi-systemic clinical features of DM1, including muscle weakness, myotonia, cataracts, cardiac conduction defects, endocrine dysfunction, and cognitive impairment.
*   **Key Concept:** The pathogenesis of DM1 is primarily driven by a toxic gain-of-function mechanism at the RNA level, where the expanded repeat sequesters RBPs, leading to aberrant splicing and RNF formation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** DM1 is not caused by constitutive activation of a tyrosine kinase receptor. This mechanism is more characteristic of certain cancers or growth disorders. DM1 involves RNA pathology, not receptor activation. The muscle weakness is progressive atrophy, not hypertrophy.
- (B): **Incorrect.** While aberrant splicing is a key feature of DM1, the primary defect is not a loss-of-function mutation in spliceosome components themselves. Instead, the expanded repeat sequesters *existing* splicing factors, leading to splicing defects in *multiple* genes. This option describes a mechanism where the spliceosome itself is deficient, which is not the case in DM1.
- (D): **Incorrect.** The expanded CTG repeat does not typically cause a frameshift mutation leading to a completely non-functional protein. While DMPK protein levels are reduced, the primary pathology stems from the RNA pathology (RNA foci, aberrant splicing), not the complete absence of the protein due to a frameshift. The repeat is in the 3' UTR, which is usually outside the coding sequence, although very large expansions can sometimes affect the coding region or translation. However, the main mechanism is RNA-based.
- (E): **Incorrect.** While ER stress and UPR activation can occur in some muscle diseases, the primary molecular mechanism in DM1 is the sequestration of RNA-binding proteins by the expanded CTG repeat RNA, leading to aberrant splicing and RNF formation. Misfolding and aggregation of the DMPK protein itself are not considered the central pathogenic mechanism, although altered protein function might contribute. The core issue is the toxic RNA.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1 (DM1) is caused by a toxic gain-of-function mechanism involving expanded CTG repeats in the *DMPK* gene 3' UTR, leading to RNA sequestration of splicing factors, aberrant splicing, and ribonuclear foci formation. Key clinical features include myotonia, distal muscle weakness, cataracts, and frontal balding. Differential diagnoses include other myopathies (e.g., muscular dystrophies like Duchenne/Becker, limb-girdle), myotonias (e.g., myotonic dystrophy type 2, paramyotonia congenita), and other neuromuscular disorders. Remember the specific gene (*DMPK*), repeat type (CTG), and location (3' UTR) for DM1.

<a id='question-116'></a>

### Question 116: Friedreich Ataxia
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Friedreich ataxia is an autosomal recessive neurodegenerative disorder caused by an expanded GAA trinucleotide repeat in the first intron of the *FXN* gene, leading to reduced expression of frataxin protein, which is crucial for mitochondrial iron-sulfur cluster (ISC) biogenesis. (Friedreich 遗 ataxia 是一种常染色体隐性遗传的神经退行性疾病，由 *FXN* 基因第一个内含子中的 GAA 三核苷酸重复序列扩增引起，导致编码铁硫蛋白 (frataxin) 的表达减少，而铁硫蛋白对线粒体铁硫簇 (ISC) 的生物合成至关重要。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 12-year-old boy presents to his pediatrician with progressive gait ataxia, slurred speech (dysarthria), and frequent falls over the past 2 years. His parents report that he initially had difficulty with coordination during sports but now struggles with walking even short distances. Physical examination reveals bilateral lower limb ataxia, decreased deep tendon reflexes, extensor plantar responses (Babinski sign), and mild scoliosis. Neurological examination also shows impaired vibration and position sense in the lower extremities. His older sister has a similar, though milder, presentation. Laboratory investigations show normal complete blood count, comprehensive metabolic panel, and thyroid function tests. An electrocardiogram (ECG) reveals hypertrophic cardiomyopathy. Genetic testing is performed. Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) A missense mutation in the *HTT* gene leading to an expanded polyglutamine tract in the huntingtin protein, causing aggregation and neuronal dysfunction.
(B) A deletion of the *SMN1* gene, resulting in insufficient survival motor neuron protein and subsequent degeneration of motor neurons.
(C) An expanded CAG repeat in the *ATXN1* gene, leading to a toxic gain-of-function of the ataxin-1 protein and cerebellar Purkinje cell loss.
(D) An expanded GAA repeat in the first intron of the *FXN* gene, leading to reduced expression of frataxin and impaired mitochondrial iron-sulfur cluster biogenesis.
(E) A mutation in the *ABCA4* gene causing impaired retinal transport of all-trans-retinol, leading to photoreceptor degeneration and progressive vision loss.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 12-year-old boy with progressive gait ataxia, dysarthria, falls, difficulty with coordination. This points towards a neurodegenerative disorder affecting the cerebellum and spinal cord pathways.
- **Physical Exam:** Bilateral lower limb ataxia, decreased DTRs, extensor plantar responses (Babinski sign), scoliosis, impaired vibration/position sense. These findings are characteristic of sensory ataxia and upper motor neuron signs, often seen in spinocerebellar degenerations.
- **Family History:** Older sister with similar, milder symptoms suggests an autosomal recessive inheritance pattern, although autosomal dominant with variable expressivity cannot be entirely ruled out without more information.
- **ECG:** Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy is a common extra-neurological manifestation associated with certain genetic disorders.
- **Age of Onset:** Onset in childhood/adolescence is typical for Friedreich ataxia.
- **Differential Diagnosis based on Clues:** The combination of progressive ataxia, sensory deficits, upper motor neuron signs, scoliosis, cardiomyopathy, and childhood onset strongly suggests Friedreich ataxia.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Friedreich Ataxia (FRDA):** The most common inherited ataxia, typically presenting before age 25. It is an autosomal recessive disorder.
- **Genetic Cause:** Caused by an expansion of a GAA trinucleotide repeat in the first intron of the *FXN* gene (also known as *RACK1*) on chromosome 9q13.
- **Normal Allele:** Typically has 5-35 GAA repeats.
- **Disease Allele:** Patients usually have >66 GAA repeats (often 200-1000+).
- **Mechanism:** The expanded GAA repeat causes hypermethylation of the promoter region (CpG islands) within the first intron. This aberrant methylation silences the *FXN* gene, leading to reduced transcription and consequently, decreased levels of frataxin protein.
- **Frataxin Function:** Frataxin is a mitochondrial protein involved in iron-sulfur cluster (ISC) biogenesis. ISCs are essential cofactors for numerous mitochondrial enzymes, including those involved in the electron transport chain (complexes I, II, III, IV), pyruvate dehydrogenase, and α-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase.
- **Pathophysiology:** Reduced frataxin levels impair ISC assembly, leading to mitochondrial dysfunction, oxidative stress, impaired energy metabolism, and ultimately, neuronal degeneration, particularly in the dorsal root ganglia (sensory neuropathy), spinocerebellar tracts (ataxia), and heart (cardiomyopathy).
- **Inheritance:** Autosomal recessive. The patient's affected sister supports this pattern.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Huntington Disease (HD)**. Caused by an expanded CAG repeat in the *HTT* gene. Leads to an elongated polyglutamine tract in the huntingtin protein, causing protein misfolding, aggregation, and neuronal death, primarily in the basal ganglia and cortex. Presents typically in adulthood (30s-40s) with chorea, cognitive decline, and psychiatric symptoms. The expanded repeat is CAG, not GAA. The primary pathology involves the basal ganglia, not primarily the spinocerebellar tracts and dorsal root ganglia.
- (B): **Spinal Muscular Atrophy (SMA)**. Caused by homozygous deletion or mutation of the *SMN1* gene on chromosome 5q13. Results in deficiency of the survival motor neuron (SMN) protein, leading to degeneration of lower motor neurons in the spinal cord and brainstem. Presents with progressive muscle weakness and atrophy, typically starting in infancy or childhood. Does not typically cause ataxia, sensory loss, or cardiomyopathy. The genetic defect is a gene deletion, not a repeat expansion.
- (C): **Spinocerebellar Ataxia type 1 (SCA1)**. Caused by an expanded CAG repeat in the *ATXN1* gene. Leads to a toxic gain-of-function of the ataxin-1 protein. Presents with progressive cerebellar ataxia, typically in adulthood. While it involves ataxia, the genetic mechanism (CAG repeat in *ATXN1*) and typical age of onset differ from the vignette.
- (E): **Stargardt Disease**. Caused by mutations (often deletions or missense mutations, less commonly repeat expansions) in the *ABCA4* gene. Leads to impaired transport of all-trans-retinol across the photoreceptor disc membrane, causing accumulation of lipofuscin and degeneration of photoreceptor cells. Presents with progressive central vision loss, typically starting in childhood or adolescence. This is a retinal dystrophy, not a neurodegenerative ataxia, and does not involve cardiomyopathy.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Friedreich ataxia is the most common hereditary ataxia, characterized by GAA repeat expansion in the *FXN* gene intron, leading to reduced frataxin and impaired mitochondrial ISC biogenesis. Key clinical features include ataxia, sensory neuropathy, upper motor neuron signs, scoliosis, and cardiomyopathy, typically presenting in childhood/adolescence. Differentiate from other ataxias (HD, SCA1) based on genetic mechanism (repeat type and gene), age of onset, and specific clinical features. Differentiate from motor neuron diseases (SMA) based on pathology (cerebellar/sensory vs. motor neuron) and genetic mechanism.

<a id='question-117'></a>

### Question 117: Friedreich Ataxia
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the clinical presentation, inheritance pattern, and underlying molecular defect in Friedreich Ataxia, specifically the role of GAA repeat expansion in the *FXN* gene and its impact on frataxin protein production. (考察弗里德里希共济失调症的临床表现、遗传模式以及潜在的分子缺陷，特别是 *FXN* 基因中 GAA 重复序列的扩增及其对弗拉他كس蛋白生成的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 12-year-old boy presents to his pediatrician with a 3-year history of progressive gait instability, slurred speech (dysarthria), and frequent falls. His mother reports that he initially tripped often but now requires a wheelchair for ambulation. Neurological examination reveals severe limb ataxia, absent deep tendon reflexes (DTRs) in the lower extremities, and extensor plantar responses (Babinski sign). He also has pes cavus (high-arched feet) and kyphoscoliosis. Cardiac evaluation reveals hypertrophic cardiomyopathy and arrhythmias. His older brother has a similar presentation, diagnosed at age 10. Family history is otherwise unremarkable. Genetic testing reveals an expanded GAA trinucleotide repeat in the *FXN* gene. A Southern blot analysis of genomic DNA shows a markedly increased band size compared to controls, corresponding to the expanded repeat region. Western blot analysis of fibroblast proteins shows significantly reduced levels of frataxin protein.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the reduced frataxin protein levels in this patient?

(A) The expanded GAA repeat directly interferes with the translation of the *FXN* mRNA.
(B) The expanded GAA repeat causes aberrant mRNA splicing, leading to a non-functional transcript.
(C) The expanded GAA repeat leads to hypermethylation and transcriptional silencing of the *FXN* gene.
(D) The expanded GAA repeat causes increased degradation of the frataxin protein by proteasomal pathways.
(E) The expanded GAA repeat leads to misfolding of the frataxin protein, targeting it for endoplasmic reticulum-associated degradation (ERAD).

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 12-year-old boy with progressive gait instability, dysarthria, falls (suggesting cerebellar/spinal cord dysfunction).
- **Neurological Exam:** Severe limb ataxia, absent DTRs (sensory neuropathy/dorsal root ganglion involvement), Babinski sign (upper motor neuron involvement), pes cavus, kyphoscoliosis (skeletal abnormalities). These are classic signs of Friedreich Ataxia (FA).
- **Cardiac Findings:** Hypertrophic cardiomyopathy and arrhythmias are common extra-neurological manifestations of FA.
- **Family History:** Older brother with similar symptoms suggests an inherited disorder.
- **Genetic Testing:** Expanded GAA trinucleotide repeat in the *FXN* gene confirms the diagnosis of FA. FA is caused by expansions of this repeat.
- **Southern Blot:** Increased band size confirms the presence of an expanded repeat sequence in the genomic DNA.
- **Western Blot:** Significantly reduced levels of frataxin protein indicate a loss-of-function mechanism.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the reduced frataxin protein levels.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Friedreich Ataxia is an autosomal recessive disorder caused by an expansion of a GAA trinucleotide repeat in the first intron of the *FXN* gene, which encodes the mitochondrial protein frataxin.
- Normal individuals have 5-35 GAA repeats. Affected individuals typically have 66-1000+ repeats.
- The expanded GAA repeat leads to hypermethylation of the CpG islands within the first exon of the *FXN* gene.
- This hypermethylation causes chromatin condensation and transcriptional silencing of the *FXN* gene.
- Consequently, the transcription of *FXN* mRNA is dramatically reduced.
- Since frataxin protein is encoded by this gene, its levels are significantly decreased.
- Frataxin is involved in mitochondrial iron homeostasis and iron-sulfur cluster biogenesis, and its deficiency leads to mitochondrial dysfunction, oxidative stress, and the characteristic clinical features of FA.
- The reduction in protein levels is primarily due to decreased transcription, not issues with translation, splicing, protein degradation, or misfolding (although some misfolding might occur secondarily). The primary defect is transcriptional repression mediated by hypermethylation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) The expanded GAA repeat primarily affects transcription, not translation directly. While very large repeats *could* potentially cause translational read-through or ribosome stalling, the main mechanism is transcriptional silencing. This option misidentifies the primary molecular defect.
- (B) Aberrant mRNA splicing can occur in some genetic disorders, but it is not the primary mechanism in FA. The main issue is the reduction in the *amount* of mRNA produced due to transcriptional silencing, rather than the production of an abnormal mRNA transcript through splicing errors.
- (D) Increased degradation of the frataxin protein by proteasomal pathways is not the primary mechanism. The problem is the lack of sufficient protein synthesis due to reduced transcription. While unstable proteins can be degraded, the fundamental issue here is insufficient production.
- (E) Misfolding of the frataxin protein and subsequent ERAD is not the primary mechanism. While the reduced amount of frataxin might lead to functional consequences related to mitochondrial processes, the core defect is the *quantity* of protein, not its folding stability leading to degradation. The primary defect is transcriptional repression.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Friedreich Ataxia is caused by a GAA repeat expansion in the *FXN* gene, leading to hypermethylation and transcriptional silencing, resulting in reduced frataxin protein levels. This is a classic example of a trinucleotide repeat disorder affecting gene expression through epigenetic mechanisms.
- **Differentials:** Other trinucleotide repeat disorders include Huntington's disease (CAG repeat, CAG repeat gain-of-function), Myotonic Dystrophy (CTG repeat, RNA toxicity), and Fragile X syndrome (CGG repeat, methylation-mediated silencing). Understanding the specific gene, repeat type, and molecular consequence (e.g., silencing vs. gain-of-function vs. RNA toxicity) is crucial.

---

<a id='question-118'></a>

### Question 118: Spinocerebellar Ataxias (SCAs)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Autosomal dominant inheritance, trinucleotide repeat expansion (CAG), anticipation, polyglutamine tracts, neurodegeneration, specific SCA types (e.g., SCA1, SCA3/Machado-Joseph disease), molecular mechanisms of toxicity (e.g., transcriptional dysregulation, protein aggregation).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male presents to a neurologist with a 5-year history of progressive gait ataxia, dysarthria, and dysphagia. His father developed similar symptoms in his late 40s, and his paternal grandfather also exhibited signs of ataxia, although the onset appeared earlier, in his late 30s. Neurological examination reveals cerebellar signs including intention tremor, dysmetria, and nystagmus. Cognitive function is currently intact. Family history is significant for a similar neurological disorder affecting multiple generations on the paternal side. Genetic testing reveals an expanded CAG repeat in the *ATXN1* gene. The patient's father had 38 CAG repeats, and the paternal grandfather had 45 CAG repeats. The patient himself has 52 CAG repeats.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the observed anticipation and neurodegenerative phenotype in this patient and his family?

(A) Gain-of-function mutation leading to constitutive activation of a kinase pathway involved in neuronal survival.
(B) Loss-of-function mutation resulting in decreased expression of a crucial protein for mitochondrial energy production in neurons.
(C) Gain-of-toxic-function due to the expanded polyglutamine tract causing protein misfolding, aggregation, and transcriptional dysregulation.
(D) Haploinsufficiency caused by a frameshift mutation leading to nonsense-mediated mRNA decay and reduced levels of the *ATXN1* protein.
(E) Dominant-negative effect resulting from a missense mutation altering the protein's interaction with its binding partner, disrupting axonal transport.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 52-year-old male with progressive gait ataxia, dysarthria, dysphagia (classic cerebellar signs).
- **Family History:** Autosomal dominant inheritance pattern (father and paternal grandfather affected), anticipation (earlier onset in successive generations: grandfather ~30s, father ~40s, patient ~52, but the repeat number increases).
- **Genetic Testing:** Expanded CAG repeat in the *ATXN1* gene. The number of repeats increases across generations (grandfather: 45, father: 38, patient: 52). Note: The father's repeat number (38) is slightly lower than the patient's (52), but the grandfather's (45) is higher than the father's (38). This is a common observation in SCA1, where transmission through the father often involves instability and can lead to a decrease in repeat number, while transmission through the mother is more stable. However, the overall trend across generations shows increasing severity/earlier onset, consistent with anticipation. The *ATXN1* gene encodes the protein ataxin-1.
- **Key Clues:** Autosomal dominant inheritance, progressive neurodegeneration (ataxia), anticipation, CAG repeat expansion in *ATXN1*. These strongly point towards a polyglutamine expansion disorder, specifically SCA1.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **SCA1 and Polyglutamine Expansion:** Spinocerebellar ataxia type 1 (SCA1) is caused by an expansion of a CAG trinucleotide repeat in the *ATXN1* gene. This gene encodes the protein ataxin-1.
- **Normal Function:** Normal ataxin-1 contains a polyglutamine (polyQ) tract of ~21 glutamines. It is involved in transcriptional regulation, chromatin remodeling, and possibly RNA processing.
- **Mutation:** Expansion of the CAG repeat leads to an elongated polyQ tract in the ataxin-1 protein (e.g., >36 repeats).
- **Mechanism of Toxicity (Gain-of-Toxic-Function):** The expanded polyQ tract causes the mutant ataxin-1 protein to misfold and aggregate, forming intranuclear inclusions. These aggregates sequester normal ataxin-1 and other proteins, disrupting their normal functions. Furthermore, the mutant protein interferes with transcription by binding to transcription factors and altering chromatin structure, leading to dysregulation of gene expression. This gain-of-toxic-function mechanism is characteristic of polyglutamine diseases.
- **Anticipation:** The phenomenon of anticipation, where symptoms appear earlier and/or are more severe in successive generations, is a hallmark of trinucleotide repeat disorders. It is caused by further expansion of the repeat during gametogenesis (especially during oogenesis and paternal meiosis in Huntington's disease and SCA1/3). The instability of the repeat leads to larger expansions in offspring compared to parents.
- **Inheritance:** SCA1 is inherited in an autosomal dominant manner.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Gain-of-function mutation leading to constitutive activation of a kinase pathway. This mechanism is seen in some cancers (e.g., activating mutations in *RAS*) or certain developmental disorders, but not typically in polyglutamine expansion diseases like SCA1. SCA1 involves protein aggregation and transcriptional dysregulation, not kinase pathway activation.
- (B): Loss-of-function mutation resulting in decreased expression of a crucial protein for mitochondrial energy production. This describes mitochondrial disorders (e.g., Leber hereditary optic neuropathy - LHON, caused by mutations in mitochondrial DNA affecting oxidative phosphorylation). SCA1 is caused by an expanded protein, not a loss of function.
- (D): Haploinsufficiency caused by a frameshift mutation leading to nonsense-mediated mRNA decay. Haploinsufficiency means that having only one functional copy of a gene is insufficient. Frameshift mutations often lead to premature stop codons and mRNA degradation. While some genetic diseases are caused by haploinsufficiency (e.g., certain forms of Charcot-Marie-Tooth disease), SCA1 is caused by the *gain* of a toxic function due to the expanded polyQ tract, not a loss of function due to haploinsufficiency. The CAG repeat expansion itself is the primary pathogenic mechanism, not a frameshift.
- (E): Dominant-negative effect resulting from a missense mutation altering the protein's interaction with its binding partner. A dominant-negative effect occurs when the mutant protein interferes with the function of the normal protein produced by the other allele. While protein-protein interactions are involved in SCA1 pathogenesis (mutant ataxin-1 sequesters normal proteins), the primary mechanism is the gain of toxic function due to the expanded polyQ tract leading to aggregation and transcriptional dysregulation, rather than a simple disruption of a specific binding partner interaction caused by a missense mutation. The root cause is the repeat expansion itself.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Trinucleotide repeat expansions (especially CAG repeats) are a major cause of inherited neurodegenerative diseases, often exhibiting autosomal dominant inheritance and anticipation. The expanded polyglutamine tracts lead to protein misfolding, aggregation, and altered transcriptional regulation (gain-of-toxic-function). Key differentials include Huntington's disease (HTT gene, CAG expansion), myotonic dystrophy type 1 (DMPK gene, CTG expansion), and fragile X syndrome (FMR1 gene, CGG expansion). SCA1 is specifically linked to the *ATXN1* gene and CAG expansion.

---

<a id='question-119'></a>

### Question 119: Spinal and Bulbar Muscular Atrophy (Kennedy Disease)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of Spinal and Bulbar Muscular Atrophy (SBMA), also known as Kennedy disease, an X-linked recessive disorder caused by a CAG trinucleotide repeat expansion in the androgen receptor (AR) gene, leading to lower motor neuron (LMN) degeneration, muscle weakness, and endocrine abnormalities. The question requires integrating clinical presentation, genetic inheritance patterns, and molecular mechanisms.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 48-year-old male presents to his primary care physician complaining of progressive muscle weakness and cramping, primarily affecting his hands, forearms, and legs, over the past 5 years. He also reports difficulty swallowing (dysphagia) and slurred speech (dysarthria) that have worsened recently. His past medical history is significant for type 2 diabetes mellitus, which he manages with metformin. Family history reveals that his maternal grandfather and two maternal uncles had similar symptoms, but his father and brothers are unaffected. Physical examination shows fasciculations in the tongue and limbs, muscle atrophy (particularly in the distal muscles), and decreased deep tendon reflexes. Laboratory tests show elevated creatine kinase (CK) levels (15x upper limit of normal), normal serum glucose, and normal thyroid function tests. Genetic testing reveals an expanded CAG repeat length of 62 within the androgen receptor (AR) gene on the X chromosome.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most directly responsible for the pathogenesis of this patient's condition?

(A) Formation of toxic protein aggregates due to misfolded huntingtin protein, leading to neuronal dysfunction and degeneration, particularly in the striatum.
(B) Gain-of-function mutation in the superoxide dismutase 1 (SOD1) gene, resulting in the production of a misfolded protein that disrupts axonal transport and causes motor neuron death.
(C) Gain-of-function mutation in the androgen receptor (AR) gene, leading to the production of a polyglutamine-expanded protein that forms intranuclear inclusions and disrupts neuronal function, particularly in lower motor neurons.
(D) Loss-of-function mutation in the survival motor neuron 1 (SMN1) gene, resulting in insufficient levels of the SMN protein required for motor neuron survival and axonal transport.
(E) Gain-of-function mutation in the ataxin-3 (ATXN3) gene, leading to the production of a polyglutamine-expanded protein that disrupts transcriptional regulation and causes neuronal dysfunction, primarily affecting the cerebellum and cortex.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 48-year-old male with progressive muscle weakness (hands, forearms, legs), dysphagia, dysarthria, fasciculations, muscle atrophy (distal), decreased reflexes, elevated CK. This constellation points towards a lower motor neuron (LMN) disorder.
*   **Family History:** Maternal grandfather and uncles affected, father and brothers unaffected. This pattern strongly suggests an X-linked recessive inheritance.
*   **Genetic Testing:** Expanded CAG repeat (62) in the androgen receptor (AR) gene on the X chromosome. This confirms the diagnosis of Spinal and Bulbar Muscular Atrophy (SBMA), also known as Kennedy disease.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism responsible for the pathogenesis.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Kennedy Disease (SBMA):** Caused by a dynamic mutation involving a CAG trinucleotide repeat expansion in the first exon of the androgen receptor (AR) gene located on the X chromosome (Xq11-12).
*   **Normal AR Gene:** Contains a CAG repeat sequence encoding a polyglutamine tract. Normal alleles typically have 9-35 CAG repeats.
*   **Pathogenic Mutation:** Expansion of the CAG repeat to >38 repeats leads to the production of an abnormally long polyglutamine tract in the N-terminal region of the androgen receptor protein.
*   **Molecular Mechanism:** The expanded polyglutamine tract causes the AR protein to misfold and aggregate, forming intranuclear inclusions. This gain-of-function mutation disrupts normal AR function (androgen signaling) and leads to toxicity, particularly affecting lower motor neurons in the spinal cord and brainstem (bulbar region). The exact mechanism of toxicity is complex but involves disruption of protein degradation pathways (ubiquitin-proteasome system), impaired axonal transport, mitochondrial dysfunction, and oxidative stress. The disease is X-linked recessive, explaining the inheritance pattern observed in the family. The symptoms (LMN weakness, dysphagia, dysarthria, fasciculations, atrophy) are characteristic of LMN degeneration. Elevated CK is common due to muscle damage.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Formation of toxic protein aggregates due to misfolded huntingtin protein, leading to neuronal dysfunction and degeneration, particularly in the striatum.** This describes Huntington's disease. Huntington's disease is an autosomal dominant disorder caused by a CAG repeat expansion in the *HTT* gene, leading to a polyglutamine expansion in the huntingtin protein. It primarily affects the striatum (basal ganglia), causing chorea, cognitive decline, and psychiatric symptoms. The inheritance pattern (autosomal dominant) and primary affected brain region (striatum) are inconsistent with the vignette.
*   **(B) Gain-of-function mutation in the superoxide dismutase 1 (SOD1) gene, resulting in the production of a misfolded protein that disrupts axonal transport and causes motor neuron death.** This describes a common cause of familial Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis (ALS). Mutations in *SOD1* are autosomal dominant and lead to the production of a misfolded enzyme that is toxic to motor neurons (both upper and lower). While ALS involves motor neuron degeneration, the inheritance pattern (autosomal dominant vs. X-linked recessive), the specific gene (*SOD1* vs. *AR*), and the typical clinical presentation (often involving UMN signs in ALS) differ from the vignette.
*   **(D) Loss-of-function mutation in the survival motor neuron 1 (SMN1) gene, resulting in insufficient levels of the SMN protein required for motor neuron survival and axonal transport.** This describes Spinal Muscular Atrophy (SMA). SMA is an autosomal recessive disorder caused by homozygous deletion or mutation of the *SMN1* gene. It leads to a loss of function of the SMN protein, crucial for motor neuron survival. SMA typically presents in infancy or childhood with proximal muscle weakness and hypotonia, although later-onset forms exist. The inheritance pattern (autosomal recessive vs. X-linked recessive), the specific gene (*SMN1* vs. *AR*), and the typical clinical presentation differ significantly.
*   **(E) Gain-of-function mutation in the ataxin-3 (ATXN3) gene, leading to the production of a polyglutamine-expanded protein that disrupts transcriptional regulation and causes neuronal dysfunction, primarily affecting the cerebellum and cortex.** This describes Spinocerebellar Ataxia type 3 (SCA3), also known as Machado-Joseph disease. SCA3 is an autosomal dominant disorder caused by a CAG repeat expansion in the *ATXN3* gene, leading to a polyglutamine expansion in the ataxin-3 protein. It primarily affects the cerebellum and cortex, causing ataxia, ophthalmoplegia, pyramidal signs, and peripheral neuropathy. The inheritance pattern (autosomal dominant vs. X-linked recessive), the specific gene (*ATXN3* vs. *AR*), and the primary clinical features (ataxia) are inconsistent with the vignette.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Kennedy disease (SBMA) is a key example of a polyglutamine expansion disorder affecting motor neurons, specifically LMNs, with X-linked recessive inheritance. It is crucial to differentiate it from other polyglutamine diseases like Huntington's disease (autosomal dominant, striatum) and SCA3 (autosomal dominant, cerebellum/cortex), and other motor neuron diseases like ALS (SOD1 mutations, UMN/LMN) and SMA (autosomal recessive, SMN1 loss-of-function). The specific gene involved (*AR*), the inheritance pattern (X-linked recessive), and the clinical features (LMN signs, endocrine abnormalities like gynecomastia and diabetes, often later onset) are diagnostic.
