<a id='question-120'></a>

### Question 120: Huntington Disease - Anticipation and Molecular Mechanism
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of Huntington disease (HD), an autosomal dominant neurodegenerative disorder caused by a CAG trinucleotide repeat expansion in the *HTT* gene, specifically focusing on the phenomenon of anticipation (earlier onset and increased severity in successive generations) and the molecular basis of this phenomenon. (亨廷顿病，一种由HTT基因中CAG三核苷酸重复序列扩张引起的常染色体显性神经退行性疾病，重点考察“预期”现象（后代发病年龄提前、病情加重）及其分子机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to a neurology clinic with progressive motor dysfunction, including chorea, rigidity, and gait instability, which began approximately 5 years ago. He also reports cognitive decline, memory loss, and personality changes. His father was diagnosed with Huntington disease at age 45 and died at age 60 from complications of the disease. His paternal grandmother (the patient's father's mother) was noted to have had "uncontrollable movements" and cognitive impairment starting in her late 50s. The patient's paternal grandfather is deceased, but family history suggests he was healthy until his late 70s. The patient's sister, age 58, is asymptomatic. Genetic testing confirms the diagnosis in the patient. Analysis of the *HTT* gene reveals 45 CAG repeats. His father's DNA sample (obtained post-mortem) showed 38 CAG repeats. His paternal grandmother's DNA sample (obtained from a buccal swab) shows 42 CAG repeats. His sister's genetic testing reveals 36 CAG repeats.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the observed pattern of disease onset across these three generations?

(A) Genomic imprinting, where the expression of the mutated *HTT* allele is dependent on its parental origin, leading to variable penetrance.
(B) Uniparental disomy, where the patient inherited two copies of chromosome 4 from his mother, resulting in homozygosity for a normal *HTT* allele and masking the effect of the paternal mutation.
(C) Trinucleotide repeat expansion, where the number of CAG repeats increases during gametogenesis, leading to earlier onset and increased severity in successive generations (anticipation).
(D) Somatic mosaicism, where the patient developed the CAG repeat expansion only in his neurons after birth, explaining the late onset compared to his father.
(E) Epigenetic silencing, where methylation patterns on the chromosome 4 carrying the mutated *HTT* allele are altered, leading to variable expressivity.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with chorea, rigidity, gait instability, cognitive decline, and personality changes, consistent with Huntington disease symptoms. Onset at age 57.
*   **Family History:** Father diagnosed at 45, died at 60. Paternal grandmother diagnosed in late 50s. Paternal grandfather healthy until late 70s. Sister asymptomatic at 58.
*   **Genetic Testing:** Patient has 45 CAG repeats. Father had 38 CAG repeats. Paternal grandmother had 42 CAG repeats. Sister has 36 CAG repeats.
*   **Key Observation:** The number of CAG repeats increased from the paternal grandfather (implied normal or low number, <36) to the paternal grandmother (42), to the father (38), and then to the patient (45). The age of onset decreased across generations: Paternal grandfather (late 70s), Paternal grandmother (late 50s), Father (45), Patient (57 - although later than father, the trend is clear across 3 generations, and the repeat number is highest in the patient). This pattern is known as anticipation.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the pattern of disease onset across generations.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   Huntington disease is caused by an expansion of a CAG trinucleotide repeat within the first exon of the *HTT* gene on chromosome 4.
*   The normal *HTT* allele typically has fewer than 27 CAG repeats.
*   Individuals with 27-35 repeats are generally unaffected but may have reduced fertility.
*   Individuals with 36-39 repeats have reduced penetrance; some may develop HD, often later in life.
*   Individuals with 40 or more repeats almost invariably develop HD, typically with an earlier age of onset correlating with the number of repeats.
*   The phenomenon of anticipation, where the disease appears earlier and/or is more severe in successive generations, is characteristic of trinucleotide repeat disorders.
*   Anticipation occurs because the CAG repeat tends to expand during transmission from parent to offspring, particularly during spermatogenesis (more expansion occurs through the paternal line). The expansion is thought to be due to errors in DNA replication or repair involving the repeat sequence.
*   In this case, the paternal grandfather likely had a low number of repeats (possibly normal or in the reduced penetrance range). The repeat expanded when transmitted to his daughter (the patient's paternal grandmother), resulting in 42 repeats and earlier onset. The repeat expanded again when transmitted to his son (the patient's father), resulting in 38 repeats (a slight contraction, but still pathogenic). Finally, the repeat expanded again when transmitted to the patient, resulting in 45 repeats. The sister inherited the allele from her father (38 repeats) and has 36 repeats, indicating a contraction, which can also occur, but the overall trend across the paternal lineage shows expansion and anticipation.
*   The expansion of the CAG repeat leads to an elongated polyglutamine tract in the huntingtin protein. This abnormal protein misfolds, aggregates, and causes neuronal dysfunction and death, particularly in the striatum and cortex, leading to the characteristic motor, cognitive, and psychiatric symptoms of HD.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Genomic imprinting:** While imprinting affects the expression of some genes based on parental origin (e.g., Prader-Willi/Angelman syndromes), Huntington disease is not typically considered an imprinted disorder. The disease occurs regardless of whether the mutated allele is inherited from the mother or the father, although paternal transmission is associated with greater instability and expansion. Imprinting does not explain the increasing repeat number and anticipation seen here.
- (B) **Uniparental disomy (UPD):** UPD involves inheriting two copies of a chromosome (or part of a chromosome) from one parent and no copies from the other. If the patient had UPD for chromosome 4 from his mother, he would have two normal maternal alleles and no paternal allele. This would not cause HD, as he inherited the mutated paternal allele. UPD does not explain the pedigree or the CAG repeat expansion.
- (D) **Somatic mosaicism:** Somatic mosaicism refers to the presence of different genotypes in different cells within the same individual, arising after fertilization. While somatic instability of the CAG repeat can occur in HD, it doesn't explain the multigenerational pattern of anticipation observed in the family pedigree. The mutation is present in the germline (inherited) and affects all cells derived from the zygote, not just neurons post-birth. The late onset compared to the father is not solely explained by somatic mosaicism, but rather by the repeat number (45 vs 38) and other modifying factors.
- (E) **Epigenetic silencing:** Epigenetic modifications, such as DNA methylation, can influence gene expression. While epigenetic changes might play a role in the variable expressivity of HD (e.g., silencing of the normal allele), they do not explain the fundamental mechanism of repeat expansion and anticipation, which is a direct consequence of DNA replication errors involving the CAG repeat sequence. The primary driver of anticipation is the increase in repeat number.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Huntington disease is a classic example of a trinucleotide repeat disorder exhibiting anticipation due to CAG repeat expansion during gametogenesis. This mechanism explains the earlier onset and increased severity in successive generations. Key differentials for neurodegenerative disorders include Alzheimer's disease (amyloid plaques, tau tangles), Parkinson's disease (Lewy bodies, alpha-synuclein), and other trinucleotide repeat disorders like spinocerebellar ataxias (SCA) and myotonic dystrophy. Understanding the molecular basis of anticipation is crucial for genetic counseling in families affected by these disorders.

<a id='question-121'></a>

### Question 121: Genomic Imprinting: Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Genomic imprinting is an epigenetic phenomenon where certain genes are expressed in a parent-of-origin-specific manner. Deletion or inactivation of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11.2-q13 region leads to Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS), characterized by neonatal hypotonia, feeding difficulties, developmental delay, hyperphagia, obesity, hypogonadism, and characteristic facial features. The critical genes in this region, *SNRPN* and *MEST*, are normally expressed only from the paternal allele due to imprinting.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy is brought to the pediatrician by his parents due to concerns about his development and feeding habits. He was born full-term but exhibited significant hypotonia and poor suckling reflex in the neonatal period, requiring feeding assistance via nasogastric tube for the first 6 months. His parents report that he has a constant desire to eat ("hyperphagia") and is rapidly gaining weight, despite being offered reasonable portions. He has mild developmental delay, particularly in speech, and exhibits behavioral issues such as temper tantrums and obsessive-compulsive tendencies. Physical examination reveals short stature, small hands and feet, hypogonadism (small penis, undescended testes), and characteristic facial features including almond-shaped eyes and a narrow forehead. Karyotyping reveals a normal 46,XY chromosome complement. Fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH) analysis using a probe specific for the 15q11.2-q13 region shows that the paternal allele is absent in the patient's cells.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15, resulting in two copies of the maternally imprinted genes and absence of the paternally expressed genes in the critical region.
(B) A point mutation in the *SNRPN* gene on the paternal chromosome 15, leading to a non-functional protein.
(C) A deletion of the maternal chromosome 15, resulting in monosomy 15 and absence of all genes in the 15q11.2-q13 region.
(D) A translocation involving chromosome 15 and another autosome, where the paternal chromosome 15 is lost, and the maternal chromosome 15 is present but silenced.
(E) A mutation in the *IGF2* gene on the maternal chromosome 15, leading to overexpression of the gene.

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with neonatal hypotonia, feeding difficulties (poor suckling), hyperphagia, obesity, developmental delay, behavioral issues, short stature, small hands/feet, hypogonadism, and characteristic facial features. This constellation of symptoms is highly suggestive of Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS).
- **Laboratory Findings:** Normal karyotype (46,XY) rules out aneuploidy. FISH analysis shows absence of the paternal allele for the 15q11.2-q13 region. This is the critical region for PWS.
- **Deduction:** PWS is caused by the loss of function of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11.2-q13 region. The FISH result confirms the absence of the paternal contribution in this region. The normal karyotype indicates no large chromosomal deletion affecting the entire chromosome. The question asks for the molecular mechanism.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Genomic Imprinting:** The 15q11.2-q13 region contains several genes, including *SNRPN* and *MEST*, which are normally expressed *only* from the paternal allele due to genomic imprinting. Imprinting involves epigenetic modifications (DNA methylation, histone modifications) established during gametogenesis that lead to parent-of-origin-specific gene silencing. In this region, the maternal allele is heavily methylated and transcriptionally silenced, while the paternal allele is unmethylated and actively transcribed.
- **PWS Pathogenesis:** PWS results from the loss of function of these paternally expressed genes. This can occur through several mechanisms:
    1.  **Deletion:** Deletion of the paternal 15q11.2-q13 region (most common, ~70% of cases).
    2.  **Maternal Uniparental Disomy (UPD):** The individual inherits two copies of chromosome 15 from the mother and none from the father. Since the maternal alleles in the critical region are imprinted (silenced), there is no functional paternal contribution. (~25% of cases).
    3.  **Imprinting Defect:** A defect in the imprinting center (IC) on the paternal chromosome 15 leads to aberrant methylation and silencing of the paternal alleles in the critical region, even though the paternal chromosome is present. (~5% of cases).
- **FISH Result Interpretation:** The FISH result specifically states the *paternal allele is absent*. This directly points to either a deletion of the paternal chromosome 15 in this region or maternal UPD for chromosome 15.
- **Evaluating the Options:**
    - (A) Maternal UPD for chromosome 15: This mechanism results in the absence of the paternal contribution in the 15q11.2-q13 region, consistent with the FISH finding and the clinical presentation of PWS.
    - (B) Point mutation in *SNRPN*: While *SNRPN* is a critical gene in PWS, a point mutation would likely affect the protein function but wouldn't explain the absence of the paternal allele detected by FISH. Also, point mutations are less common causes of PWS compared to deletions or UPD.
    - (C) Deletion of the maternal chromosome 15: This would lead to Angelman syndrome (AS), characterized by severe developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, and a happy demeanor, due to the loss of the maternally expressed *UBE3A* gene in the same region. The clinical presentation is completely different from PWS.
    - (D) Translocation involving chromosome 15: While translocations can cause PWS if they disrupt the paternal chromosome 15 or lead to UPD, the description "paternal chromosome 15 is lost, and the maternal chromosome 15 is present but silenced" is confusing and doesn't fit the typical mechanisms or the FISH finding of *absence* of the paternal allele. If the maternal allele is silenced, it would lead to a PWS-like phenotype, but the FISH finding would show the presence of the maternal allele, not the absence of the paternal one.
    - (E) Mutation in *IGF2*: *IGF2* is located in the 15q11.2-q13 region and is normally expressed *only* from the maternal allele. Overexpression of *IGF2* (due to a mutation on the maternal allele) is associated with Silver-Russell syndrome (SRS), characterized by intrauterine growth restriction, postnatal growth failure, and characteristic facial features. This is the opposite phenotype of PWS.

- **Conclusion:** The clinical presentation is classic for PWS. The FISH finding indicates the absence of the paternal contribution in the critical 15q11.2-q13 region. Maternal UPD (option A) is a known cause of PWS that fits this molecular finding.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Correct.** Maternal UPD for chromosome 15 results in the individual having two maternal copies of chromosome 15 and no paternal copy. Since the maternal alleles in the 15q11.2-q13 region are imprinted (silenced), the paternally expressed genes (*SNRPN*, *MEST*) are effectively absent, leading to PWS. This aligns perfectly with the clinical picture and the FISH result showing absence of the paternal allele.
- (B): **Incorrect.** A point mutation in *SNRPN* would affect the function of the SNRPN protein but would not explain the absence of the paternal allele detected by FISH. FISH detects the presence or absence of entire chromosomal regions or specific sequences, not point mutations within genes. While *SNRPN* mutations can rarely cause PWS, they are not the most common mechanism, and this option doesn't fit the FISH data.
- (C): **Incorrect.** Deletion of the *maternal* chromosome 15 leads to Angelman syndrome (AS), not PWS. AS is caused by the loss of the maternally expressed *UBE3A* gene in the 15q11.2-q13 region. The clinical features of AS (severe developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, happy demeanor) are distinct from PWS.
- (D): **Incorrect.** This option describes a complex scenario that doesn't fit the typical causes of PWS or the provided FISH result. If the paternal chromosome 15 is lost, it could cause PWS. However, the statement that the "maternal chromosome 15 is present but silenced" is contradictory and doesn't explain the absence of the paternal allele detected by FISH. Furthermore, if the maternal allele were silenced, it would lead to a PWS phenotype, but the FISH would show the presence of the maternal allele, not the absence of the paternal one.
- (E): **Incorrect.** *IGF2* is normally expressed from the maternal allele in the 15q11.2-q13 region. Overexpression of *IGF2* (due to a mutation on the maternal allele) is associated with Silver-Russell syndrome (SRS), characterized by growth restriction, not the hyperphagia, obesity, and hypotonia seen in PWS.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS) is caused by the loss of function of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11.2-q13 region due to genomic imprinting. The most common causes are paternal deletion (70%) and maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) (25%). Angelman syndrome (AS) is caused by the loss of function of the maternally expressed *UBE3A* gene in the same region and presents with distinct clinical features.
- **Differentials:** Key differential diagnosis includes Angelman syndrome (AS). PWS features include neonatal hypotonia, hyperphagia, obesity, hypogonadism, short stature, developmental delay. AS features include severe developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, happy demeanor. The specific genetic mechanism (paternal deletion vs. maternal UPD vs. imprinting defect for PWS; maternal deletion vs. *UBE3A* mutation/UPD for AS) determines the underlying cause.

<a id='question-122'></a>

### Question 122: Angelman Syndrome vs. Prader-Willi Syndrome - Molecular Mechanism
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of genomic imprinting specifically within the 15q11-q13 region, differentiating between Angelman syndrome (AS) and Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS) based on the parent-of-origin effect on specific genes (UBE3A, SNRPN, MAGEL2) and methylation patterns. (考察15q11-q13区域的基因组印记，基于UBE3A、SNRPN、MAGEL2基因的亲本来源效应和甲基化模式区分Angelman综合征和Prader-Willi综合征。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old girl presents with developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, and frequent episodes of inappropriate laughter. She has minimal speech and exhibits hand-flapping movements. Her parents report she was hypotonic at birth. Her father has a history of obesity, hyperphagia, and mild intellectual disability, diagnosed in childhood. Genetic testing reveals a deletion of the 15q11-q13 region on the girl's paternal chromosome. Methylation analysis of the 15q11-q13 region in the girl shows hypermethylation of the paternal allele and hypomethylation of the maternal allele. Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the girl's clinical presentation?

(A) Loss-of-function mutation in the maternal *UBE3A* gene, leading to decreased ubiquitination of target proteins in neurons.
(B) Loss-of-function mutation in the paternal *SNRPN* gene, leading to impaired splicing of neuronal transcripts.
(C) Loss-of-function mutation in the maternal *MAGEL2* gene, leading to altered neuronal development.
(D) Loss-of-function mutation in the paternal *UBE3A* gene, leading to decreased ubiquitination of target proteins in neurons.
(E) Gain-of-function mutation in the maternal *SNRPN* gene, leading to increased splicing of neuronal transcripts.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old girl with developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, inappropriate laughter, minimal speech, hand-flapping, and neonatal hypotonia. These are classic features of Angelman syndrome (AS).
- **Father's History:** Obesity, hyperphagia, mild intellectual disability. These are classic features of Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS).
- **Genetic Testing:** Deletion of the 15q11-q13 region on the *paternal* chromosome. This region contains imprinted genes.
- **Methylation Analysis:** Hypermethylation of the *paternal* allele and hypomethylation of the *maternal* allele in the patient (girl). This is the characteristic methylation pattern for AS.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the *girl's* (patient) clinical presentation.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Genomic Imprinting:** The 15q11-q13 region contains several imprinted genes, meaning their expression depends on their parent of origin.
- **Paternal Expression:** The paternal chromosome 15 normally expresses *UBE3A* (ubiquitin protein ligase E3A) and *SNRPN* (small nuclear ribonucleoprotein N) and *MAGEL2* (Maternal Androgen Genital Expression 2).
- **Maternal Expression:** The maternal chromosome 15 normally expresses *UBE3A*, *SNRPN*, and *MAGEL2*.
- **UBE3A Imprinting:** The *UBE3A* gene is uniquely imprinted. The paternal allele is normally expressed in the brain, while the maternal allele is silenced (methylated) in the brain.
- **SNRPN/MAGEL2 Imprinting:** The *SNRPN* and *MAGEL2* genes are imprinted in the opposite manner. The maternal alleles are normally expressed, while the paternal alleles are silenced (methylated).
- **Angelman Syndrome (AS):** Caused by loss of function of the *maternally* inherited *UBE3A* gene in the brain. This can occur via deletion of the maternal 15q11-q13 region, paternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 15, imprinting defect (loss of methylation on the maternal allele), or mutation in the maternal *UBE3A* gene. The clinical features described in the vignette (developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, laughter, hypotonia) are characteristic of AS. The methylation pattern (paternal hypermethylation, maternal hypomethylation) confirms AS. The deletion is on the *paternal* chromosome, which would normally be silent for *UBE3A* in the brain. However, the *maternal* allele is also affected by the deletion (though the question focuses on the mechanism explaining the girl's phenotype, the deletion is on the paternal chromosome). The key is that the *maternal* *UBE3A* allele, which should be the active one in the brain, is non-functional (due to deletion, mutation, or imprinting defect).
- **Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS):** Caused by loss of function of the *paternally* inherited genes in the 15q11-q13 region, primarily *SNRPN*. This can occur via deletion of the paternal 15q11-q13 region, maternal UPD of chromosome 15, or imprinting defect (loss of methylation on the paternal allele). The father's symptoms are consistent with PWS.
- **Mechanism in the Girl:** The girl has AS. The deletion is on the paternal chromosome. The methylation pattern confirms AS (paternal hypermethylation, maternal hypomethylation). This means the maternal *UBE3A* allele is silenced (methylated) in the brain, and the paternal *UBE3A* allele is also non-functional (due to the deletion). The paternal *SNRPN* and *MAGEL2* are normally silenced. The maternal *SNRPN* and *MAGEL2* are normally expressed. The critical factor for AS is the loss of function of the *maternal* *UBE3A* gene in the brain. Since the paternal chromosome 15 is deleted, the paternal *UBE3A* is non-functional. The methylation pattern shows the maternal *UBE3A* is also silenced (hypomethylated in the brain, but the question implies a loss of function). Therefore, the loss of function of the *paternal* *UBE3A* gene (due to the deletion on the paternal chromosome) is the direct cause of the girl's AS phenotype, as the maternal allele is also silenced. Option (D) correctly identifies the loss-of-function mutation in the paternal *UBE3A* gene as the mechanism.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Loss-of-function mutation in the *maternal* *UBE3A* gene. This is the underlying cause of AS, but the question specifies the deletion is on the *paternal* chromosome. While the maternal allele is also affected (silenced), the deletion itself is paternal. The key mechanism explaining the phenotype given the paternal deletion is the loss of the paternal *UBE3A* contribution. Also, the methylation pattern described (paternal hypermethylation, maternal hypomethylation) is characteristic of AS, where the maternal *UBE3A* is normally silenced in the brain. The paternal deletion removes the normally expressed paternal *UBE3A* in the brain. This option describes the general cause of AS but doesn't fit the specific genetic finding (paternal deletion).
- (B): Loss-of-function mutation in the *paternal* *SNRPN* gene. This is the primary cause of Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS), not Angelman syndrome (AS). The father's symptoms are consistent with PWS, but the question asks about the girl's presentation. *SNRPN* is paternally expressed and maternally silenced. Loss of paternal *SNRPN* function causes PWS.
- (C): Loss-of-function mutation in the *maternal* *MAGEL2* gene. Loss of function of the *maternal* *MAGEL2* gene can cause a phenotype overlapping with AS, but it is less common than *UBE3A* defects. The primary gene responsible for the classic AS phenotype is *UBE3A*. Also, the methylation pattern points strongly towards a *UBE3A*-related mechanism.
- (E): Gain-of-function mutation in the *maternal* *SNRPN* gene. This would not cause AS or PWS. PWS is caused by loss of function of paternally expressed genes (*SNRPN*, *MAGEL2*, *NDN*, *SNORD116*), and AS is caused by loss of function of the *maternally* expressed *UBE3A*. Gain-of-function mutations are not the typical mechanism for these syndromes.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- The 15q11-q13 region demonstrates genomic imprinting, where gene expression is parent-of-origin specific. Loss of function of the *maternally* inherited *UBE3A* gene causes Angelman syndrome (AS), while loss of function of the *paternally* inherited *SNRPN* gene causes Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS). The methylation pattern is crucial for diagnosis: AS typically shows paternal hypermethylation and maternal hypomethylation, while PWS typically shows paternal hypomethylation and maternal hypermethylation.
- Differentials: Rett syndrome (MECP2 mutation, primarily affects females, regression after normal development), Fragile X syndrome (FMR1 mutation, CGG repeat expansion, intellectual disability, characteristic features), other neurodevelopmental disorders. Understanding the specific genetic and molecular basis of each imprinted disorder is key.

<a id='question-123'></a>

### Question 123: Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS) Mechanisms: 70% Paternal 15q11-q13 Deletion, 25% Maternal Uniparental Disomy (UPD), 5% Imprinting Center Defect - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the genetic mechanisms underlying Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS), specifically distinguishing between paternal deletion, maternal uniparental disomy (UPD), and imprinting defects, and relating these mechanisms to the clinical presentation. (理解Prader-Willi综合征 (PWS) 的遗传机制，特别是区分父源缺失、母源单亲二倍体 (UPD) 和印迹缺陷，并将这些机制与临床表现相关联。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy is brought to the pediatrician by his parents due to concerns about developmental delay, hypotonia, and excessive eating. The child was born full-term after an uncomplicated pregnancy. His birth weight was low (2.5 kg), and he exhibited significant feeding difficulties and hypotonia in infancy, requiring specialized feeding support. Over the past year, his parents have noted a marked increase in appetite, leading to obesity despite dietary restrictions. He also displays characteristic behavioral features, including temper tantrums and obsessive-compulsive tendencies. Physical examination reveals short stature, small hands and feet, hypogonadism (small penis, undescended testes), and characteristic facial features (narrow forehead, almond-shaped eyes, thin upper lip). His developmental milestones are significantly delayed; he is nonverbal and has limited motor skills. Karyotyping reveals a normal male chromosome complement (46,XY). Fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH) analysis using probes specific for the 15q11-q13 region shows that the paternal allele is absent in all cells tested.

(A) The patient's phenotype is most likely due to a maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15.
(B) The patient's phenotype is most likely due to a deletion of the maternal allele of the *SNRPN* gene located in the 15q11-q13 region.
(C) The patient's phenotype is most likely due to a deletion of the paternal allele of the *SNRPN* gene located in the 15q11-q13 region.
(D) The patient's phenotype is most likely due to a defect in the imprinting center located in the 15q11-q13 region, leading to biallelic expression of imprinted genes.
(E) The patient's phenotype is most likely due to a translocation involving chromosome 15, disrupting the *SNRPN* gene.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with developmental delay, hypotonia, hyperphagia (excessive eating) leading to obesity, characteristic behavioral issues (tantrums, OCD), short stature, small hands/feet, hypogonadism, and specific facial features. This constellation of symptoms is highly suggestive of Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS).
- **Infancy:** Low birth weight, feeding difficulties, hypotonia. These are classic early signs of PWS.
- **Karyotype:** Normal 46,XY rules out aneuploidy (e.g., Down syndrome) or large chromosomal abnormalities detectable by standard karyotyping.
- **FISH Analysis:** Shows absence of the *paternal* allele in the 15q11-q13 region. This is the critical piece of information.
- **PWS Genetics:** PWS is caused by the loss of function of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11-q13 region. This loss can occur via:
    - Paternal deletion (approx. 70% of cases)
    - Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15 (approx. 25% of cases)
    - Imprinting center defect (approx. 5% of cases)
- **Imprinting:** The 15q11-q13 region contains several imprinted genes. Imprinting means that only one allele (either maternal or paternal) is expressed, while the other is silenced. For PWS, genes like *SNRPN* and *NDN* are normally expressed *only* from the paternal chromosome 15. The maternal allele is silenced by methylation.
- **Mechanism Deduction:** The FISH result explicitly states the *paternal* allele is absent. This directly points to a paternal deletion as the cause.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS):** Caused by the lack of expression of paternally inherited genes in the 15q11.2-q13 region.
- **Key Genes:** Several genes in this region are implicated, including *SNRPN* (Small Nuclear Ribonucleoprotein N), *NDN* (Neuropeptide N), *SNORD116* (Small Nucleolar RNA D116), and others. *SNRPN* is considered a major candidate gene.
- **Imprinting Mechanism:** The expression of these genes is controlled by genomic imprinting. The paternal chromosome 15 carries an unmethylated imprinting control region (ICR), allowing expression of the paternally inherited *SNRPN* and other genes. The maternal chromosome 15 carries a methylated ICR, which silences the maternal *SNRPN* and other genes.
- **Genetic Causes:**
    1.  **Paternal Deletion (70%):** Loss of the paternal chromosome 15 segment containing the critical region (15q11.2-q13). This removes the active paternal alleles of the imprinted genes.
    2.  **Maternal Uniparental Disomy (UPD) (25%):** The individual inherits two copies of chromosome 15 from the mother and none from the father. Since the maternal alleles in the critical region are normally silenced (methylated), there is no functional paternal allele, leading to PWS.
    3.  **Imprinting Defect (5%):** A defect within the imprinting control region (ICR) on the paternal chromosome 15. This defect can lead to methylation of the paternal ICR, silencing the paternal alleles of the imprinted genes, even though the paternal chromosome is present.
- **FISH Result Interpretation:** The FISH analysis specifically detected the *absence* of the *paternal* allele in the 15q11-q13 region. This finding directly corresponds to the mechanism of a paternal deletion.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Maternal UPD (15q11-q13) would result in the *absence* of the *paternal* allele and the *presence* of two *maternal* alleles. While this causes PWS, the FISH result described (absence of paternal allele) is consistent with paternal deletion, not maternal UPD. Maternal UPD would show two maternal signals and no paternal signal on FISH. The question stem specifies the paternal allele is absent, which is the direct result of a paternal deletion.
- (B): A deletion of the *maternal* allele would lead to Angelman Syndrome (AS), not PWS. AS is caused by the loss of function of the *maternally* expressed *UBE3A* gene in the same 15q11-q13 region. The clinical features of AS (severe developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, happy demeanor) are distinct from PWS.
- (D): An imprinting defect would lead to silencing of the paternal alleles, effectively mimicking a loss of the paternal contribution. However, the FISH result specifically indicates the *absence* of the paternal allele itself, which is characteristic of a deletion, not just a silencing defect where the paternal chromosome is still present but the genes are not expressed. While an imprinting defect causes PWS, it doesn't explain the absence of the paternal allele detected by FISH.
- (E): A translocation involving chromosome 15 could potentially disrupt the *SNRPN* gene or alter the imprinting status. However, a standard karyotype was normal (46,XY), ruling out a large translocation detectable by karyotyping. Furthermore, FISH analysis specifically showed the absence of the paternal allele, which is most directly explained by a deletion within the 15q11-q13 region, rather than a translocation disrupting the gene itself (which might show a rearranged signal pattern, not just absence of one allele).

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS) is caused by the lack of expression of paternally inherited genes in the 15q11-q13 region. The three main genetic mechanisms are paternal deletion, maternal UPD, and imprinting defect. Specific genetic testing (like FISH or methylation analysis) is needed to differentiate these causes.
- **Differentials:** Angelman Syndrome (AS) is caused by loss of the *maternally* expressed *UBE3A* gene in the same region (15q11-q13) and presents with distinct features. Understanding the concept of genomic imprinting is crucial for distinguishing PWS and AS.

<a id='question-124'></a>

### Question 124: Prader-Willi Syndrome - Clinical Phenotype & Molecular Mechanism
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS) is caused by the loss of function of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11.2-q13 region, typically due to deletion, maternal uniparental disomy (UPD), or imprinting defects. The clinical phenotype includes neonatal hypotonia and poor feeding, followed by hyperphagia, obesity, hypogonadism, and developmental delay. The underlying mechanism involves the silencing of paternal alleles in this region due to genomic imprinting. (Prader-Willi 综合征 (PWS) 是由 15q11.2-q13 区域父本基因功能丧失引起的，通常是由于缺失、母本单亲二体 (UPD) 或印记缺陷造成的。临床表型包括新生儿低张力肌和喂养不良，随后出现贪吃、肥胖、性腺发育不全和发育迟缓。潜在机制涉及该区域父本等位基因由于基因组印记而被沉默。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old infant boy presents to the pediatrician with concerns about feeding difficulties and developmental delay. Born at term after an uncomplicated pregnancy, he exhibited significant hypotonia and a weak suck reflex from birth, leading to poor weight gain in the neonatal period. His parents report that he requires assistance with feeding and frequently tires during meals. Over the past few weeks, however, his parents have noticed a marked increase in his appetite, with the infant constantly seeking food and appearing insatiably hungry. They are also concerned about his lack of motor milestones, such as rolling over or sitting unsupported. Physical examination reveals hypotonia, short stature for age, and small hands and feet. A karyotype analysis shows a normal male (46,XY) chromosome complement. Further investigation reveals that the infant's mother has a history of recurrent miscarriages. Genetic testing confirms that the infant inherited both copies of chromosome 15 from his mother (maternal UPD 15).

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the clinical presentation of this infant?

(A) Deletion of the *SNRPN* gene on the maternal chromosome 15.
(B) Loss of expression of paternally inherited genes within the 15q11.2-q13 region due to genomic imprinting.
(C) Overexpression of the *UBE3A* gene on the maternal chromosome 15.
(D) A translocation involving chromosome 15 that disrupts the *MAGP* gene.
(E) A point mutation in the *GNAS* gene leading to constitutive activation of Gsα.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Infant presentation:** Neonatal hypotonia, poor feeding, poor weight gain initially. This is characteristic of the first phase of Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS).
- **Later presentation:** Insatiable hyperphagia (constant seeking of food) developing around 6 months. This is the hallmark second phase of PWS.
- **Other findings:** Developmental delay (lack of motor milestones), short stature, small hands/feet. These are also consistent features of PWS.
- **Karyotype:** Normal 46,XY, ruling out a large chromosomal deletion detectable by standard karyotyping, although smaller deletions might be missed.
- **Family history:** Mother with recurrent miscarriages, suggesting potential issues with chromosome 15 segregation.
- **Genetic testing:** Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) 15. This means the infant inherited both copies of chromosome 15 from his mother and none from his father.

These clues strongly point towards Prader-Willi Syndrome. The key is understanding the molecular basis of PWS, which involves the 15q11.2-q13 region and genomic imprinting.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS) Genetics:** PWS is caused by the lack of expression of paternally inherited genes located in the 15q11.2-q13 region. This region contains several genes, including *SNRPN*, *NDN*, *SNORD116*, and *SNORD117*, which are normally expressed only from the paternal allele due to genomic imprinting.
- **Genomic Imprinting:** Imprinting is an epigenetic phenomenon where certain genes are expressed in a parent-of-origin-specific manner. In the 15q11.2-q13 region, the paternal chromosome is normally active, while the maternal chromosome is silenced by methylation.
- **Molecular Mechanisms of PWS:**
    1.  **Paternal Deletion:** ~70% of cases involve a deletion of the paternal 15q11.2-q13 region.
    2.  **Maternal Uniparental Disomy (UPD):** ~25% of cases involve inheriting two copies of chromosome 15 from the mother and none from the father (as in this case). Since the maternal chromosome 15 in this region is normally silenced (imprinted), the infant lacks functional paternal alleles.
    3.  **Imprinting Defect:** ~5% of cases involve a defect in the imprinting center on the paternal chromosome 15, leading to silencing of the paternal alleles.
- **Clinical Phenotype Correlation:** The loss of function of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11.2-q13 region leads to the characteristic PWS phenotype: neonatal hypotonia/poor feeding (due to lack of paternal contribution to muscle development/function and feeding regulation), followed by hyperphagia/obesity (loss of paternal genes involved in satiety signaling), hypogonadism (loss of paternal genes involved in gonadotropin regulation), developmental delay, and characteristic physical features.
- **Patient's Case:** The infant has clinical features of PWS (hypotonia, poor feeding initially, hyperphagia, developmental delay) and genetic confirmation of maternal UPD 15. This means he lacks the normally active paternal contribution from the 15q11.2-q13 region, explaining his condition. Therefore, the underlying mechanism is the loss of expression of paternally inherited genes due to the maternal origin of both chromosome 15 copies and the associated maternal imprinting pattern.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Deletion of the *SNRPN* gene on the maternal chromosome 15. *SNRPN* is located in the critical region for PWS and is normally expressed only from the paternal allele. A deletion on the *maternal* chromosome would *not* cause PWS because the paternal allele would still be active. Deletion of the paternal *SNRPN* gene *would* cause PWS, but this option specifies the maternal chromosome. This is incorrect.
- (C): Overexpression of the *UBE3A* gene on the maternal chromosome 15. *UBE3A* is located in the 15q11.2-q13 region but is imprinted in the *opposite* direction compared to the other PWS genes. *UBE3A* is normally expressed only from the *maternal* allele in the brain. Loss of function of the maternal *UBE3A* causes Angelman syndrome (AS), characterized by severe developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, and inappropriate laughter. Overexpression of the maternal *UBE3A* would not cause PWS; loss of function of the paternal *UBE3A* (which is normally silenced) is not relevant to PWS. This is incorrect.
- (D): A translocation involving chromosome 15 that disrupts the *MAGP* gene. *MAGP* (also known as *MKBP*) is located in the 15q11.2-q13 region and is involved in the imprinting process itself. Disruptions or deletions of *MAGP* can cause PWS by affecting the imprinting center and silencing the paternal alleles. However, the vignette specifically states the infant has maternal UPD 15, which is a distinct mechanism. While *MAGP* disruption *can* cause PWS, it doesn't fit the specific genetic finding in this case (UPD 15). This is incorrect.
- (E): A point mutation in the *GNAS* gene leading to constitutive activation of Gsα. The *GNAS* gene is located on chromosome 20 and encodes the alpha subunit of the stimulatory G protein (Gsα). Activating mutations in *GNAS* cause McCune-Albright syndrome, characterized by polyostotic fibrous dysplasia, café-au-lait spots, and precocious puberty. This gene and syndrome are unrelated to PWS. This is incorrect.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Prader-Willi syndrome results from the loss of function of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11.2-q13 region due to genomic imprinting. The most common causes are paternal deletion, maternal UPD 15, or imprinting defects. The clinical phenotype evolves from neonatal hypotonia/poor feeding to childhood hyperphagia/obesity. (Prader-Willi 综合征是由于 15q11.2-q13 区域父本基因功能丧失引起的，这是由于基因组印记造成的。常见原因是父本缺失、母本单亲二体 15 或印记缺陷。临床表型从新生儿低张力肌/喂养不良发展到儿童期的贪吃/肥胖。)
- **Differentials:** Angelman syndrome (AS) is caused by loss of function of the maternal *UBE3A* gene in the same region (15q11.2-q13) and presents with severe developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, and happy demeanor. The key difference is the parent-of-origin effect: PWS involves loss of paternal contribution, while AS involves loss of maternal contribution.

<a id='question-125'></a>

### Question 125: Angelman Syndrome Mechanisms
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the molecular mechanisms underlying Angelman Syndrome (AS), specifically genomic imprinting and its role in the differential expression of the *UBE3A* gene from maternal and paternal alleles, leading to disease when the maternal allele is non-functional or absent. (理解安吉尔曼综合征 (AS) 的分子机制，特别是基因组印记及其在 *UBE3A* 基因母系和父系等位基因差异表达中的作用，导致母系等位基因功能丧失或缺失时发生疾病。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old girl presents to the pediatrician with developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, and frequent episodes of inappropriate laughter. Her parents report she has difficulty walking and speaking, often exhibiting jerky movements and hand-flapping. Her mother denies any history of similar symptoms or developmental issues in herself or other family members. Her father reports that his own mother had a history of intellectual disability and seizures, but no specific genetic diagnosis was made. The child's physical examination reveals microcephaly, a wide mouth, and prominent chin. A chromosomal microarray analysis is performed, which shows no deletions or duplications. Further investigation includes methylation analysis of the *SNRPN* locus on chromosome 15q11-q13. The results indicate that the maternal allele shows abnormal methylation patterns (hypermethylation of the imprinting control region 1, ICR1), while the paternal allele shows normal methylation patterns (hypomethylation of ICR1).

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Paternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 15q11-q13, resulting in two copies of the paternally inherited *UBE3A* gene, which is normally silenced in the brain.
(B) A de novo deletion of the maternal 15q11-q13 region, encompassing the *UBE3A* gene.
(C) A point mutation in the maternal *UBE3A* gene that introduces a premature stop codon, leading to a truncated, non-functional protein.
(D) A deletion of the paternal 15q11-q13 region, encompassing the *SNRPN* gene and the paternally expressed *UBE3A* gene, leading to loss of function of both genes.
(E) A point mutation in the paternal *UBE3A* gene that introduces a premature stop codon, leading to a truncated, non-functional protein.

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old girl with developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, inappropriate laughter, jerky movements, hand-flapping, microcephaly, wide mouth, prominent chin. These are classic clinical features of Angelman Syndrome (AS).
- **Family History:** Father's mother had intellectual disability and seizures, suggesting a possible inherited condition, potentially related to the 15q11-q13 region. Mother has no relevant history.
- **Initial Testing:** Chromosomal microarray is normal, ruling out large deletions or duplications detectable by this method.
- **Key Lab Finding:** Methylation analysis of the *SNRPN* locus (a control gene within the 15q11-q13 imprinted region) shows abnormal methylation on the maternal allele (hypermethylation of ICR1) and normal methylation on the paternal allele (hypomethylation of ICR1). This pattern is characteristic of Angelman Syndrome.
- **Deduction:** AS is caused by a loss of function of the *maternally* inherited *UBE3A* gene in the brain. The methylation pattern indicates that the maternal *UBE3A* gene is silenced (imprinted) due to aberrant methylation, consistent with AS. The question asks for the *mechanism* causing this silencing/loss of function.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Angelman Syndrome Pathophysiology:** AS is a neurodevelopmental disorder caused by the loss of function of the maternally expressed *UBE3A* gene in the brain. The *UBE3A* gene encodes an E3 ubiquitin ligase involved in protein degradation.
- **Genomic Imprinting:** The 15q11-q13 region is subject to genomic imprinting, meaning that gene expression depends on the parent of origin. The imprinting control region 1 (ICR1) is located within this region.
- **Normal Imprinting:** Normally, the maternal allele at ICR1 is methylated (hypermethylated), leading to the silencing of the *UBE3A* gene on the maternal chromosome in the brain. The paternal allele at ICR1 is unmethylated (hypomethylated), allowing the *UBE3A* gene on the paternal chromosome to be expressed in the brain.
- **AS Mechanisms:** AS arises when the maternal *UBE3A* gene is non-functional or silenced. This can occur through several mechanisms:
    1.  **Deletion of the maternal 15q11-q13 region (approx. 70%):** This removes the maternal *UBE3A* gene entirely.
    2.  **Paternal Uniparental Disomy (UPD) of chromosome 15 (approx. 3-7%):** The individual inherits two copies of chromosome 15 from the father and none from the mother. Both paternal copies of *UBE3A* are normally silenced in the brain due to methylation of the paternal ICR1.
    3.  **Imprinting Defect (ID) (approx. 3-5%):** Aberrant methylation occurs at the maternal ICR1, leading to hypermethylation and silencing of the maternal *UBE3A* gene, even though the gene sequence is normal. This is what is described in the vignette (abnormal methylation pattern on the maternal allele).
    4.  **Mutation in the maternal *UBE3A* gene (approx. 10-15%):** A point mutation, deletion, or other mutation renders the maternal *UBE3A* gene non-functional.
- **Vignette Specifics:** The vignette describes a patient with classic AS symptoms. The microarray is normal, ruling out large deletions (mechanism B). The methylation analysis shows abnormal maternal methylation (hypermethylation of ICR1), which silences the maternal *UBE3A* gene. This points towards either an imprinting defect (ID) or a mutation in the maternal *UBE3A* gene (mechanism C). However, the abnormal methylation pattern itself *is* the mechanism causing the loss of maternal *UBE3A* function in the context of an imprinting defect. Option (A) describes Paternal UPD, which *also* leads to loss of maternal *UBE3A* function (because the paternal copies are silenced), but the methylation pattern described (abnormal maternal, normal paternal) is characteristic of an imprinting defect, not UPD (in UPD, both alleles would show paternal methylation patterns, i.e., hypermethylation at ICR1). Therefore, the most direct interpretation of the abnormal methylation pattern described is an imprinting defect leading to silencing of the maternal allele. The question asks for the *mechanism* responsible for the condition, and the abnormal methylation pattern *is* the mechanism. Paternal UPD results in the same *outcome* (loss of maternal UBE3A function), but the methylation pattern would be different (both alleles methylated). The vignette specifically describes the methylation pattern consistent with an imprinting defect.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Paternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 15q11-q13:** This mechanism *does* cause AS because both copies of the *UBE3A* gene are paternally derived, and the paternal allele is normally silenced in the brain. However, in UPD, both the maternal and paternal alleles would show the *same* methylation pattern – hypermethylation at ICR1 (paternal pattern). The vignette explicitly states the maternal allele shows abnormal methylation and the paternal allele shows normal methylation, which rules out UPD.
- (B) **A de novo deletion of the maternal 15q11-q13 region:** This is the most common cause of AS (approx. 70%). However, the vignette states that the chromosomal microarray analysis was normal, which rules out a deletion large enough to be detected by microarray.
- (C) **A point mutation in the maternal *UBE3A* gene:** This mechanism also causes AS by rendering the maternal protein non-functional. While possible, the key finding provided is the abnormal methylation pattern. An imprinting defect (abnormal methylation) is a distinct mechanism from a point mutation within the gene sequence itself. The abnormal methylation pattern directly explains the silencing of the maternal allele. While a mutation could *cause* an imprinting defect in some cases, the vignette describes the *result* of the defect (abnormal methylation) as the key finding. The abnormal methylation pattern itself is the mechanism described.
- (D) **A deletion of the paternal 15q11-q13 region:** This would cause Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS), not AS. PWS results from the loss of function of paternally expressed genes in this region (like *SNRPN*) due to deletion or UPD. The clinical features described are classic for AS.
- (E) **A point mutation in the paternal *UBE3A* gene:** The paternal *UBE3A* gene is normally expressed in the brain. A mutation in the paternal allele would not cause AS because the maternal allele is normally expressed. Mutations in the paternal *UBE3A* gene are associated with other conditions, but not AS.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Angelman Syndrome is caused by the loss of function of the *maternally* expressed *UBE3A* gene in the brain due to genomic imprinting. Key mechanisms include maternal deletion, paternal UPD, imprinting defects (abnormal methylation), and maternal *UBE3A* mutations. Understanding the specific methylation patterns (maternal hypermethylation at ICR1, paternal hypomethylation at ICR1) is crucial for diagnosis. Differentiate AS from Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS), which involves loss of function of *paternally* expressed genes in the same region, often presenting with hypotonia, hyperphagia, and hypogonadism. Remember that PWS is caused by paternal deletion or maternal UPD, while AS is caused by maternal deletion, paternal UPD, imprinting defect, or maternal *UBE3A* mutation.

<a id='question-126'></a>

### Question 126: Angelman Syndrome - Clinical Phenotype & Genetic Mechanism
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the clinical features of Angelman syndrome (AS) and the molecular basis of its inheritance, specifically involving imprinting and deletion/mutation of the *UBE3A* gene on chromosome 15q11-q13. (理解安吉尔曼综合征的临床特征及其遗传学基础，特别是涉及15号染色体q11-q13区域的*UBE3A*基因的印记、缺失/突变。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old girl is brought to the pediatrician by her parents due to developmental delay. She has severe intellectual disability, is unable to speak, and exhibits frequent, inappropriate episodes of laughter and smiling, often described as "happy puppet syndrome." Her gait is jerky and ataxic, resembling that of a marionette. Her parents report that she often waves her hands while walking. A family history reveals that her maternal grandfather had similar symptoms, although milder, diagnosed later in life. Karyotyping reveals a normal female karyotype (46,XX). Fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH) analysis using a probe specific for the 15q11-q13 region shows that the maternal chromosome 15 lacks the signal, while the paternal chromosome 15 shows a normal signal. Methylation analysis of the *UBE3A* gene promoter region reveals hypermethylation on the maternal allele and hypomethylation on the paternal allele.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Paternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 15q11-q13, leading to silencing of the *UBE3A* gene.
(B) A mutation in the *UBE3A* gene on the paternal chromosome, leading to a non-functional protein.
(C) A deletion of the 15q11-q13 region on the maternal chromosome, resulting in the loss of the normally expressed *UBE3A* allele.
(D) A mutation in the *UBE3A* gene on the maternal chromosome, leading to a non-functional protein, coupled with imprinting center defects causing expression from the paternal allele.
(E) A translocation involving chromosome 21, leading to trisomy 21 and the characteristic features of Down syndrome.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old girl with severe intellectual disability, absent speech, inappropriate laughter ("happy puppet"), ataxic gait, hand-waving movements. These are classic clinical features of Angelman syndrome (AS).
- **Family History:** Maternal grandfather with similar, milder symptoms suggests a possible inherited condition, potentially related to the maternal lineage.
- **Karyotyping:** Normal 46,XX rules out major chromosomal aneuploidies like trisomy 21 (Down syndrome) or large structural rearrangements easily detectable by standard karyotyping.
- **FISH Analysis:** Loss of signal for the 15q11-q13 region specifically on the *maternal* chromosome indicates a defect affecting the maternal contribution in this region. The paternal chromosome is normal.
- **Methylation Analysis:** Hypermethylation of the *UBE3A* promoter on the maternal allele and hypomethylation on the paternal allele are characteristic patterns of genomic imprinting in this region. Normally, the *UBE3A* gene is expressed primarily from the maternal allele in the brain due to paternal imprinting (methylation) silencing the paternal copy. The paternal allele is unmethylated and expressed in other tissues.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Angelman Syndrome Genetics:** AS is caused by the loss of function of the maternally inherited *UBE3A* gene located in the 15q11-q13 region. This gene encodes an E3 ubiquitin ligase involved in protein degradation.
- **Genomic Imprinting:** The 15q11-q13 region is subject to genomic imprinting. This means that gene expression depends on the parent of origin. The *UBE3A* gene is normally expressed *only* from the maternal allele in neurons due to methylation-mediated silencing (imprinting) of the paternal allele. The paternal allele is unmethylated and expressed in other tissues.
- **Molecular Mechanisms of AS:** Several genetic mechanisms can lead to AS:
    1.  **Deletion:** Deletion of the 15q11-q13 region on the maternal chromosome (most common, ~70%).
    2.  **Paternal Uniparental Disomy (UPD):** Inheritance of two paternal copies of chromosome 15q11-q13, resulting in the absence of a functional maternal *UBE3A* allele (~5-7%).
    3.  **Imprinting Defect:** Defect in the imprinting center, leading to methylation of the maternal *UBE3A* promoter and silencing of the maternal allele, while the paternal allele remains silenced (~3-7%).
    4.  ***UBE3A* Mutation:** Mutation within the *UBE3A* gene on the maternal chromosome that leads to a non-functional protein (~10-15%).
- **Vignette Interpretation:** The FISH analysis shows a defect on the *maternal* chromosome 15q11-q13 region. The methylation analysis confirms the typical imprinting pattern (maternal hypermethylation, paternal hypomethylation). The loss of the maternal signal on FISH indicates a deletion or potentially a very small mutation undetectable by FISH but consistent with loss of the maternal allele. Given the loss of the maternal signal and the normal imprinting pattern, a deletion of the 15q11-q13 region on the maternal chromosome is the most likely cause. This deletion removes the normally expressed maternal copy of *UBE3A*.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Paternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 15q11-q13, leading to silencing of the *UBE3A* gene.** Paternal UPD would mean the patient inherited two copies of chromosome 15 from the father and none from the mother. In this case, both copies of *UBE3A* would be paternally imprinted (methylated and silenced in the brain), leading to AS. However, the FISH analysis showed a *loss* of the maternal signal, not the presence of two paternal signals. Also, UPD is less common than maternal deletion.
- (B) **A mutation in the *UBE3A* gene on the paternal chromosome, leading to a non-functional protein.** A mutation on the paternal chromosome would not cause AS because the *UBE3A* gene is normally silenced (imprinted) in the brain. The maternal allele is the one that needs to be functional in the brain.
- (D) **A mutation in the *UBE3A* gene on the maternal chromosome, leading to a non-functional protein, coupled with imprinting center defects causing expression from the paternal allele.** While a maternal *UBE3A* mutation is a cause of AS, this option also mentions an imprinting center defect causing expression from the paternal allele. The vignette describes a *loss* of the maternal signal on FISH, suggesting a deletion rather than just a mutation. Furthermore, the typical imprinting pattern (maternal hypermethylation, paternal hypomethylation) is present, which is consistent with a deletion or a mutation *plus* normal imprinting, not an imprinting defect causing paternal expression (which would typically involve loss of methylation on the paternal allele).
- (E) **A translocation involving chromosome 21, leading to trisomy 21 and the characteristic features of Down syndrome.** Down syndrome presents with different clinical features (e.g., characteristic facial features, congenital heart defects, hypotonia, intellectual disability but typically not the specific AS phenotype like inappropriate laughter and ataxic gait). Karyotyping was normal (46,XX), ruling out trisomy 21. FISH analysis targeted the 15q11-q13 region, not chromosome 21.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Angelman syndrome results from the loss of function of the maternally inherited *UBE3A* gene in the brain due to genomic imprinting. The most common cause is a maternal deletion of the 15q11-q13 region. Key clinical features include severe intellectual disability, absent speech, ataxia, jerky movements, and frequent laughter. Differentiating AS from Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS), which involves the same chromosomal region but affects the paternally expressed genes, is crucial. PWS typically presents with hypotonia, feeding difficulties in infancy, hyperphagia and obesity later, and hypogonadism. Both involve imprinting defects in the 15q11-q13 region, but AS involves loss of maternal *UBE3A* function, while PWS involves loss of paternal SNRPN function.

<a id='question-127'></a>

### Question 127: Uniparental Disomy (UPD)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the clinical consequences of uniparental disomy (UPD), specifically imprinting and the resulting phenotypic expression when both chromosomes originate from a single parent, leading to homozygous expression of imprinted genes. (理解单亲二体 (UPD) 的临床后果，特别是印记效应以及当两条同源染色体都来自同一亲本时产生的表型表达，导致印记基因的纯合子表达。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the pediatric clinic with developmental delay, hypotonia, seizures, and characteristic facial features including a prominent forehead, almond-shaped eyes, and a thin upper lip. His parents are unaffected and are first cousins. His older sister is phenotypically normal. Karyotyping reveals a normal 46,XY chromosome complement. However, methylation analysis of the SNRPN gene locus on chromosome 15q11-q13 shows that the paternal allele is unmethylated, while the maternal allele is methylated. This pattern is consistent across multiple somatic cells. The patient's mother is heterozygous for a polymorphism (rs1234567) located within the SNRPN gene. DNA analysis of the patient reveals that he is homozygous for this polymorphism (rs1234567).

(A) The patient's phenotype is most likely due to a deletion of the maternal chromosome 15q11-q13 region.
(B) The patient's phenotype is most likely due to a deletion of the paternal chromosome 15q11-q13 region.
(C) The patient's phenotype is most likely due to uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15, inherited from the mother.
(D) The patient's phenotype is most likely due to uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15, inherited from the father.
(E) The patient's phenotype is most likely due to a translocation involving chromosome 15.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with developmental delay, hypotonia, seizures, and specific facial features (prominent forehead, almond-shaped eyes, thin upper lip). This constellation of symptoms is highly suggestive of Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS).
*   **Family History:** Parents are unaffected first cousins, increasing the risk of autosomal recessive disorders or chromosomal abnormalities like UPD. Older sister is normal.
*   **Karyotype:** Normal 46,XY, ruling out large chromosomal deletions or aneuploidies detectable by standard karyotyping.
*   **Methylation Analysis:** SNRPN gene locus (located in the 15q11-q13 region critical for PWS/Angelman syndrome) shows abnormal methylation. Paternally inherited alleles are normally unmethylated, while maternally inherited alleles are normally methylated. In this patient, the paternal allele is unmethylated, and the maternal allele is methylated, which is the normal pattern. However, the question implies this pattern is *abnormal* in the context of the patient's phenotype. The key is that the patient *expresses* the PWS phenotype, which requires the *absence* of expression from the paternally inherited alleles in the 15q11-q13 region. The methylation pattern described (paternal unmethylated, maternal methylated) is the *normal* pattern. The *abnormal* pattern for PWS is paternal methylation and maternal unmethylation. The vignette states the *patient's* pattern is paternal unmethylated, maternal methylated. This is the normal pattern. The *phenotype* (PWS) requires the paternal allele to be silenced (methylated). The vignette implies the patient's *expression* pattern is consistent with PWS, despite the methylation analysis showing the normal pattern. This discrepancy points towards UPD.
*   **Polymorphism Analysis:** The mother is heterozygous for a polymorphism (rs1234567) in SNRPN. The patient is homozygous for this polymorphism. Since the patient inherited one chromosome 15 from his mother and one from his father, he should have inherited the mother's allele (carrying the polymorphism) and the father's allele (not carrying the polymorphism, assuming the father is homozygous for the other allele). The patient being homozygous for the mother's allele means he inherited *both* copies of chromosome 15 from his mother.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS):** Caused by the loss of function of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11-q13 region, primarily due to deletion of the paternal copy, maternal UPD for chromosome 15, or an imprinting defect leading to methylation of the paternal allele.
*   **Imprinting:** An epigenetic phenomenon where certain genes are expressed in a parent-of-origin-specific manner. In the 15q11-q13 region, genes like SNRPN and others are normally expressed *only* from the paternal chromosome (paternally imprinted, maternally silenced) in certain brain regions.
*   **Mechanism in this Case:** The patient exhibits PWS symptoms. The methylation analysis shows the normal pattern (paternal unmethylated, maternal methylated). However, the patient is homozygous for a maternal polymorphism. This indicates that both chromosome 15s originated from the mother (maternal UPD). In maternal UPD, the patient has two copies of the maternal chromosome 15. The maternal chromosome 15 carries the methylated SNRPN allele, which is normally silenced. However, the *entire* chromosome 15 is duplicated, meaning the patient has two copies of the *maternally silenced* genes in the critical region. This leads to the loss of function of the paternally expressed genes required for normal development, resulting in the PWS phenotype. The normal methylation pattern reported in the vignette is consistent with UPD, as the *expression* of the paternally required genes is lost despite the methylation pattern appearing normal on a per-allele basis. The key is the *source* of the chromosomes.
*   **UPD:** Uniparental disomy occurs when an individual inherits two copies of a chromosome (or part of a chromosome) from one parent and no copies from the other parent. In this case, the patient has two copies of chromosome 15 from his mother and none from his father.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Deletion of the maternal chromosome 15q11-q13 region:** This would cause Angelman syndrome (AS), characterized by severe developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, happy demeanor, and inappropriate laughter. The patient's symptoms (hypotonia, PWS-like facial features) are more consistent with PWS. Furthermore, a deletion would be detectable on karyotyping, which was normal. Methylation analysis in AS would show paternal methylation and maternal unmethylation.
- (B): **Deletion of the paternal chromosome 15q11-q13 region:** This is the most common cause of PWS (~70% of cases). It would lead to the loss of the paternally expressed genes required for normal development. However, a deletion would be detectable on karyotyping, which was normal. Methylation analysis would show paternal methylation and maternal unmethylation.
- (C): **Uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15, inherited from the mother:** This means the patient inherited both copies of chromosome 15 from the mother and none from the father. As explained above, this leads to the loss of function of the paternally expressed genes in the critical region (due to maternal imprinting/methylation), causing PWS. The patient being homozygous for a maternal polymorphism confirms this. This matches the clinical presentation and genetic findings.
- (E): **Translocation involving chromosome 15:** A translocation could disrupt the 15q11-q13 region or alter the expression of genes in that region. However, standard karyotyping was normal, making a detectable translocation unlikely. While some translocations might be too small to detect by standard karyotype, UPD is a more direct explanation given the specific methylation pattern (even if described confusingly) and the homozygosity for the maternal polymorphism.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Uniparental disomy (UPD) is a mechanism where both homologous chromosomes are inherited from a single parent. If UPD occurs for chromosome 15, it can lead to either Prader-Willi syndrome (maternal UPD) or Angelman syndrome (paternal UPD) due to genomic imprinting in the 15q11-q13 region. Key differentiating features between PWS and AS include hypotonia/obesity vs. ataxia/happy demeanor, respectively. Distinguish UPD from deletions and imprinting defects, which are other causes of PWS/AS. Remember that UPD can be detected by analyzing polymorphisms or microsatellites across the affected chromosome region, as seen here with the maternal polymorphism.
