<a id='question-128'></a>

### Question 128: Heterodisomy vs Isodisomy: Nondisjunction in Meiosis I (Heterodisomy, Non-Identical Homologs) vs Meiosis II (Isodisomy, Identical Copies) - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating between heterodisomy (resulting from nondisjunction in meiosis I, leading to chromosomes from different homologs) and isodisomy (resulting from nondisjunction in meiosis II or post-zygotic mitosis, leading to identical chromosome copies) based on genetic marker analysis. (区分异源二体畸 (Meiosis I 不分离, 不同同源染色体) 和同源二体畸 (Meiosis II 不分离或减数分裂后有丝分裂不分离, 相同染色体副本) 的分子遗传学基础)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old girl presents with developmental delay, hypotonia, and characteristic facial features including hypertelorism, epicanthal folds, and a flat nasal bridge. Her parents are phenotypically normal. Karyotyping reveals a 46,XX karyotype with an extra copy of chromosome 21, consistent with mosaic Down syndrome. Further molecular genetic analysis using single nucleotide polymorphism (SNP) arrays across chromosome 21 is performed on peripheral blood lymphocytes. The SNP array shows that one copy of chromosome 21 is derived from the maternal homolog, while the other copy of chromosome 21 is also derived from the maternal homolog. The patient's mother is a carrier of a balanced Robertsonian translocation between chromosomes 14 and 21, t(14;21)(q10;q10).

Which of the following mechanisms is the MOST likely explanation for the patient's mosaicism and the specific genetic findings on the SNP array?

(A) Nondisjunction of the translocated chromosome 21 during maternal meiosis I, followed by fertilization with a normal haploid sperm.
(B) Nondisjunction of the normal chromosome 21 during maternal meiosis II, followed by fertilization with a normal haploid sperm.
(C) Nondisjunction of the translocated chromosome 21 during maternal meiosis II, followed by fertilization with a normal haploid sperm.
(D) Nondisjunction of the translocated chromosome 21 during maternal meiosis I, followed by fertilization with a haploid sperm carrying an extra copy of chromosome 21.
(E) Nondisjunction of the normal chromosome 21 during maternal meiosis I, followed by fertilization with a normal haploid sperm.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old girl with developmental delay, hypotonia, and dysmorphic features suggestive of Down syndrome.
*   **Karyotype:** 46,XX with an extra chromosome 21 (mosaic Down syndrome). This indicates trisomy 21 in some cells, but not all.
*   **Maternal History:** Carrier of a balanced Robertsonian translocation t(14;21)(q10;q10). This means the mother has one normal chromosome 21 and one fused chromosome 14/21. She is phenotypically normal because she has two copies of the long arms of chromosomes 14 and 21 (which contain most of the genes).
*   **SNP Array Results:** Both copies of chromosome 21 in the patient's cells are derived from the maternal homolog. This is the crucial piece of information. It means the extra chromosome 21 came from the mother, and it is identical to the normal chromosome 21 she carries (or rather, the long arm of chromosome 21 from the fused chromosome).
*   **Question:** What mechanism explains the mosaic trisomy 21 and the SNP array finding that both copies of chromosome 21 are maternal in origin?

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Robertsonian Translocation:** The mother carries t(14;21). During meiosis, the chromosomes 14 and 21 pair with their normal homologs. The translocated chromosome 21 pairs with the normal chromosome 21, and the translocated chromosome 14 pairs with the normal chromosome 14.
*   **Meiosis I vs. Meiosis II Nondisjunction:**
    *   **Meiosis I:** Homologous chromosomes separate. If nondisjunction occurs here involving the translocated chromosome 21 and the normal chromosome 21, the resulting gametes will have either both chromosome 21s (one normal, one translocated) or no chromosome 21s.
    *   **Meiosis II:** Sister chromatids separate. If nondisjunction occurs here involving the translocated chromosome 21, the resulting gametes will have either both copies of the translocated chromosome 21 or no copies.
*   **Analyzing the Options:**
    *   **(A) Nondisjunction of the translocated chromosome 21 during maternal meiosis I:** This would lead to gametes with either both the normal chromosome 21 and the translocated chromosome 21, or neither. Fertilization with a normal sperm (containing one normal chromosome 21) would result in a zygote with three copies of chromosome 21: one normal, one translocated, and one normal (from the sperm). The SNP array would show two different maternal copies (one from the normal homolog, one from the translocated homolog) and one paternal copy. This contradicts the finding that *both* copies are maternal.
    *   **(B) Nondisjunction of the normal chromosome 21 during maternal meiosis II:** This is impossible. The mother carries a *translocated* chromosome 21, not a normal one. Nondisjunction of the normal chromosome 21 homolog would not lead to trisomy 21 in the offspring, as the mother only has one normal chromosome 21 homolog.
    *   **(C) Nondisjunction of the translocated chromosome 21 during maternal meiosis II:** During meiosis II, the sister chromatids of the translocated chromosome 21 separate. If nondisjunction occurs, one gamete will have two copies of the translocated chromosome 21, and the other will have none. Fertilization with a normal sperm (containing one normal chromosome 21) would result in a zygote with three copies of chromosome 21: two identical copies of the translocated chromosome 21 (from the egg) and one normal chromosome 21 (from the sperm). This results in *isodisomy* for chromosome 21 (specifically, maternal isodisomy for the long arm, as the short arm comes from chromosome 14). The SNP array would show that both copies of chromosome 21 are derived from the maternal homolog (the one involved in the translocation). This matches the clinical findings.
    *   **(D) Nondisjunction of the translocated chromosome 21 during maternal meiosis I, followed by fertilization with a haploid sperm carrying an extra copy of chromosome 21:** This scenario is biologically impossible. Sperm do not carry extra copies of chromosomes; they are haploid. Even if a sperm somehow carried an extra chromosome 21, the result of meiosis I nondisjunction involving the translocated chromosome 21 would still lead to gametes with either both chromosome 21s (normal + translocated) or none, not the specific pattern observed.
    *   **(E) Nondisjunction of the normal chromosome 21 during maternal meiosis I:** Similar to option (B), this is impossible as the mother carries a translocated chromosome 21, not a normal one.

*   **Conclusion:** The SNP array data (both copies of chromosome 21 are maternal) strongly indicates that the extra chromosome 21 originated from a single gamete that contained two copies of the maternal chromosome 21 homolog. This occurs due to nondisjunction of the translocated chromosome 21 during meiosis II. This results in maternal isodisomy for chromosome 21.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): This option describes nondisjunction in meiosis I. Meiosis I involves separation of homologous chromosomes. If the translocated chromosome 21 and the normal chromosome 21 fail to separate, the resulting gamete will have both. Fertilization with a normal sperm would lead to a zygote with two maternal copies (one normal, one translocated) and one paternal copy, resulting in *heterodisomy* (two different maternal homologs and one paternal homolog). The SNP array would show two different maternal patterns and one paternal pattern, contradicting the findings. Students might confuse meiosis I and II nondisjunction or heterodisomy and isodisomy.
- (B): This option describes nondisjunction of the *normal* chromosome 21 during meiosis II. The mother carries a *translocated* chromosome 21, not a normal one. Therefore, nondisjunction of the normal homolog is not the mechanism leading to the extra chromosome 21 in the offspring. Students might incorrectly assume the mother has two normal chromosome 21s.
- (D): This option describes nondisjunction in meiosis I followed by fertilization with a sperm carrying an extra chromosome 21. Sperm are haploid and do not carry extra chromosomes. Furthermore, meiosis I nondisjunction involving the translocated chromosome 21 would lead to heterodisomy, not the observed isodisomy. Students might misunderstand gamete formation or the ploidy of sperm.
- (E): This option describes nondisjunction of the *normal* chromosome 21 during meiosis I. Similar to option (B), the mother carries a translocated chromosome 21, making this mechanism impossible for generating the extra chromosome 21 in the offspring. Students might incorrectly assume the mother has two normal chromosome 21s.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Understanding the difference between heterodisomy (Meiosis I nondisjunction) and isodisomy (Meiosis II nondisjunction or post-zygotic mitosis) is crucial. SNP arrays or other genetic marker analyses can distinguish these by revealing whether the extra chromosome copies are derived from different homologs (heterodisomy) or identical homologs (isodisomy). Robertsonian translocations are a common cause of unbalanced chromosome complements in offspring, often leading to mosaicism and isodisomy. Differential diagnoses include other causes of Down syndrome (e.g., free trisomy 21, translocation carriers) and other mosaic syndromes.

<a id='question-129'></a>

### Question 129: Trinucleotide Repeat Expansion: Dynamic Unstable Repeat Expansions Amplifying in Meiosis Causing Disease Exacerbation - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Dynamic mutation (dynamic unstable repeat expansion) in trinucleotide repeat disorders, specifically Fragile X syndrome, leading to anticipation (earlier onset and/or increased severity in successive generations) due to preferential amplification of the expanded allele during meiosis. (动态突变/动态不稳定重复序列扩增，在三核苷酸重复序列疾病中，特别是脆性X综合征，导致预期现象（代际提前发病和/或加重），因为扩增的等位基因在减数分裂过程中优先扩增。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 65-year-old male presents to his physician complaining of progressive cognitive decline, ataxia, and tremors over the past 5 years. His symptoms began in his late 50s. His paternal grandfather also exhibited similar neurological symptoms, which started in his 40s. His father, however, was asymptomatic throughout his life, although he did have a history of infertility. The patient's 30-year-old son is currently asymptomatic but has undergone genetic testing due to the family history. Genetic analysis of the patient reveals an expanded CGG repeat in the 5' untranslated region (UTR) of the *FMR1* gene. The patient has 250 CGG repeats. His son's genetic testing shows 100 CGG repeats. The patient's father (the asymptomatic son of the grandfather) had 50 CGG repeats, which was considered within the normal range at the time of his testing. The patient's paternal grandfather's DNA was not available for testing.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the observed pattern of inheritance and symptom presentation in this family?

(A) Genomic imprinting of the *FMR1* gene, leading to preferential expression of the mutated allele from the father.
(B) Meiotic recombination between the normal and expanded *FMR1* alleles, resulting in mosaicism in the patient.
(C) Dynamic mutation (dynamic unstable repeat expansion) of the CGG repeat in the *FMR1* gene during spermatogenesis, leading to anticipation.
(D) Somatic mutation in the *FMR1* gene occurring post-zygotically in the patient's neurons, causing late-onset symptoms.
(E) X-chromosome inactivation skewing, leading to preferential expression of the mutated allele in the patient's somatic cells.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 65-year-old male with late-onset (late 50s) progressive cognitive decline, ataxia, and tremors. This suggests a neurodegenerative disorder.
*   **Family History:** Paternal grandfather had similar symptoms starting earlier (40s). Father was asymptomatic but infertile. Son (patient's son) is currently asymptomatic but carries an expanded repeat (100 CGG). This pattern points towards anticipation (earlier onset/increased severity in successive generations).
*   **Genetic Findings:** Patient has 250 CGG repeats in the *FMR1* gene (associated with Fragile X syndrome/Fragile X-associated tremor/ataxia syndrome - FXTAS). Father has 50 CGG repeats (normal range, typically <45). Son has 100 CGG repeats (intermediate range, premutation).
*   **Father's Infertility:** Often associated with the premutation range of CGG repeats in *FMR1*.
*   **Key Deduction:** The progression from a normal repeat number in the grandfather (implied, as his son had 50 and the son was asymptomatic) to a premutation in the father (50 repeats, infertile) to a full mutation in the patient (250 repeats, symptomatic) and a premutation in the son (100 repeats) strongly indicates a dynamic mutation process where the repeat number increases during transmission through the germline. This phenomenon is called anticipation.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Fragile X Syndrome/FXTAS:** Caused by mutations in the *FMR1* gene on the X chromosome. The most common mutation is an expansion of a CGG trinucleotide repeat in the 5' UTR.
*   **Normal Allele:** Typically has <45 CGG repeats.
*   **Premutation Allele:** 55-200 CGG repeats. Associated with Fragile X-associated tremor/ataxia syndrome (FXTAS) and Fragile X-associated primary ovarian insufficiency (FXPOI). Can also be transmitted to offspring, often with expansion.
*   **Full Mutation Allele:** >200 CGG repeats. Associated with classic Fragile X syndrome (intellectual disability, characteristic physical features). Methylation of the CpG islands within the repeat region leads to transcriptional silencing of the *FMR1* gene.
*   **Dynamic Mutation:** In trinucleotide repeat disorders, the number of repeats can change during transmission from parent to offspring, particularly through the germline (sperm or egg). This change is often an increase (expansion) but can sometimes be a decrease (contraction).
*   **Mechanism of Expansion:** During DNA replication in the germline, the repeat sequence can form secondary structures (like G-quadruplexes for GGG repeats or hairpin loops for CGG repeats) that impede normal replication. This can lead to slippage of the DNA polymerase, resulting in the insertion or deletion of repeat units, thus expanding or contracting the repeat length.
*   **Anticipation:** The phenomenon where a genetic disorder manifests earlier and/or more severely in successive generations. This is a hallmark of dynamic mutations, particularly in disorders like Huntington's disease, myotonic dystrophy, and Fragile X syndrome. In this case, the grandfather likely had FXTAS (or possibly classic Fragile X if he had >200 repeats, though the father's 50 repeats makes this less likely for the grandfather), the father had a premutation (50 repeats) and infertility, the patient has a full mutation (250 repeats) and FXTAS symptoms, and the son has a premutation (100 repeats). The expansion from ~45 repeats (grandfather, inferred) -> 50 repeats (father) -> 250 repeats (patient) -> 100 repeats (son) demonstrates the dynamic nature and anticipation. The expansion during transmission from father to son is particularly notable, as expansions are often larger when transmitted through males.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Genomic imprinting of the *FMR1* gene, leading to preferential expression of the mutated allele from the father.** Genomic imprinting involves differential expression of genes depending on parental origin. While some trinucleotide repeat disorders (like Beckwith-Wiedemann syndrome involving *IGF2*) are influenced by imprinting, *FMR1* is not typically considered an imprinted gene in the context of Fragile X syndrome or FXTAS. The symptoms arise from the lack of FMRP protein (due to methylation in full mutations) or potentially toxic RNA gain-of-function (in full mutations) or other mechanisms in premutations (like GAA repeat expansion in the *FMR1* gene), not from parent-of-origin specific expression differences that would explain the anticipation pattern.
*   **(B) Meiotic recombination between the normal and expanded *FMR1* alleles, resulting in mosaicism in the patient.** Meiotic recombination can occur, but it doesn't explain the *increase* in repeat number across generations (anticipation). Mosaicism refers to the presence of cells with different genotypes within an individual. While somatic mosaicism can occur, the primary issue here is the germline transmission and expansion of the repeat. Recombination wouldn't inherently cause the repeat to expand; it might lead to different combinations of alleles in offspring but not the progressive increase seen here.
*   **(D) Somatic mutation in the *FMR1* gene occurring post-zygotically in the patient's neurons, causing late-onset symptoms.** Somatic mutations (occurring after fertilization) are generally not inherited. The pattern described (grandfather -> father -> patient -> son) is clearly inherited. Furthermore, somatic mutations typically affect only a subset of cells, not the entire organism in a way that explains the family history. While somatic instability of repeats can occur, it doesn't explain the germline transmission and anticipation.
*   **(E) X-chromosome inactivation skewing, leading to preferential expression of the mutated allele in the patient's somatic cells.** X-chromosome inactivation is a process in females (XX) where one X chromosome is randomly inactivated in each cell. Skewing occurs when one X chromosome is preferentially inactivated over the other. While this can influence the phenotype in female carriers of X-linked disorders like Fragile X, it doesn't explain the inheritance pattern in males (who have only one X chromosome) or the anticipation phenomenon observed across generations in this pedigree. The patient is male, so X-inactivation is not the primary mechanism explaining his symptoms or the inheritance pattern.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Dynamic mutation (unstable repeat expansion) is the key mechanism causing anticipation in trinucleotide repeat disorders like Fragile X syndrome (FXS) and FXTAS. The repeat number increases during germline transmission, leading to earlier onset and/or increased severity in subsequent generations. Key differentials include other trinucleotide repeat disorders (Huntington's, myotonic dystrophy) and other causes of neurodegenerative disease. Remember that anticipation is a hallmark of dynamic mutations.

---

<a id='question-130'></a>

### Question 130: Genetic Anticipation: Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1 (DM1)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Genetic anticipation is a phenomenon where a genetic disorder manifests at an earlier age and/or with increased severity in successive generations. This is most commonly associated with trinucleotide repeat expansion disorders, such as Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1 (DM1), caused by an unstable CTG repeat expansion in the *DMPK* gene. (遗传性预期，即后代发病年龄提前、病情加重，常见于三核苷酸重复序列扩张疾病，如肌强直性肌营养不良1型(DM1)，由*DMPK*基因中CTG重复序列不稳定扩张引起。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to his physician complaining of progressive muscle weakness, myotonia (difficulty relaxing muscles after contraction), cataracts, cardiac conduction abnormalities, and frontal balding. His symptoms began in his late 40s. His son, a 35-year-old male, presents with similar symptoms, but they began in his early 20s and are more severe, including significant cognitive impairment and early-onset insulin resistance. The son's son, the patient's 5-year-old grandson, exhibits developmental delay, facial weakness, and hypotonia, symptoms not seen in either his father or grandfather at this age. Genetic testing reveals an expansion of a CTG trinucleotide repeat in the 3' untranslated region (UTR) of the *DMPK* gene. In the grandfather, the repeat count is 50. In the father, the repeat count is 150. In the grandson, the repeat count is 2000. Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the observed pattern of inheritance and disease progression across these three generations?

(A) Genomic imprinting, where the expression of the *DMPK* gene is silenced depending on the parent of origin, leading to variable expressivity.
(B) Uniparental disomy, where the grandson inherited two copies of chromosome 19 from his mother, resulting in homozygosity for the expanded allele.
(C) Meiotic instability, specifically anticipation, where the CTG repeat expansion increases during gametogenesis (spermatogenesis in this case), leading to earlier onset and increased severity in subsequent generations.
(D) Somatic mosaicism, where the expanded CTG repeat arose de novo in the grandson during early embryonic development, affecting only a subset of his cells.
(E) Epigenetic modification, such as DNA methylation, which increases with age and leads to silencing of the *DMPK* gene, causing the observed symptoms.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** Three generations (grandfather, father, grandson) affected by similar symptoms (muscle weakness, myotonia, cataracts, cardiac issues, cognitive impairment, developmental delay).
- **Age of Onset:** Progressively earlier onset across generations (Grandfather: late 40s; Father: early 20s; Grandson: infancy/early childhood).
- **Severity:** Progressively increasing severity across generations (Grandson has developmental delay and facial weakness at age 5, which were not present in the grandfather or father at the same age; Father has cognitive impairment and insulin resistance, which were not mentioned in the grandfather).
- **Genetic Testing:** CTG trinucleotide repeat expansion in the *DMPK* gene.
- **Repeat Count:** Increasing number of repeats across generations (Grandfather: 50; Father: 150; Grandson: 2000).
- **Key Clue:** The combination of earlier onset, increased severity, and increasing repeat size across generations is the hallmark of genetic anticipation. The specific gene (*DMPK*) and repeat type (CTG) point towards Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1 (DM1), a classic example of a trinucleotide repeat disorder exhibiting anticipation.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **DM1 Pathophysiology:** DM1 is caused by an expansion of a CTG trinucleotide repeat in the 3' UTR of the *DMPK* gene on chromosome 19. Normal individuals have <35 repeats. Premutation carriers have 35-50 repeats. Affected individuals typically have >50 repeats.
- **Mechanism of Anticipation:** The CTG repeat expansion occurs primarily during meiosis, particularly during spermatogenesis. The expanded allele is prone to further expansion when passed from father to son. This expansion is thought to be due to slippage of the DNA polymerase during replication within the repeat region. The larger the repeat size, the greater the probability of further expansion in the next generation.
- **Correlation with Phenotype:** The size of the CTG repeat generally correlates with the age of onset and severity of the disease. Larger expansions lead to earlier onset and more severe phenotypes. The grandson has the largest expansion (2000 repeats) and the earliest onset/most severe symptoms (developmental delay at age 5). The father has an intermediate expansion (150 repeats) and intermediate onset/severity. The grandfather has the smallest expansion (50 repeats) and the latest onset.
- **Molecular Consequences:** The expanded CTG repeat in the *DMPK* mRNA forms a stable double-stranded RNA structure (hairpin) that sequesters RNA-binding proteins, such as hnRNPA1, away from other target mRNAs. This leads to aberrant splicing and altered expression of numerous downstream genes, contributing to the diverse clinical manifestations of DM1.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Genomic imprinting:** While imprinting affects the expression of some genes, DM1 is not primarily an imprinted disorder. The severity and anticipation are related to the repeat size itself, not the parent of origin (although paternal transmission is more prone to expansion). Imprinting involves differential expression based on parental origin, which is not the core mechanism here.
- (B) **Uniparental disomy (UPD):** UPD occurs when an individual inherits both copies of a chromosome (or part of a chromosome) from one parent and no copy from the other parent. While UPD can cause disease, it doesn't explain the progressive increase in repeat size and earlier onset across generations seen in anticipation. UPD would result in homozygosity for the allele inherited from the single parent, but not necessarily an increase in repeat size.
- (D) **Somatic mosaicism:** Somatic mosaicism refers to the presence of cells with different genotypes within a single individual, arising from a mutation during post-zygotic development. While mosaicism can occur in DM1, it doesn't explain the transgenerational pattern of increasing repeat size and earlier onset. The expanded repeat is present in the germline (sperm) and is transmitted to the next generation.
- (E) **Epigenetic modification:** Epigenetic modifications (like DNA methylation or histone modification) can influence gene expression without altering the DNA sequence. While epigenetic changes might play a role in DM1 pathogenesis or disease progression, they do not explain the fundamental mechanism of genetic anticipation, which is the increase in the number of trinucleotide repeats during gametogenesis. The core issue is the unstable repeat expansion itself.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Genetic anticipation is a key feature of trinucleotide repeat expansion disorders. DM1 is the classic example, characterized by CTG repeat expansion in the *DMPK* gene. Other examples include Huntington's disease (CAG repeat in *HTT*), Fragile X syndrome (CGG repeat in *FMR1*), and Friedreich's ataxia (GAA repeat in *FXN*). The mechanism involves meiotic instability leading to increased repeat size in successive generations, resulting in earlier onset and/or increased severity. Differentiating anticipation from other inheritance patterns (e.g., autosomal dominant inheritance without anticipation) is crucial.

<a id='question-131'></a>

### Question 131: Huntington Disease Repeat Mechanics: CAG Expansion (Polyglutamine) in Coding Exon, Paternal Transmission Leading to Larger Expansions - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Huntington disease (HD) is an autosomal dominant neurodegenerative disorder caused by a CAG trinucleotide repeat expansion in the *HTT* gene. The expansion occurs within a coding exon, leading to an elongated polyglutamine tract in the huntingtin protein. The expansion is unstable and tends to increase with successive generations, particularly when transmitted paternally (anticipation).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male presents to a neurology clinic with progressive motor dysfunction, including chorea, rigidity, and bradykinesia, along with cognitive decline and psychiatric disturbances (irritability, depression). His symptoms began approximately 10 years ago. His father was diagnosed with Huntington disease at age 45 and died at age 60 from complications of the disease. The patient's paternal grandfather also had HD, diagnosed at age 40. The patient underwent genetic testing, which revealed an *HTT* gene mutation with 45 CAG repeats. His father's genetic testing, performed post-mortem on archived DNA, showed 38 CAG repeats. The patient's 28-year-old daughter, who is asymptomatic, underwent predictive genetic testing and was found to have 32 CAG repeats. The patient's 30-year-old sister, who also developed HD symptoms at age 48, had 40 CAG repeats. Which of the following mechanisms best explains the observed pattern of CAG repeat expansion in this family?

(A) The CAG repeat expansion in the *HTT* gene is primarily influenced by maternal transmission, leading to larger expansions in daughters compared to sons.
(B) The CAG repeat expansion in the *HTT* gene occurs in a non-coding intron, leading to altered splicing and reduced huntingtin protein levels.
(C) The CAG repeat expansion in the *HTT* gene is unstable during meiosis, with a higher propensity for expansion during spermatogenesis (paternal transmission) compared to oogenesis (maternal transmission).
(D) The CAG repeat expansion in the *HTT* gene is caused by a point mutation in the DNA sequence, leading to a missense mutation in the huntingtin protein.
(E) The CAG repeat expansion in the *HTT* gene is associated with a loss of heterozygosity (LOH) event, leading to haploinsufficiency of the normal *HTT* allele.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 52-year-old male with classic symptoms of Huntington disease (chorea, rigidity, bradykinesia, cognitive decline, psychiatric disturbances) starting at age 42. This establishes the diagnosis of HD.
*   **Family History:** Father diagnosed at 45, paternal grandfather diagnosed at 40. This indicates an autosomal dominant inheritance pattern with anticipation (earlier onset in successive generations).
*   **Genetic Testing Data:**
    *   Patient: 52 years old, 45 CAG repeats.
    *   Father: Diagnosed at 45, died at 60, 38 CAG repeats.
    *   Daughter: 28 years old, asymptomatic, 32 CAG repeats.
    *   Sister: Diagnosed at 48, 40 CAG repeats.
*   **Key Observation:** The number of CAG repeats increased from 38 in the father to 45 in the son (patient), demonstrating anticipation. The daughter inherited the expanded allele (45 repeats) but has fewer repeats (32) than her father (38), suggesting a possible reduction during oogenesis. The sister inherited the father's allele (38 repeats) and gained 2 repeats (40), showing expansion but less than the father-to-son expansion.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism explaining the observed pattern of CAG repeat expansion.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   Huntington disease is caused by an expansion of a CAG trinucleotide repeat within the first exon of the *HTT* gene on chromosome 4. This expanded repeat codes for an abnormally long polyglutamine (polyQ) tract in the N-terminal region of the huntingtin protein.
*   Normal alleles have fewer than 26 CAG repeats. Intermediate alleles (27-35 repeats) are typically asymptomatic but can expand further. Reduced penetrance alleles (36-39 repeats) may show symptoms later in life. Full penetrance alleles are generally >40 repeats.
*   The patient (45 repeats) and his father (38 repeats) both have full penetrance alleles, consistent with their clinical presentation. The daughter (32 repeats) has a reduced penetrance allele and is asymptomatic. The sister (40 repeats) has a full penetrance allele and symptoms.
*   The expansion of the CAG repeat is unstable during meiosis, particularly in the germline. The mechanism involves DNA polymerase slippage during replication.
*   **Anticipation:** The phenomenon where the disease manifests earlier and/or more severely in successive generations is called anticipation. This is primarily due to the tendency for the CAG repeat to increase in size during transmission.
*   **Transmission Bias:** Studies have shown that CAG repeat expansions are more likely to occur and tend to be larger when the affected allele is transmitted through the male germline (paternal transmission) compared to the female germline (maternal transmission). This is thought to be related to the higher number of cell divisions during spermatogenesis compared to oogenesis, providing more opportunities for polymerase slippage. The father-to-son transmission in this family (38 repeats -> 45 repeats) shows a significant expansion, consistent with this bias. The father-to-daughter transmission (38 repeats -> 32 repeats) shows a reduction, which can also occur, although expansion is more common. The sister's expansion (38 repeats -> 40 repeats) is less pronounced than the father-to-son expansion.
*   Therefore, the observed pattern of anticipation, particularly the larger expansion during paternal transmission, is best explained by the instability of the CAG repeat during meiosis, with a higher propensity for expansion in the male germline.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) The CAG repeat expansion in the *HTT* gene is primarily influenced by maternal transmission, leading to larger expansions in daughters compared to sons.** This is incorrect. While expansion can occur during maternal transmission, the evidence strongly suggests that expansion is *more frequent* and *larger* during paternal transmission (spermatogenesis). The father-to-son expansion (38 -> 45) in the vignette directly contradicts this statement.
*   **(B) The CAG repeat expansion in the *HTT* gene occurs in a non-coding intron, leading to altered splicing and reduced huntingtin protein levels.** This is incorrect. The CAG repeat expansion occurs within the *first coding exon* of the *HTT* gene. This leads to an elongated polyglutamine tract within the protein itself, causing a toxic gain-of-function, not reduced protein levels due to splicing issues.
*   **(D) The CAG repeat expansion in the *HTT* gene is caused by a point mutation in the DNA sequence, leading to a missense mutation in the huntingtin protein.** This is incorrect. CAG repeat expansion is a form of *dynamic mutation* involving an increase in the number of trinucleotide repeats, not a single point mutation (substitution, insertion, or deletion of a single nucleotide). While it results in a missense change (elongated polyQ tract), the underlying mechanism is repeat expansion, not a point mutation.
*   **(E) The CAG repeat expansion in the *HTT* gene is associated with a loss of heterozygosity (LOH) event, leading to haploinsufficiency of the normal *HTT* allele.** This is incorrect. Huntington disease is caused by a *dominant* gain-of-function mechanism due to the mutant huntingtin protein. LOH is not the primary mechanism. While LOH can occur in some cancers, it is not the cause of HD. The disease arises from the presence of even one expanded allele, which is sufficient to cause toxicity. Haploinsufficiency implies that having only one functional copy of a gene is insufficient, which is not the case for the normal *HTT* allele.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
*   **High-Yield Takeaway:** Huntington disease is caused by CAG repeat expansion in the *HTT* gene, located in a coding exon. Anticipation (earlier onset/larger expansion in subsequent generations) is common, particularly with paternal transmission due to increased instability during spermatogenesis.
*   **Differentials:** Other trinucleotide repeat disorders include Fragile X syndrome (CGG repeat in *FMR1* gene, non-coding region, X-linked), Myotonic dystrophy (CTG repeat in *DMPK* gene, non-coding region, autosomal dominant), and Spinocerebellar ataxias (various repeat types and locations). Understand the specific gene, repeat type, location (coding vs. non-coding), and inheritance pattern for each. Also differentiate from other neurodegenerative diseases like Alzheimer's, Parkinson's, and ALS, which have different genetic and molecular mechanisms.

<a id='question-132'></a>

### Question 132: Fragile X Syndrome - Repeat Expansion Mechanism
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Fragile X syndrome is caused by expansion of a CGG trinucleotide repeat in the 5' untranslated region (UTR) of the *FMR1* gene. This expansion leads to hypermethylation and silencing of the gene, resulting in a deficiency of the FMRP protein. The expansion is particularly prone to occurring during oogenesis, leading to anticipation (increasing severity in successive generations) through maternal inheritance. (脆性X综合征是由FMR1基因5'非编码区CGG三核苷酸重复序列扩增引起的。这种扩增导致基因甲基化和沉默，从而导致FMRP蛋白缺乏。这种扩增特别容易在卵子形成过程中发生，导致通过母系遗传的预期现象（后代症状加重）。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old woman presents to a genetics clinic concerned about her family history. Her brother has moderate intellectual disability, autism spectrum disorder, and characteristic facial features (long face, prominent jaw, large ears). Her brother's son (the patient's nephew) has severe intellectual disability and seizures. The patient herself has had two children: a son with mild learning difficulties and a daughter who appears developmentally normal. The patient undergoes genetic testing, which reveals a *FMR1* gene analysis showing 150 CGG repeats in her allele. Her brother's genetic testing showed 800 CGG repeats. Her nephew's testing showed >1000 CGG repeats. Her son's testing showed 180 CGG repeats, and her daughter's testing showed 50 CGG repeats. Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the transmission of the expanded CGG repeat allele from the patient's brother to her nephew, and the subsequent transmission from the patient to her son?

(A) The CGG repeat expansion occurs primarily during spermatogenesis due to errors in DNA replication by DNA polymerase, and the number of repeats is generally stable during oogenesis.
(B) The CGG repeat expansion occurs primarily during oogenesis due to the involvement of RNA polymerase I in transcribing the repeat region, leading to secondary structure formation that stalls DNA replication, and the number of repeats is generally stable during spermatogenesis.
(C) The CGG repeat expansion occurs primarily during spermatogenesis due to errors in DNA repair mechanisms involving mismatch repair proteins, and the number of repeats is generally stable during oogenesis.
(D) The CGG repeat expansion occurs primarily during oogenesis due to the involvement of RNA polymerase III in transcribing the repeat region, leading to secondary structure formation that stalls DNA replication, and the number of repeats is generally stable during spermatogenesis.
(E) The CGG repeat expansion occurs primarily during spermatogenesis due to errors in DNA replication by DNA polymerase, and the number of repeats is generally stable during oogenesis, but the repeat number can fluctuate significantly in both gametes.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** A family history suggestive of Fragile X syndrome (intellectual disability, autism, specific facial features) across multiple generations.
- **Inheritance Pattern:** The condition appears to be X-linked, affecting males more severely. The patient's brother is affected, his son is severely affected, and the patient's son has mild symptoms. The patient herself has a borderline repeat number (150), placing her in the intermediate range, potentially explaining her lack of significant symptoms. Her daughter has a normal repeat number (50).
- **Genetic Testing Results:**
    - Patient's brother: 800 CGG repeats (Full mutation range, >200 repeats).
    - Patient: 150 CGG repeats (Intermediate/Gray zone range, 55-200 repeats).
    - Patient's nephew: >1000 CGG repeats (Full mutation range).
    - Patient's son: 180 CGG repeats (Intermediate/Gray zone range).
    - Patient's daughter: 50 CGG repeats (Normal range, <55 repeats).
- **Key Observation:** The repeat number increased significantly from the brother (800) to the nephew (>1000), demonstrating anticipation. The patient (150 repeats) transmitted an expanded allele (180 repeats) to her son and a normal allele (50 repeats) to her daughter.
- **Question Focus:** The question asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the repeat expansion, specifically focusing on the germline (spermatogenesis vs. oogenesis) and the underlying biochemical process.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Fragile X Syndrome Genetics:** Caused by expansion of a CGG trinucleotide repeat in the 5' UTR of the *FMR1* gene on the X chromosome.
- **Normal Allele:** Typically has 5-44 CGG repeats.
- **Intermediate Allele (Premutation):** 55-200 CGG repeats. Carriers may have Fragile X-associated tremor/ataxia syndrome (FXTAS) or Fragile X-associated primary ovarian insufficiency (FXPOI).
- **Full Mutation Allele:** >200 CGG repeats. Associated with Fragile X syndrome.
- **Mechanism of Expansion:** The expansion is unstable and occurs predominantly during meiosis, particularly during oogenesis.
    - **Oogenesis:** During oogenesis, the *FMR1* gene is transcribed by RNA polymerase I (not III). The long RNA transcript containing the CGG repeat forms a stable secondary structure (G-quadruplex). This structure can impede the progression of the replication fork during DNA replication, leading to slippage of the DNA polymerase and the addition of extra CGG repeats. This process is error-prone and results in significant expansion of the repeat sequence.
    - **Spermatogenesis:** The repeat expansion is much less frequent and less pronounced during spermatogenesis. The repeat number tends to be more stable in sperm compared to eggs.
- **Anticipation:** The tendency for the disease to become more severe and/or appear at an earlier age in successive generations is called anticipation. This is primarily due to the preferential expansion of the CGG repeat during oogenesis. A female carrier with a premutation allele can transmit an allele with a significantly larger number of repeats to her offspring, potentially converting it into a full mutation.
- **Clinical Vignette Application:** The brother had a full mutation (800 repeats). His daughter (the patient) inherited this allele, but it expanded further during her oogenesis to 150 repeats (intermediate). When the patient produced her own eggs, the allele expanded again in one egg (transmitted to her son, resulting in 180 repeats) and remained relatively stable in another egg (transmitted to her daughter, resulting in 50 repeats). The brother's son inherited the severely expanded allele (>1000 repeats) from his mother (the patient's sister, who likely had a full mutation allele inherited from her father, which expanded during her oogenesis). The expansion from 800 to >1000 repeats in one generation highlights the dramatic expansion potential during oogenesis.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Incorrect. While repeat expansion can occur during spermatogenesis, it is much less frequent and less dramatic than during oogenesis. The primary site of expansion for the CGG repeat in *FMR1* is the oocyte. Also, the repeat number is not generally stable during oogenesis; it is prone to expansion.
- (C): Incorrect. The expansion is not primarily due to errors in DNA repair mechanisms like mismatch repair during spermatogenesis. The mechanism involves RNA polymerase I transcription and secondary structure formation during oogenesis.
- (D): Incorrect. The *FMR1* gene is transcribed by RNA polymerase I, not RNA polymerase III, during the process leading to repeat expansion. RNA polymerase III transcribes tRNA and 5S rRNA genes.
- (E): Incorrect. While the repeat number can fluctuate, the expansion is not equally likely in both gametes, nor is it equally likely during both spermatogenesis and oogenesis. The expansion is significantly more pronounced and frequent during oogenesis. The statement that the repeat number is generally stable during oogenesis is also incorrect.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Fragile X syndrome involves CGG repeat expansion in the *FMR1* 5' UTR, primarily occurring during oogenesis due to RNA polymerase I transcription and secondary structure formation, leading to anticipation.
- **Differentials:** Other trinucleotide repeat disorders (e.g., Huntington's disease - CAG expansion, Myotonic dystrophy - CTG expansion) also exhibit anticipation, but the specific mechanism (polymerase involved, direction of expansion) differs. Huntington's expansion occurs primarily in spermatogenesis. Myotonic dystrophy expansion occurs in both but is more pronounced in oogenesis. Understanding the specific polymerase and germline involved is key.

<a id='question-133'></a>

### Question 133: Fragile X Syndrome & FMRP
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the molecular mechanism underlying Fragile X syndrome, specifically the role of FMR1 gene promoter hypermethylation leading to the absence of FMRP and its downstream effects on synaptic plasticity and translation regulation. (考察脆性X综合征的分子机制，特别是FMR1基因启动子甲基化导致FMRP缺失，及其对突触可塑性和翻译调控的下游影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy is brought to the pediatrician by his parents due to developmental delay, speech impairment, and autistic-like behaviors (e.g., hand flapping, poor eye contact). His physical examination reveals a long face, prominent jaw, large ears, and macroorchidism. His older brother has a confirmed diagnosis of Fragile X syndrome. The parents undergo genetic counseling and testing. The mother is found to be a carrier for a full mutation of the FMR1 gene. The patient's genetic testing reveals an expanded CGG repeat in the 5' untranslated region (UTR) of the FMR1 gene, with >200 repeats. Analysis of the patient's leukocytes shows significantly reduced levels of FMRP protein compared to unaffected individuals. Further investigation reveals that the FMR1 promoter region in the patient's cells is heavily methylated, while the promoter region in unaffected individuals is unmethylated. Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the absence of FMRP protein in this patient?

(A) The expanded CGG repeat directly inhibits ribosome binding to the FMR1 mRNA, preventing translation of FMRP.
(B) The expanded CGG repeat forms a stable secondary structure (G-quadruplex) in the FMR1 mRNA, blocking ribosomal progression.
(C) The expanded CGG repeat in the 5' UTR leads to promoter hypermethylation, which silences FMR1 gene transcription.
(D) The expanded CGG repeat causes aberrant splicing of the FMR1 pre-mRNA, leading to a non-functional transcript.
(E) The expanded CGG repeat recruits specific microRNAs (miRNAs) that target the FMR1 mRNA for degradation.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with developmental delay, speech impairment, autistic-like behaviors, long face, prominent jaw, large ears, macroorchidism. These are classic clinical features of Fragile X syndrome.
- **Family History:** Older brother with Fragile X syndrome, mother is a carrier (full mutation). This points towards an X-linked inheritance pattern consistent with Fragile X.
- **Genetic Testing:** Expanded CGG repeat (>200 repeats) in the 5' UTR of the FMR1 gene. This confirms the molecular basis of Fragile X syndrome.
- **Protein Level:** Significantly reduced levels of FMRP protein. This indicates a lack of functional protein production.
- **Epigenetic Analysis:** FMR1 promoter region is heavily methylated in the patient, but unmethylated in unaffected individuals. This is the key finding linking the genetic mutation to the lack of protein.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the absence of FMRP protein.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Fragile X syndrome is caused by the expansion of a CGG trinucleotide repeat in the 5' UTR of the FMR1 gene on the X chromosome.
- Normal alleles have 5-44 repeats. Premutation alleles have 55-200 repeats. Full mutation alleles have >200 repeats.
- The expanded CGG repeat (>200) in the 5' UTR leads to epigenetic modification, specifically hypermethylation of the CpG islands within the FMR1 promoter region.
- DNA methylation is an epigenetic mark that typically represses gene transcription.
- Hypermethylation of the FMR1 promoter recruits methyl-CpG-binding domain (MBD) proteins and associated histone deacetylases (HDACs), leading to chromatin condensation and transcriptional silencing of the FMR1 gene.
- Consequently, the FMR1 gene is not transcribed into mRNA, and therefore, the FMRP protein is not produced.
- FMRP is an RNA-binding protein crucial for regulating the translation of numerous mRNAs, particularly those involved in synaptic plasticity and neuronal development. Its absence leads to the characteristic neurological and behavioral symptoms of Fragile X syndrome.
- The clinical vignette explicitly states the presence of the expanded CGG repeat, reduced FMRP levels, and FMR1 promoter hypermethylation. This directly links the genetic mutation to the epigenetic modification (hypermethylation) and the resulting lack of protein production (transcriptional silencing).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): The expanded CGG repeat is located in the 5' UTR, not typically within the coding sequence or immediately adjacent to the start codon where it would directly block ribosome binding. While large repeats *can* sometimes affect translation initiation, the primary mechanism in Fragile X is transcriptional silencing via promoter methylation. This option confuses the location and primary effect.
- (B): While G-quadruplex structures can form in regions with repetitive sequences (like the CGG repeat) and potentially impede transcription or translation, the established primary mechanism for FMRP absence in Fragile X is promoter hypermethylation leading to transcriptional silencing, not G-quadruplex formation blocking ribosomal progression on the mRNA. This is a plausible but incorrect mechanism.
- (D): Aberrant splicing can occur in some genetic disorders, but it is not the primary mechanism responsible for the lack of FMRP in Fragile X syndrome. The main issue is the failure to transcribe the gene due to promoter methylation. While some studies suggest potential splicing abnormalities in certain contexts, it's not the central, high-yield mechanism for the full mutation phenotype.
- (E): MicroRNAs (miRNAs) are involved in post-transcriptional regulation by targeting mRNA for degradation or translational repression. While miRNAs can regulate FMR1 expression, the primary cause of FMRP absence in Fragile X syndrome with a full mutation is transcriptional silencing due to promoter hypermethylation, not miRNA-mediated degradation. This option describes a different regulatory mechanism.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Fragile X syndrome is a classic example of how trinucleotide repeat expansion can lead to epigenetic modifications (promoter hypermethylation) causing transcriptional silencing and disease. Remember that the expanded CGG repeat in the 5' UTR of FMR1 leads to methylation of the promoter, silencing transcription, and absence of FMRP. Key differentials for developmental delay and intellectual disability include Down syndrome (trisomy 21), Rett syndrome (MECP2 mutation), and Angelman syndrome (UBE3A deletion/mutation). However, Fragile X is unique in its specific molecular mechanism involving repeat expansion and promoter methylation.

<a id='question-134'></a>

### Question 134: Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1 (DM1)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1 (DM1) is caused by a CTG trinucleotide repeat expansion in the 3' untranslated region (UTR) of the *DMPK* gene. This expansion leads to RNA toxicity through sequestration of RNA-binding proteins (like hnRNPA1) and aberrant splicing of other pre-mRNAs, resulting in characteristic clinical features including myotonia, muscle weakness, cataracts, cardiac conduction defects, and endocrine dysfunction. (甲型肌强直型肌营养不良症 (DM1) 是由 *DMPK* 基因 3' 非翻译区 (UTR) 中 CTG 三核苷酸重复序列扩增引起的。这种扩增通过捕获 RNA 结合蛋白 (如 hnRNPA1) 和其他前 mRNA 的异常剪接导致 RNA 毒性，从而产生特征性临床表现，包括肌强直、肌肉无力、白内障、心脏传导障碍和内分泌功能障碍。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male presents to his primary care physician complaining of progressive muscle weakness, particularly in his facial and distal limb muscles, and difficulty relaxing his grip after shaking hands (myotonia). He also reports blurred vision and cataracts. His father had similar symptoms, developing them in his 50s. Physical examination reveals ptosis, temporal wasting, and a positive myotonic grip sign. Electrocardiogram (ECG) shows prolonged PR interval and T-wave inversion. Serum creatine kinase (CK) is mildly elevated at 250 U/L (normal < 200 U/L). A genetic test reveals a CTG repeat expansion of 1200 repeats in the 3' UTR of the *DMPK* gene. Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the pathogenesis of the cardiac conduction abnormalities observed in this patient?

(A) Gain-of-function mutation in the cardiac sodium channel (SCN5A) gene, leading to increased sodium influx and prolonged action potential duration.
(B) Sequestration of hnRNPA1 by the expanded CTG repeat RNA, leading to aberrant splicing of pre-mRNAs encoding cardiac ion channels, specifically affecting the potassium channel (KCNQ1).
(C) Reduced expression of the dystrophin protein due to mutations in the *DMD* gene, impairing muscle cell membrane integrity and leading to arrhythmias.
(D) Impaired mitochondrial function due to mutations in mitochondrial DNA, resulting in decreased ATP production and cardiac myopathy.
(E) Increased expression of the myostatin protein due to mutations in the *MSTN* gene, leading to muscle atrophy and cardiac dysfunction.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 45-year-old male with progressive muscle weakness (facial, distal limbs), myotonia (difficulty relaxing grip), blurred vision, cataracts. These are classic symptoms of Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1 (DM1).
- **Family History:** Father had similar symptoms, suggesting an autosomal dominant inheritance pattern, consistent with DM1.
- **Physical Exam:** Ptosis, temporal wasting, positive myotonic grip sign further support DM1 diagnosis.
- **ECG Findings:** Prolonged PR interval and T-wave inversion indicate cardiac conduction abnormalities, a known complication of DM1.
- **Lab Findings:** Mildly elevated CK is common in DM1.
- **Genetic Test:** CTG repeat expansion (1200 repeats) in the 3' UTR of the *DMPK* gene confirms the diagnosis of DM1.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the *cardiac conduction abnormalities* in this patient with confirmed DM1.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- DM1 is caused by a CTG repeat expansion in the 3' UTR of the *DMPK* gene.
- The expanded RNA transcript contains the toxic CTG repeats.
- This expanded RNA sequesters RNA-binding proteins, particularly hnRNPA1 (heterogeneous nuclear ribonucleoprotein A1), which normally binds to specific sequences in pre-mRNAs.
- Sequestration of hnRNPA1 leads to its depletion from its normal targets.
- hnRNPA1 is crucial for proper pre-mRNA splicing. Its depletion causes aberrant splicing of numerous target pre-mRNAs, including those encoding proteins involved in various tissues, including the heart.
- In the heart, aberrant splicing affects genes encoding ion channels critical for cardiac conduction, such as potassium channels (e.g., KCNQ1, KCNH2) and sodium channels (SCN5A).
- Mis-splicing of ion channel genes leads to dysfunctional ion channels, disrupting the normal electrical activity of the heart.
- This disruption manifests as conduction defects like prolonged PR interval (AV block) and arrhythmias, as seen in the patient's ECG.
- Therefore, the cardiac conduction abnormalities in DM1 are primarily due to RNA toxicity mediated by the expanded CTG repeat, leading to hnRNPA1 sequestration and subsequent aberrant splicing of cardiac ion channel genes.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Gain-of-function mutation in *SCN5A* is associated with Long QT syndrome (LQTS) type 3, characterized by prolonged QT interval and increased risk of Torsades de Pointes, not typically the primary conduction defects (prolonged PR) seen in DM1. While SCN5A splicing can be affected in DM1, the primary mechanism described here (gain-of-function) is incorrect for DM1 pathogenesis.
- (C): Reduced dystrophin expression is characteristic of Duchenne Muscular Dystrophy (DMD) or Becker Muscular Dystrophy (BMD), caused by mutations in the *DMD* gene. These conditions primarily affect skeletal muscle strength and lead to cardiomyopathy, but the underlying genetic defect and primary mechanism (dystrophin deficiency) are different from DM1.
- (D): Mitochondrial DNA mutations cause various mitochondrial disorders, often presenting with multi-system involvement including myopathy, encephalopathy, and cardiac abnormalities. However, the specific genetic defect (CTG repeat expansion in *DMPK*) and the mechanism (RNA toxicity and aberrant splicing) are distinct from mitochondrial dysfunction.
- (E): Increased myostatin expression is associated with conditions leading to muscle wasting, such as certain forms of muscular dystrophy or cachexia. Mutations in the *MSTN* gene (encoding myostatin) can cause muscle hypertrophy. This mechanism is unrelated to the pathogenesis of DM1, which involves RNA toxicity and aberrant splicing, not altered myostatin levels.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1 (DM1) is caused by a CTG repeat expansion in the *DMPK* gene, leading to RNA toxicity via sequestration of hnRNPA1 and subsequent aberrant splicing of target pre-mRNAs. This explains the multi-systemic features, including myotonia, muscle weakness, cataracts, and cardiac conduction defects. Key differentials include other muscular dystrophies (DMD, BMD, Limb-Girdle MD), mitochondrial disorders, and other forms of myotonic dystrophy (DM2, caused by CCTG repeat expansion in *CNBP* gene). The specific mechanism involves RNA toxicity and splicing defects, not primary protein dysfunction (like dystrophin) or mitochondrial defects.

<a id='question-135'></a>

### Question 135: Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1 (DM1)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the molecular basis of Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1 (DM1), specifically the role of CTG repeat expansion in the 3' untranslated region (UTR) of the *DMPK* gene, leading to RNA toxicity via sequestration of splicing factors and altered RNA processing, resulting in the characteristic clinical triad (myotonia, muscle weakness, cataracts) and other systemic manifestations.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male presents to his primary care physician complaining of progressive muscle weakness, particularly in his distal limbs, and difficulty relaxing his grip after shaking hands (myotonia). He also reports blurred vision and has noticed thinning hair on his forehead. His father exhibited similar symptoms, including cardiac conduction abnormalities, and died at age 60. On physical examination, the patient displays ptosis, facial muscle weakness (giving a "hatchet face" appearance), and delayed hand relaxation after percussion. His grip strength is significantly reduced. Ophthalmologic examination reveals early cataracts. Electromyography (EMG) shows myotonic discharges (high-frequency repetitive discharges followed by a period of electromechanical silence). Genetic testing reveals an expansion of a CTG trinucleotide repeat in the 3' UTR of the *DMPK* gene, with 1500 repeats. Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the pathogenesis of this patient's condition?

(A) Gain-of-function mutation leading to constitutive activation of a kinase involved in muscle contraction, causing sustained muscle fiber depolarization and impaired relaxation.
(B) Loss-of-function mutation resulting in deficient production of a chaperone protein required for proper folding of dystrophin, leading to muscle fiber instability and degeneration.
(C) RNA toxicity caused by the expanded CTG repeat sequestering splicing factors, leading to aberrant splicing of multiple target mRNAs, including *CLCN1* (chloride channel), contributing to myotonia and other systemic features.
(D) Haploinsufficiency of a gene encoding a structural component of the nuclear pore complex, impairing nuclear transport and leading to widespread cellular dysfunction, particularly affecting tissues with high metabolic demands like muscle and lens.
(E) Dominant-negative effect of a mutated protein involved in calcium homeostasis, disrupting excitation-contraction coupling and causing muscle stiffness and weakness.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation**: 45-year-old male with progressive distal muscle weakness, myotonia (delayed grip relaxation), frontal balding, cataracts, ptosis, facial weakness ("hatchet face"). These are classic features of Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1 (DM1).
- **Family History**: Father with similar symptoms and cardiac issues, suggesting an autosomal dominant inheritance pattern, consistent with DM1.
- **Physical Exam**: Confirms myotonia (delayed relaxation), muscle weakness (grip strength), ptosis, facial weakness, cataracts.
- **EMG**: Shows myotonic discharges, characteristic of DM1.
- **Genetic Testing**: Confirms the diagnosis by identifying an expanded CTG repeat (1500 repeats) in the 3' UTR of the *DMPK* gene. Normal individuals have <35 repeats; premutation carriers have 35-50 repeats; DM1 patients typically have >50 repeats, often >1000.
- **Question Stem**: Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the pathogenesis.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- DM1 is caused by an expansion of a CTG trinucleotide repeat in the 3' UTR of the *DMPK* gene on chromosome 19q13.3.
- The expanded repeat forms a large RNA structure (double-stranded RNA) within the pre-mRNA transcript of the *DMPK* gene.
- This expanded RNA structure acts as a "molecular sponge," sequestering RNA-binding proteins, particularly splicing factors like hnRNPA1 and hnRNPA2/B1.
- Sequestration of these splicing factors leads to their depletion from the nucleus and cytoplasm, disrupting the normal splicing of numerous target pre-mRNAs.
- Aberrant splicing affects a wide range of genes, leading to the production of abnormal proteins or altered protein levels.
- One key target is the *CLCN1* gene, which encodes the skeletal muscle chloride channel. Aberrant splicing of *CLCN1* leads to a non-functional or poorly functioning channel, impairing muscle relaxation after contraction (causing myotonia).
- Other affected genes contribute to the multi-systemic nature of DM1, including cataracts (lens opacity), cardiac conduction defects, endocrine dysfunction, and central nervous system involvement.
- The expanded RNA also interferes with other RNA processing events, such as mRNA transport and translation.
- Therefore, the primary pathogenic mechanism is RNA toxicity due to the expanded repeat, leading to sequestration of splicing factors and widespread aberrant RNA processing.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Gain-of-function kinase activation is not the mechanism of DM1. While muscle contraction involves kinases, DM1's primary defect is related to RNA processing and splicing factor sequestration, not constitutive kinase activation. This distractor might confuse students with other muscle disorders or signaling pathways.
- (B): Loss-of-function mutation affecting dystrophin folding is characteristic of Duchenne Muscular Dystrophy (DMD) or Becker Muscular Dystrophy (BMD), caused by mutations in the *DMD* gene. DM1 involves the *DMPK* gene and RNA toxicity, not dystrophin.
- (D): Haploinsufficiency of a nuclear pore complex component is characteristic of certain forms of congenital muscular dystrophy or other genetic disorders affecting nuclear transport. DM1 is caused by repeat expansion and RNA toxicity, not a primary defect in the nuclear pore complex. This might confuse students with disorders like X-linked Emery-Dreifuss muscular dystrophy (caused by mutations in *EMD* gene encoding emerin, a nuclear envelope protein).
- (E): Dominant-negative effect on calcium homeostasis is not the primary mechanism of DM1. While calcium handling is crucial for muscle function, the core defect in DM1 lies in the expanded RNA and its downstream effects on splicing and RNA processing, particularly affecting the *CLCN1* chloride channel. This distractor might relate to other myopathies or channelopathies.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1 (DM1) is caused by a CTG repeat expansion in the *DMPK* gene, leading to RNA toxicity via sequestration of splicing factors and aberrant splicing of target mRNAs, resulting in myotonia, muscle weakness, cataracts, and other systemic features. Key differentials include Myotonic Dystrophy Type 2 (DM2, caused by CCTG repeat expansion in *CNBP* gene), Duchenne/Becker Muscular Dystrophy (DMD/BMD, *DMD* gene), Limb-Girdle Muscular Dystrophies (various genes), and other myopathies. The hallmark of DM1 is the expanded CTG repeat and its downstream effects on RNA processing.

<a id='question-136'></a>

### Question 136: Friedreich Ataxia - Pathophysiology of Frataxin Deficiency
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Friedreich ataxia (FA) is an autosomal recessive neurodegenerative disorder caused by an expanded GAA trinucleotide repeat in the first intron of the *FXN* gene, leading to reduced expression of frataxin, a mitochondrial protein essential for iron-sulfur cluster (ISC) biogenesis. Reduced ISC synthesis impairs mitochondrial function, particularly in neurons and cardiomyocytes. (弗里德里希共济失调症 (FA) 是一种常染色体隐性遗传的神经退行性疾病，由 *FXN* 基因第一个内含子中的 GAA 三核苷酸重复序列扩增引起，导致编码铁硫簇生物合成所需线粒体蛋白弗拉他كس丁的表达减少。 铁硫簇合成减少会损害线粒体功能，尤其是在神经元和心肌细胞中。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 12-year-old boy presents to his pediatrician with progressive gait ataxia, slurred speech (dysarthria), and difficulty with fine motor skills over the past 3 years. His symptoms began with clumsiness and frequent falls. Family history reveals that his older brother died at age 15 from hypertrophic cardiomyopathy. Physical examination shows bilateral pes cavus (high-arched feet), scoliosis, and absent deep tendon reflexes in the lower extremities. Neurological examination confirms cerebellar ataxia, dysarthria, and sensory neuropathy. An electrocardiogram (ECG) shows evidence of left ventricular hypertrophy. Genetic testing reveals an expanded GAA repeat in the *FXN* gene. A muscle biopsy shows ragged red fibers on Gomori trichrome stain, indicative of mitochondrial dysfunction. Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the pathophysiology underlying this patient's condition?

(A) Impaired mitochondrial oxidative phosphorylation due to defects in the electron transport chain complexes I, II, and III caused by reduced levels of coenzyme Q10.
(B) Decreased synthesis of iron-sulfur clusters (ISCs) required for the function of several mitochondrial enzymes, including those involved in the electron transport chain and pyruvate dehydrogenase complex.
(C) Accumulation of reactive oxygen species (ROS) due to impaired activity of superoxide dismutase (SOD) caused by reduced levels of copper and zinc.
(D) Defective mitochondrial DNA replication due to mutations in genes encoding mitochondrial polymerase gamma, leading to multiple deletions and depletion of mtDNA.
(E) Impaired fatty acid oxidation due to deficiency of carnitine palmitoyltransferase I (CPT I), resulting in accumulation of long-chain fatty acids and energy deficiency.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 12-year-old boy with progressive gait ataxia, dysarthria, fine motor difficulties, clumsiness, falls (neurological symptoms).
- **Family History:** Older brother died young from hypertrophic cardiomyopathy (cardiomyopathy association).
- **Physical Exam:** Pes cavus, scoliosis, absent DTRs (neurological/skeletal signs).
- **Neurological Exam:** Cerebellar ataxia, dysarthria, sensory neuropathy (neurological signs).
- **ECG:** Left ventricular hypertrophy (cardiomyopathy).
- **Genetic Testing:** Expanded GAA repeat in *FXN* gene (confirms Friedreich Ataxia).
- **Muscle Biopsy:** Ragged red fibers (indicates mitochondrial dysfunction).
- **Question:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the pathophysiology.

The combination of progressive ataxia, cardiomyopathy, skeletal deformities, sensory neuropathy, absent reflexes, ragged red fibers, and a confirmed *FXN* gene GAA repeat expansion strongly points to Friedreich Ataxia. The core defect in FA is reduced frataxin protein levels due to the GAA repeat expansion silencing the *FXN* gene. Frataxin is a mitochondrial protein crucial for iron-sulfur cluster (ISC) biogenesis. Impaired ISC synthesis affects multiple mitochondrial enzymes, leading to mitochondrial dysfunction, particularly in high-energy-demand tissues like neurons and the heart.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Gene:** *FXN* gene on chromosome 9q13.
- **Mutation:** Expansion of a GAA trinucleotide repeat in the first intron. Normal alleles have 5-35 repeats; FA alleles typically have >66 repeats (often 80-1000+).
- **Mechanism:** The expanded repeat causes epigenetic modifications (hypermethylation of the promoter region) and sequestration of transcription factors, leading to transcriptional silencing of the *FXN* gene and severely reduced frataxin mRNA and protein levels.
- **Frataxin Function:** Frataxin is a mitochondrial protein involved in iron homeostasis and the biogenesis of iron-sulfur clusters (ISCs). It is thought to protect mitochondria from oxidative stress and facilitate the insertion of iron into ISCs.
- **Iron-Sulfur Clusters (ISCs):** These are essential prosthetic groups for numerous enzymes involved in critical metabolic pathways, including:
    - **Electron Transport Chain (ETC):** Complexes I (NADH dehydrogenase), II (succinate dehydrogenase), III (cytochrome bc1 complex), and IV (cytochrome c oxidase) contain ISCs.
    - **Pyruvate Dehydrogenase Complex (PDH):** Converts pyruvate to acetyl-CoA.
    - **α-Ketoglutarate Dehydrogenase Complex:** Links glycolysis to the citric acid cycle.
    - **Aconitase:** Enzyme in the citric acid cycle.
    - **Succinate Dehydrogenase:** Enzyme in both the citric acid cycle and ETC.
- **Pathophysiology:** Reduced frataxin leads to impaired ISC synthesis. This deficiency affects the function of ISC-dependent enzymes, particularly in the ETC and PDH complex. Impaired ETC function leads to decreased ATP production via oxidative phosphorylation and increased ROS production. Impaired PDH function disrupts energy metabolism. The combination of energy deficiency and oxidative stress contributes to the neurodegeneration (ataxia, neuropathy), cardiomyopathy, and other clinical features of FA. Ragged red fibers seen on muscle biopsy are a hallmark of mitochondrial dysfunction, representing accumulations of abnormal mitochondria.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Impaired mitochondrial oxidative phosphorylation due to defects in the electron transport chain complexes I, II, and III caused by reduced levels of coenzyme Q10. This is incorrect. While ETC complexes I, II, and III are affected in FA due to ISC deficiency, the primary defect is not reduced coenzyme Q10 (ubiquinone). CoQ10 is a mobile electron carrier within the ETC, and its deficiency causes different conditions (e.g., primary CoQ10 deficiency). The problem in FA is the lack of functional ISC-containing enzymes within these complexes.
- (C): Accumulation of reactive oxygen species (ROS) due to impaired activity of superoxide dismutase (SOD) caused by reduced levels of copper and zinc. This is incorrect. While ROS accumulation *is* a consequence of mitochondrial dysfunction in FA (due to impaired ETC), the primary defect is not SOD deficiency or reduced copper/zinc levels. SOD enzymes (Cu/Zn-SOD, Mn-SOD) are crucial for ROS detoxification, but their function is not the primary molecular target affected by frataxin deficiency. Frataxin itself may play a role in protecting against oxidative stress, but the core mechanism is ISC biogenesis.
- (D): Defective mitochondrial DNA replication due to mutations in genes encoding mitochondrial polymerase gamma, leading to multiple deletions and depletion of mtDNA. This is incorrect. This describes mitochondrial depletion syndromes or disorders caused by mutations in mitochondrial DNA polymerase gamma (POLG), such as Alpers-Huttenlocher syndrome or progressive external ophthalmoplegia (PEO). FA is caused by a nuclear gene mutation (*FXN*) affecting mitochondrial function, not a primary defect in mtDNA replication or integrity.
- (E): Impaired fatty acid oxidation due to deficiency of carnitine palmitoyltransferase I (CPT I), resulting in accumulation of long-chain fatty acids and energy deficiency. This is incorrect. This describes CPT I deficiency, a disorder of fatty acid metabolism. While energy deficiency is a feature of FA, the primary metabolic defect is not in fatty acid oxidation or CPT I activity. The defect lies in ISC biogenesis affecting the ETC and other pathways.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Friedreich ataxia is caused by a GAA repeat expansion in the *FXN* gene intron, leading to reduced frataxin protein. Frataxin is essential for mitochondrial iron-sulfur cluster (ISC) biogenesis. Defective ISC synthesis impairs enzymes like those in the electron transport chain and pyruvate dehydrogenase, causing mitochondrial dysfunction, energy deficiency, and oxidative stress, primarily affecting neurons and the heart. Key differentials include other ataxias (e.g., spinocerebellar ataxias, mitochondrial ataxias like MELAS/MERRF), but the specific genetic defect (*FXN* GAA repeat), clinical triad (ataxia, cardiomyopathy, diabetes - though diabetes is less common), and ragged red fibers point strongly to FA.
