<a id='question-137'></a>

### Question 137: Friedreich Ataxia
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the clinical presentation, inheritance pattern, and molecular basis of Friedreich Ataxia, specifically focusing on the role of GAA repeat expansion in the *FXN* gene and its impact on frataxin protein levels and mitochondrial function. (考察弗里德里希共济失调症的临床表现、遗传模式和分子基础，特别是 *FXN* 基因中 GAA 重复序列的扩增及其对弗拉他كس蛋白水平和线粒体功能的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 12-year-old boy presents to his pediatrician with a 3-year history of progressive gait instability, frequent falls, and slurred speech. His parents report that he initially had difficulty with coordination during sports but now requires a wheelchair for ambulation. Neurological examination reveals severe limb ataxia, dysarthria, absent deep tendon reflexes (DTRs) in the lower extremities, and extensor plantar responses (Babinski sign). He also has pes cavus bilaterally. Cardiac evaluation reveals hypertrophic cardiomyopathy with left ventricular hypertrophy and arrhythmias. Skeletal imaging shows progressive kyphoscoliosis. Family history is significant for a maternal uncle who died in his 30s from heart failure. Genetic testing reveals an expanded GAA trinucleotide repeat in the *FXN* gene. A muscle biopsy shows ragged red fibers on Gomori trichrome stain. Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the pathophysiology underlying this patient's condition?

(A) Reduced synthesis of mitochondrial respiratory chain complexes due to impaired iron-sulfur cluster biogenesis, leading to decreased ATP production and increased oxidative stress.
(B) Impaired DNA repair mechanisms due to a deficiency in mismatch repair proteins, resulting in genomic instability and increased mutation rates.
(C) Defective protein folding and aggregation of mutant huntingtin protein in the striatum, causing neuronal dysfunction and degeneration.
(D) Decreased activity of the enzyme galactose-1-phosphate uridyltransferase (GALT) leading to accumulation of galactose-1-phosphate and galactitol, causing cataracts and liver damage.
(E) Impaired degradation of polyglutamine tracts in proteins due to a deficiency in the ubiquitin-proteasome system, leading to neuronal dysfunction.

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 12-year-old boy with progressive gait instability, falls, slurred speech (dysarthria), limb ataxia, absent DTRs, extensor plantar responses (Babinski sign), pes cavus, hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, kyphoscoliosis. This constellation of neurological (ataxia, sensory loss, areflexia, Babinski), cardiac (cardiomyopathy), and skeletal (pes cavus, scoliosis) findings is highly suggestive of Friedreich Ataxia (FRDA).
*   **Family History:** Maternal uncle died young from heart failure, suggesting a possible inherited condition, potentially autosomal recessive given the pattern.
*   **Genetic Testing:** Expanded GAA trinucleotide repeat in the *FXN* gene confirms the diagnosis of FRDA. The *FXN* gene encodes frataxin.
*   **Muscle Biopsy:** Ragged red fibers indicate mitochondrial dysfunction.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism underlying the pathophysiology.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **FRDA Genetics:** FRDA is an autosomal recessive disorder caused by an expansion of a GAA trinucleotide repeat in the first intron of the *FXN* gene on chromosome 9. Normal individuals have 5-35 repeats, while affected individuals typically have >200 repeats.
*   **Mechanism of Pathogenesis:** The expanded GAA repeat leads to hypermethylation of the CpG islands within the repeat sequence and the promoter region of the *FXN* gene. This epigenetic modification silences the *FXN* gene, resulting in reduced transcription and consequently, severely decreased levels of frataxin protein.
*   **Frataxin Function:** Frataxin is a mitochondrial protein involved in iron-sulfur cluster (ISC) biogenesis. ISCs are essential prosthetic groups for numerous proteins, including components of the electron transport chain (ETC) complexes I, II, and IV, as well as aconitase and succinate dehydrogenase.
*   **Consequences of Frataxin Deficiency:** Reduced frataxin levels impair ISC synthesis, leading to:
    *   **Mitochondrial Dysfunction:** Deficiency of ISCs in ETC complexes impairs oxidative phosphorylation, reducing ATP production.
    *   **Increased Oxidative Stress:** Impaired ETC function leads to electron leakage and increased production of reactive oxygen species (ROS), causing oxidative damage to mitochondrial DNA, proteins, and lipids.
    *   **Iron Accumulation:** Frataxin is also implicated in iron homeostasis within mitochondria. Its deficiency can lead to iron accumulation, further contributing to oxidative stress.
*   **Clinical Manifestations:** The mitochondrial dysfunction and oxidative stress primarily affect tissues with high energy demands, such as the nervous system (especially dorsal root ganglia, spinocerebellar tracts, posterior columns), heart, and skeletal muscle, explaining the patient's ataxia, cardiomyopathy, and other symptoms. Ragged red fibers on muscle biopsy are a histological hallmark of mitochondrial myopathy, reflecting the accumulation of abnormal mitochondria.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(B) Impaired DNA repair mechanisms due to a deficiency in mismatch repair proteins, resulting in genomic instability and increased mutation rates.** This describes conditions like Lynch syndrome (hereditary nonpolyposis colorectal cancer, HNPCC) caused by mutations in mismatch repair genes (e.g., *MLH1*, *MSH2*). FRDA is caused by a repeat expansion leading to transcriptional silencing, not a primary defect in DNA repair proteins.
*   **(C) Defective protein folding and aggregation of mutant huntingtin protein in the striatum, causing neuronal dysfunction and degeneration.** This describes Huntington's disease, an autosomal dominant neurodegenerative disorder caused by a CAG repeat expansion in the *HTT* gene, leading to an expanded polyglutamine tract in the huntingtin protein. The clinical features (chorea, cognitive decline, psychiatric symptoms) and molecular mechanism are distinct from FRDA.
*   **(D) Decreased activity of the enzyme galactose-1-phosphate uridyltransferase (GALT) leading to accumulation of galactose-1-phosphate and galactitol, causing cataracts and liver damage.** This describes classic galactosemia, an autosomal recessive metabolic disorder caused by mutations in the *GALT* gene. The clinical features (feeding intolerance, jaundice, cataracts, intellectual disability) and biochemical defect are unrelated to FRDA.
*   **(E) Impaired degradation of polyglutamine tracts in proteins due to a deficiency in the ubiquitin-proteasome system, leading to neuronal dysfunction.** While expanded polyglutamine tracts are involved in some neurodegenerative diseases (like Huntington's disease), FRDA is caused by a GAA repeat expansion, not a polyglutamine expansion. Furthermore, the primary defect in FRDA is transcriptional silencing and reduced frataxin protein levels, not impaired degradation of polyglutamine tracts.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Friedreich Ataxia is caused by a GAA repeat expansion in the *FXN* gene, leading to reduced frataxin protein levels. Frataxin deficiency impairs mitochondrial iron-sulfur cluster biogenesis, resulting in mitochondrial dysfunction, increased oxidative stress, and ATP depletion, primarily affecting neurons, heart, and muscle. Key differentials include other spinocerebellar ataxias (SCAs), mitochondrial disorders (e.g., MELAS, MERRF), and Huntington's disease. Remember the specific genetic repeat (GAA), gene (*FXN*), protein (frataxin), and functional consequence (impaired ISC biogenesis/mitochondrial dysfunction).

<a id='question-138'></a>

### Question 138: Spinocerebellar Ataxias (SCAs)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the molecular basis of trinucleotide repeat expansion disorders, specifically polyglutamine expansions in Spinocerebellar Ataxias (SCAs), and their impact on protein function and neuronal degeneration. (理解三核苷酸重复序列扩张疾病的分子基础，特别是脊髓小脑共济失调症 (SCA) 中的多谷氨酰胺扩张，以及它们对蛋白质功能和神经元退化的影响。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 48-year-old male presents to a neurology clinic with a 5-year history of progressive gait ataxia, dysarthria, and dysphagia. His father and paternal grandfather also exhibited similar symptoms, with onset in their late 40s and 50s, respectively. Neurological examination reveals cerebellar signs including intention tremor, dysmetria, and nystagmus. Cognitive function is currently intact. Family history is significant for a known genetic disorder affecting the nervous system. Genetic testing reveals an expansion of a CAG repeat in the *ATXN1* gene. The patient's allele size is 38 CAG repeats, while his asymptomatic adult daughter carries an allele with 22 CAG repeats (normal range < 26) and another with 31 CAG repeats (intermediate range). A Western blot analysis of the patient's brain tissue shows an abnormally large protein product compared to controls, and this abnormal protein forms intranuclear inclusions. The patient is considering potential therapeutic strategies. Which of the following mechanisms is most likely responsible for the pathogenesis of this patient's condition?

(A) Gain-of-function mutation leading to constitutive activation of a tyrosine kinase receptor, promoting uncontrolled cell proliferation and neuronal apoptosis.
(B) Loss-of-function mutation resulting in decreased expression of a crucial chaperone protein, leading to misfolding and aggregation of other cellular proteins.
(C) Gain-of-function mutation involving the formation of toxic polyglutamine tracts within the ATXN1 protein, leading to protein misfolding, aggregation, and neuronal dysfunction.
(D) Dominant-negative effect where the mutant ATXN1 protein interferes with the function of the wild-type ATXN1 protein, impairing its normal role in transcriptional regulation.
(E) Haploinsufficiency caused by reduced expression of the ATXN1 protein, disrupting the balance of proteins involved in synaptic transmission.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 48-year-old male with progressive gait ataxia, dysarthria, dysphagia (classic cerebellar signs).
*   **Family History:** Autosomal dominant inheritance pattern (father, paternal grandfather affected), onset in late 40s/50s.
*   **Neurological Exam:** Confirms cerebellar dysfunction (intention tremor, dysmetria, nystagmus).
*   **Genetic Testing:** CAG repeat expansion in *ATXN1* gene (38 repeats, pathogenic range typically > 36 for ATXN1). Daughter has normal (22) and intermediate (31) alleles. This points towards a trinucleotide repeat disorder.
*   **Western Blot:** Abnormally large protein product in brain tissue, intranuclear inclusions. This indicates the expanded CAG repeat leads to an elongated protein with a propensity to aggregate.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism of pathogenesis.

These clues strongly suggest a polyglutamine expansion disorder, specifically SCA type 1 (SCA1) caused by *ATXN1* CAG repeat expansion. The key pathogenic mechanism involves the expanded polyglutamine tract.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **SCA1 and *ATXN1*:** Spinocerebellar ataxia type 1 (SCA1) is an autosomal dominant neurodegenerative disorder caused by a CAG trinucleotide repeat expansion in the *ATXN1* gene (located on chromosome 6p23.3).
*   **Normal Function:** The normal ATXN1 protein (ataxin-1) is involved in transcriptional regulation, chromatin remodeling, and DNA repair.
*   **Pathogenic Expansion:** In SCA1, the CAG repeat number expands beyond a threshold (typically > 36 repeats for *ATXN1*). This leads to the insertion of additional glutamine (Gln) residues into the N-terminal region of the ataxin-1 protein.
*   **Polyglutamine Tract:** The expanded polyglutamine tract (polyQ) is highly prone to misfolding and aggregation.
*   **Toxic Gain-of-Function:** The elongated, misfolded ataxin-1 protein forms intranuclear inclusions and aggregates, disrupting normal cellular processes, including transcription, protein degradation pathways (ubiquitin-proteasome system), and mitochondrial function. This leads to neuronal dysfunction and selective degeneration, particularly in the cerebellum and brainstem. This is considered a toxic gain-of-function mechanism.
*   **Anticipation:** Trinucleotide repeat disorders often exhibit anticipation, meaning the severity increases and/or the age of onset decreases in successive generations, due to further expansion of the repeat during gametogenesis. The daughter's intermediate allele (31 repeats) could potentially expand further if passed on.
*   **Mechanism Summary:** The core pathogenic mechanism is the toxic gain-of-function resulting from the expanded polyglutamine tract in the mutant ATXN1 protein, leading to protein misfolding, aggregation, and neuronal toxicity.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Gain-of-function mutation leading to constitutive activation of a tyrosine kinase receptor, promoting uncontrolled cell proliferation and neuronal apoptosis. This mechanism is characteristic of certain oncogenes (e.g., EGFR mutations in cancer) or some developmental disorders, but not SCA1. SCA1 involves protein aggregation, not receptor tyrosine kinase activation.
- (B): Loss-of-function mutation resulting in decreased expression of a crucial chaperone protein, leading to misfolding and aggregation of other cellular proteins. While protein misfolding and aggregation are involved in SCA1, the primary defect is not a loss-of-function of a chaperone protein. The mutant ATXN1 protein itself is the primary misfolded and aggregating species, acting in a toxic gain-of-function manner. Chaperone dysfunction can occur downstream, but it's not the initiating event.
- (D): Dominant-negative effect where the mutant ATXN1 protein interferes with the function of the wild-type ATXN1 protein, impairing its normal role in transcriptional regulation. While some dominant mutations act via a dominant-negative mechanism, the primary pathology in SCA1 is attributed to the toxic effects of the mutant protein itself (gain-of-function toxicity due to aggregation), rather than its interference with the wild-type protein's function. Although some interference might occur, it's not the main driver of neurodegeneration.
- (E): Haploinsufficiency caused by reduced expression of the ATXN1 protein, disrupting the balance of proteins involved in synaptic transmission. Haploinsufficiency involves a loss-of-function due to having only one functional copy of a gene. SCA1 is caused by an expanded repeat leading to a toxic gain-of-function, not a loss of function or reduced expression (although some studies suggest altered expression levels, the primary toxicity is from the expanded protein itself).

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Trinucleotide repeat expansions, particularly CAG repeats leading to polyglutamine tracts, are a major cause of inherited neurodegenerative diseases like Huntington's disease (HTT gene) and Spinocerebellar Ataxias (e.g., ATXN1, ATXN3, ATXN6, ATXN7, ATXN8, CACNA1A). The pathogenesis typically involves a toxic gain-of-function due to protein misfolding and aggregation. Key differentials include other SCAs (caused by different gene expansions), Huntington's disease, and other forms of ataxia (e.g., Friedreich's ataxia - GAA repeat in FXN, mitochondrial disorders). Understanding the specific gene involved and the nature of the repeat expansion (CAG, CTG, CCTG, GAA) is crucial.

<a id='question-139'></a>

### Question 139: Spinal and Bulbar Muscular Atrophy (Kennedy Disease)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of Spinal and Bulbar Muscular Atrophy (SBMA), also known as Kennedy disease, an X-linked recessive disorder caused by a CAG trinucleotide repeat expansion in the androgen receptor (AR) gene, leading to lower motor neuron (LMN) degeneration, muscle weakness, and endocrine abnormalities. The question focuses on the molecular mechanism and clinical presentation.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male presents to his primary care physician complaining of progressive muscle weakness and cramping, primarily affecting his hands, forearms, and legs, over the past 5 years. He also reports difficulty swallowing (dysphagia) and slurred speech (dysarthria) that have worsened recently. His past medical history is significant for type 2 diabetes mellitus, diagnosed 3 years ago, and gynecomastia, noted since puberty. Family history reveals that his maternal grandfather and two maternal uncles had similar symptoms of muscle weakness and died in their 50s. Physical examination shows fasciculations in the tongue and limbs, muscle atrophy in the distal muscles of the upper and lower extremities, and decreased deep tendon reflexes. Serum creatine kinase (CK) levels are elevated at 15 times the upper limit of normal. Electromyography (EMG) shows evidence of denervation and reinnervation in multiple muscle groups. Genetic testing reveals an expanded CAG repeat in the androgen receptor gene.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most directly responsible for the lower motor neuron degeneration observed in this patient?

(A) Impaired mitochondrial oxidative phosphorylation due to mutations in mitochondrial DNA, leading to energy deficiency in motor neurons.
(B) Formation of toxic polyglutamine aggregates from the expanded CAG repeat in the androgen receptor protein, disrupting neuronal function and proteasomal degradation.
(C) Defective axonal transport due to mutations in the neurofilament genes, causing accumulation of axonal components and neuronal dysfunction.
(D) Impaired RNA processing and splicing due to mutations in the SMN1 gene, leading to reduced levels of functional survival motor neuron (SMN) protein.
(E) Aberrant prion protein folding and aggregation, leading to neuronal cell death and spongiform changes in the central nervous system.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 45-year-old male with progressive muscle weakness, cramping, dysphagia, dysarthria. This points towards a neuromuscular disorder affecting both upper and lower motor neurons, or primarily lower motor neurons with bulbar involvement.
*   **Past Medical History:** Type 2 diabetes and gynecomastia. Gynecomastia is a common endocrine feature associated with androgen receptor dysfunction. Diabetes is also sometimes associated.
*   **Family History:** Maternal grandfather and uncles with similar symptoms, suggesting an X-linked recessive inheritance pattern (males affected, transmitted through carrier females).
*   **Physical Exam:** Fasciculations, muscle atrophy (distal > proximal), decreased reflexes. These are classic signs of lower motor neuron (LMN) degeneration.
*   **Lab Findings:** Elevated CK (muscle damage/degeneration), EMG showing denervation/reinnervation (LMN pathology).
*   **Genetic Testing:** Expanded CAG repeat in the androgen receptor (AR) gene. This is the definitive molecular diagnosis for Spinal and Bulbar Muscular Atrophy (Kennedy disease).
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism responsible for LMN degeneration.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Disease:** Spinal and Bulbar Muscular Atrophy (SBMA), also known as Kennedy disease.
*   **Inheritance:** X-linked recessive.
*   **Gene:** Androgen Receptor (AR) gene located on the X chromosome (Xq11-12).
*   **Mutation:** Expansion of a CAG trinucleotide repeat within exon 1 of the AR gene. Normal alleles have 9-36 repeats; affected individuals typically have >38 repeats (often >50).
*   **Molecular Mechanism:** The expanded CAG repeat leads to the production of an androgen receptor protein with an abnormally long polyglutamine tract near its N-terminus. This elongated polyglutamine tract causes the AR protein to misfold and aggregate, forming toxic intranuclear inclusions. These aggregates disrupt normal cellular processes, including proteasomal function, transcription, and axonal transport. The accumulation of these toxic polyglutamine aggregates, particularly in motor neurons (especially LMNs in the spinal cord and brainstem), leads to neuronal dysfunction and eventual degeneration. The AR protein is normally expressed in various tissues, including motor neurons, skeletal muscle, and endocrine tissues, explaining the neuromuscular and endocrine manifestations. The polyglutamine expansion is a classic example of a "toxic gain-of-function" mechanism, where the mutant protein acquires a new, detrimental function (aggregation) rather than losing its normal function.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Impaired mitochondrial oxidative phosphorylation due to mutations in mitochondrial DNA:** This describes mitochondrial myopathies (e.g., MELAS, MERRF). While mitochondrial dysfunction can cause muscle weakness, it typically presents differently (e.g., ophthalmoplegia, lactic acidosis, multi-system involvement) and is not associated with CAG repeat expansions in the AR gene or X-linked inheritance in this specific pattern. The genetic basis is different (mtDNA vs. nuclear AR gene).
*   **(C) Defective axonal transport due to mutations in the neurofilament genes:** Mutations in neurofilament genes (e.g., NFL, NFM, NFH) can cause hereditary motor and sensory neuropathies (HMSN) or amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS), leading to axonal transport defects and neurodegeneration. However, these are typically autosomal dominant or recessive disorders, not X-linked, and are not caused by CAG repeat expansions in the AR gene. The specific gene and mutation type are incorrect for Kennedy disease.
*   **(D) Impaired RNA processing and splicing due to mutations in the SMN1 gene:** This describes Spinal Muscular Atrophy (SMA), an autosomal recessive disorder caused by deletions or mutations in the SMN1 gene, leading to reduced levels of the survival motor neuron (SMN) protein. SMA primarily affects LMNs, causing progressive muscle weakness and atrophy, but it is not X-linked, not caused by a CAG repeat expansion in the AR gene, and typically does not present with gynecomastia or diabetes. The genetic basis and molecular mechanism are entirely different.
*   **(E) Aberrant prion protein folding and aggregation:** This describes prion diseases (e.g., Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease, Gerstmann-Sträussler-Scheinker syndrome), which are neurodegenerative disorders caused by the misfolding and aggregation of the prion protein (PrPSc). These diseases typically cause rapidly progressive dementia, ataxia, and myoclonus, and are not associated with CAG repeat expansions in the AR gene or X-linked inheritance. The clinical presentation and underlying molecular pathology are distinct from Kennedy disease.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Kennedy disease (SBMA) is a key example of a trinucleotide repeat disorder (specifically, a polyglutamine expansion disease) causing neurodegeneration. The expanded CAG repeat in the AR gene leads to toxic polyglutamine aggregates, primarily affecting lower motor neurons. Remember to differentiate it from other neuromuscular disorders like SMA (SMN1 gene, autosomal recessive), mitochondrial myopathies (mtDNA mutations), and Huntington's disease (HTT gene, autosomal dominant, CAG repeat in brain). Also, recall the X-linked inheritance pattern and associated endocrine features (gynecomastia, diabetes).

<a id='question-140'></a>

### Question 140: Huntington Disease - Anticipation and Molecular Mechanism
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of Huntington disease (HD), an autosomal dominant neurodegenerative disorder caused by a CAG repeat expansion in the *HTT* gene, specifically focusing on the phenomenon of anticipation (earlier onset and increased severity in successive generations) and the molecular basis of this phenomenon. (亨廷顿病，一种由 *HTT* 基因中 CAG 重复序列扩增引起的常染色体显性神经退行性疾病，重点考察“预期”现象（后代发病年龄提前、病情加重）及其分子机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to a neurology clinic with progressive motor dysfunction, including chorea, rigidity, and bradykinesia, along with cognitive decline and psychiatric disturbances (irritability, depression). His symptoms began approximately 10 years ago. His father was diagnosed with Huntington disease at age 55 and died at age 68. His paternal grandmother also had Huntington disease, diagnosed at age 40. The patient's younger sister, age 58, was diagnosed with Huntington disease 5 years ago at age 53. Genetic testing confirms the presence of an expanded CAG repeat in the *HTT* gene. The patient's allele has 45 CAG repeats. His father's DNA sample (obtained post-mortem) showed 38 CAG repeats. His paternal grandmother's DNA sample showed 30 CAG repeats. The patient's sister has 42 CAG repeats. The patient's adult son, age 35, is asymptomatic but underwent predictive genetic testing, which revealed 50 CAG repeats. Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the earlier onset of Huntington disease in the patient's son compared to his father?

(A) Somatic mosaicism for the expanded CAG repeat in the patient's germline cells, leading to a higher proportion of affected sperm.
(B) Increased DNA methylation of the *HTT* gene promoter in the patient's son, leading to reduced expression of the mutant huntingtin protein.
(C) Uniparental disomy for chromosome 4 in the patient's son, resulting in homozygosity for the expanded allele.
(D) Increased frequency of meiotic recombination events between the *HTT* locus and nearby genes in the patient's father, leading to instability of the CAG repeat.
(E) Increased rate of DNA polymerase slippage during replication of the *HTT* gene in the patient's son's germline cells, leading to further CAG repeat expansion.

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with classic symptoms of Huntington disease (chorea, rigidity, bradykinesia, cognitive decline, psychiatric issues) starting at age 52.
- **Family History:** Strong multigenerational history of Huntington disease. Father diagnosed at 55, paternal grandmother at 40, sister at 53. This pattern suggests autosomal dominant inheritance.
- **Genetic Testing:** Confirms Huntington disease. Patient has 45 CAG repeats. Father had 38 repeats. Grandmother had 30 repeats. Sister has 42 repeats.
- **Anticipation:** The son (age 35, asymptomatic) has 50 CAG repeats, which is more than the father (38 repeats) and the patient (45 repeats). The son is predicted to have an earlier onset than his father, despite having a larger repeat expansion than his father. This phenomenon is called anticipation.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the earlier onset in the son compared to the father.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Huntington Disease:** Caused by an expansion of a CAG trinucleotide repeat in the *HTT* gene on chromosome 4. The expanded CAG repeat codes for an elongated polyglutamine (polyQ) tract in the huntingtin protein.
- **Mechanism of HD:** The mutant huntingtin protein with an expanded polyQ tract misfolds, aggregates, and disrupts neuronal function, particularly in the striatum and cortex, leading to neurodegeneration.
- **Inheritance Pattern:** Autosomal dominant.
- **Anticipation:** A characteristic feature of trinucleotide repeat disorders, including Huntington disease. Anticipation refers to the tendency for the disease to appear at an earlier age and/or with increased severity in successive generations.
- **Molecular Basis of Anticipation in HD:** The CAG repeat expansion is unstable and tends to increase in size during transmission from parent to offspring, particularly through the paternal germline (though maternal transmission can also lead to expansion). This instability is primarily due to DNA polymerase slippage during DNA replication in the germ cells. During replication, the polymerase can "slip" backward on the template strand, adding extra CAG repeats to the new strand. The larger the repeat size in the parent, the higher the probability of slippage and further expansion in the offspring.
- **Applying to the Vignette:** The father had 38 repeats and onset at 55. The patient has 45 repeats and onset at 52 (earlier onset). The son has 50 repeats (larger expansion than the father) and is predicted to have an even earlier onset. This progression fits the pattern of anticipation driven by repeat expansion. The mechanism responsible for this expansion is DNA polymerase slippage during replication in the germline.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Somatic mosaicism:** While somatic mosaicism can occur in Huntington disease, it typically refers to variations in repeat size within different tissues of an individual, not necessarily the germline. Furthermore, while germline mosaicism (where some germ cells have a larger repeat than others) can contribute to variable expressivity or anticipation, the primary driver of *increasing* repeat size across generations is slippage during replication, not just the proportion of affected sperm due to pre-existing mosaicism. The core mechanism is the *increase* in repeat number, not just the distribution of existing repeats.
- (B) **DNA methylation:** Increased DNA methylation typically leads to gene silencing or reduced expression. While epigenetic modifications can influence HD progression, increased methylation of the *HTT* promoter would *decrease* the expression of the mutant huntingtin protein, potentially ameliorating the phenotype, not explaining the earlier onset associated with a larger repeat expansion. In fact, some studies suggest decreased methylation in HD.
- (C) **Uniparental disomy (UPD):** UPD involves inheriting both copies of a chromosome from a single parent. If the son inherited both copies of chromosome 4 from his father (who had the expanded allele), he would be homozygous for the expanded allele (38 repeats). However, the son has 50 repeats, which is significantly larger than his father's 38 repeats. UPD cannot explain the *increase* in repeat size. Also, UPD is a rare event and not the typical mechanism for anticipation in HD.
- (D) **Meiotic recombination:** Increased recombination events between the *HTT* locus and nearby genes could potentially lead to instability, but the primary mechanism for CAG repeat expansion in HD is DNA polymerase slippage during replication, not recombination. Recombination primarily shuffles existing alleles, it doesn't inherently cause the addition of repeat units.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Huntington disease is a classic example of a trinucleotide repeat disorder exhibiting anticipation, primarily due to DNA polymerase slippage leading to CAG repeat expansion during germline transmission. Larger repeat sizes correlate with earlier onset and increased severity.
- **Differentials:** Other trinucleotide repeat disorders showing anticipation include myotonic dystrophy (CTG repeat in *DMPK*), fragile X syndrome (CGG repeat in *FMR1*), and spinocerebellar ataxias (various repeats). The specific mechanism (slippage) and the direction of anticipation (paternal in HD, maternal in myotonic dystrophy and fragile X) are important distinctions. Understand the difference between anticipation (earlier onset/severity) and genetic instability (repeat expansion).

<a id='question-141'></a>

### Question 141: Genomic Imprinting: Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Genomic imprinting is an epigenetic phenomenon where certain genes are expressed in a parent-of-origin-specific manner. Deletion or inactivation of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11.2-q13 region leads to Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS), characterized by neonatal hypotonia, feeding difficulties, hyperphagia, obesity, developmental delay, and hypogonadism. The critical genes in this region include *SNRPN* and *NKX1*. Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15 or an imprinting defect can also cause PWS.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy is brought to the pediatrician by his parents due to concerns about his development and eating habits. He was born full-term but exhibited significant hypotonia and poor suckling reflex in the neonatal period, requiring feeding assistance via nasogastric tube for the first 6 months. Since infancy, he has shown developmental delay, particularly in motor skills and speech. Over the past year, his parents have noticed an insatiable appetite (hyperphagia) leading to rapid weight gain and obesity. He also displays behavioral issues, including temper tantrums and obsessive-compulsive tendencies. Physical examination reveals short stature, small hands and feet, hypogonadism (small penis, undescended testes), and characteristic facial features including almond-shaped eyes and a narrow forehead. Karyotyping reveals a normal male karyotype (46,XY). Fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH) analysis using probes specific for the 15q11-q13 region shows that the paternal chromosome 15 is missing. Methylation analysis of the maternal chromosome 15 at the imprinting control region (ICR1) shows hypermethylation, consistent with paternal imprinting.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 21, leading to Down syndrome.
(B) Deletion of the maternal copy of the *UBE3A* gene on chromosome 15, leading to Angelman syndrome.
(C) Deletion of the paternal copy of the critical region on chromosome 15 (15q11.2-q13), leading to Prader-Willi syndrome.
(D) A point mutation in the *CFTR* gene on chromosome 7, leading to cystic fibrosis.
(E) A translocation involving chromosomes 9 and 22, leading to chronic myeloid leukemia (CML).

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Neonatal hypotonia and feeding difficulties:** Classic early signs of Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS).
*   **Developmental delay:** Common feature of PWS.
*   **Hyperphagia and obesity (starting around age 2):** Hallmark feature of PWS.
*   **Behavioral issues (tantrums, OCD):** Frequently observed in PWS.
*   **Short stature, small hands/feet, hypogonadism:** Characteristic physical features of PWS.
*   **Normal karyotype (46,XY):** Rules out aneuploidies like Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) or Klinefelter syndrome.
*   **FISH showing missing paternal chromosome 15 (15q11-q13):** Directly indicates a deletion of the paternal contribution in the critical region.
*   **Methylation analysis showing hypermethylation of the maternal chromosome 15 at the ICR1:** Confirms paternal imprinting on the maternal allele, which is normal. The *absence* of the paternal allele (due to deletion) means there are no normally expressed genes from that region.

These clues strongly point towards Prader-Willi syndrome caused by a deletion of the paternally inherited chromosome 15q11.2-q13 region.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS) is a classic example of genomic imprinting. The 15q11.2-q13 region contains several genes, including *SNRPN* (Small Nuclear Ribonucleoprotein N) and *NKX1* (NKX1 Homeobox), which are normally expressed *only* from the paternal chromosome. The maternal allele in this region is silenced through epigenetic modifications, primarily DNA methylation, at the Imprinting Control Region 1 (ICR1).

In this patient, FISH analysis confirms a deletion of the paternal chromosome 15 in the 15q11.2-q13 region. This means the patient lacks the normally expressed paternal copies of genes like *SNRPN* and *NKX1*. Although the maternal chromosome 15 is present and normally imprinted (hypermethylated at ICR1, silencing its genes), the absence of the paternal contribution leads to the PWS phenotype. The methylation analysis confirms the normal maternal imprinting pattern. The clinical features described (neonatal hypotonia, feeding difficulties, hyperphagia, obesity, developmental delay, hypogonadism, characteristic facial features) are all consistent with PWS.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 21 results in Down syndrome (Trisomy 21 phenotype). This patient has a normal karyotype and features consistent with PWS, not Down syndrome. UPD is a mechanism for PWS (maternal UPD of chromosome 15), but this option specifies chromosome 21.
- (B): Deletion of the *maternal* copy of the *UBE3A* gene on chromosome 15q11.2 leads to Angelman syndrome. Angelman syndrome presents with severe developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, happy demeanor, and characteristic facial features. The *UBE3A* gene is imprinted, expressed primarily from the maternal allele in the brain. The patient's symptoms (hyperphagia, obesity, hypotonia) and the specific genetic finding (paternal deletion) are inconsistent with Angelman syndrome.
- (D): A point mutation in the *CFTR* gene on chromosome 7 causes cystic fibrosis. Cystic fibrosis is an autosomal recessive disorder affecting chloride transport, leading to thick mucus production in the lungs, pancreas, and other organs. The clinical presentation (respiratory infections, pancreatic insufficiency, salty skin) is completely different from PWS.
- (E): A translocation involving chromosomes 9 and 22, specifically t(9;22), creates the Philadelphia chromosome and is characteristic of chronic myeloid leukemia (CML). CML is a myeloproliferative neoplasm. This option is unrelated to the patient's developmental and metabolic phenotype.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Genomic imprinting causes parent-of-origin specific gene expression. Deletion of the paternal 15q11.2-q13 region causes Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS), while deletion of the maternal 15q11.2-q13 region causes Angelman syndrome (AS). Both syndromes involve the same chromosomal region but have distinct clinical features due to the imprinted expression of different critical genes (*SNRPN/NKX1* for PWS, *UBE3A* for AS). Key differentials for PWS include Bardet-Biedl syndrome (obesity, hypogonadism, retinal dystrophy, polydactyly) and other causes of developmental delay and obesity.

<a id='question-142'></a>

### Question 142: Angelman Syndrome vs. Prader-Willi Syndrome - 15q11-q13 Imprinting Region
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of genomic imprinting, specifically the 15q11-q13 region, and the distinct clinical phenotypes resulting from defects in maternal versus paternal contributions of specific genes (UBE3A, SNRPN, MAGEL2). It requires differentiating between Angelman syndrome (AS) and Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS) based on clinical presentation and molecular mechanisms. (本题考察基因组印记，特别是15q11-q13区域，以及母系和父系贡献缺陷导致的不同临床表型（安吉尔曼综合征和普拉德-威利综合征）。需要根据临床表现和分子机制区分安吉尔曼综合征和普拉德-威利综合征。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old girl presents with severe developmental delay, ataxia, seizures (generalized tonic-clonic, occurring multiple times per week), and frequent episodes of inappropriate laughter ("happy puppet syndrome"). She has minimal speech development and exhibits microcephaly. Her parents report that she sleeps poorly and has a history of hypotonia in infancy. Her father has a history of obesity, hyperphagia, and mild intellectual disability, diagnosed in childhood. Karyotype analysis reveals a normal 46,XX chromosome complement. Methylation analysis of the 15q11-q13 region shows hypermethylation of the paternal allele and hypomethylation of the maternal allele.

(A) The patient's symptoms are most consistent with Prader-Willi syndrome due to maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 15.
(B) The patient's symptoms are most consistent with Angelman syndrome due to a deletion of the maternal 15q11-q13 region.
(C) The patient's symptoms are most consistent with Angelman syndrome due to paternal UPD of chromosome 15.
(D) The patient's symptoms are most consistent with Prader-Willi syndrome due to a deletion of the paternal 15q11-q13 region.
(E) The patient's symptoms are most consistent with Angelman syndrome due to hypermethylation of the maternal UBE3A allele.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old girl with severe developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, inappropriate laughter ("happy puppet"), minimal speech, microcephaly, poor sleep, history of infantile hypotonia. These features strongly suggest Angelman syndrome (AS).
- **Father's History:** Obesity, hyperphagia, mild intellectual disability. These features strongly suggest Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS).
- **Karyotype:** Normal 46,XX, ruling out large chromosomal abnormalities like trisomy 21 or monosomy 15.
- **Methylation Analysis:** Hypermethylation of the paternal allele and hypomethylation of the maternal allele at the 15q11-q13 region. This is the critical piece of information.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **15q11-q13 Imprinting Region:** This region on chromosome 15 exhibits genomic imprinting, meaning gene expression depends on the parent of origin.
- **Normal Imprinting:**
    - **Maternal Allele:** The maternal allele is normally hypomethylated (unmethylated) in the brain, allowing expression of *UBE3A* and *MAGEL2*. The maternal alleles of *SNRPN* and *MEST* are methylated (silenced).
    - **Paternal Allele:** The paternal allele is normally hypermethylated (methylated) in the brain, silencing the *UBE3A*, *MAGEL2*, *SNRPN*, and *MEST* genes.
- **Angelman Syndrome (AS):** Caused by loss of function of the *maternally* inherited *UBE3A* gene in the brain. This can occur via:
    - Deletion of the maternal 15q11-q13 region (~70% of cases).
    - Paternal Uniparental Disomy (UPD) of chromosome 15 (maternal chromosome 15 is missing, only paternal chromosome 15 is present; ~7% of cases). In this case, the patient inherits two copies of the paternal chromosome 15, both of which have methylated *UBE3A*, leading to its silencing.
    - Imprinting defect (failure to methylate the paternal allele, leading to silencing of the maternal allele; ~3% of cases).
    - Mutation in the maternal *UBE3A* gene (~11% of cases).
- **Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS):** Caused by loss of function of the *paternally* inherited genes in the 15q11-q13 region, primarily *SNRPN* and *MEST*. This can occur via:
    - Deletion of the paternal 15q11-q13 region (~70% of cases).
    - Maternal Uniparental Disomy (UPD) of chromosome 15 (paternal chromosome 15 is missing, only maternal chromosome 15 is present; ~25% of cases). In this case, the patient inherits two copies of the maternal chromosome 15, both of which have unmethylated *SNRPN* and *MEST*, but the paternal alleles are missing.
    - Imprinting defect (failure to methylate the paternal allele, leading to silencing of the maternal allele; ~5% of cases).
- **Connecting to the Vignette:**
    - The patient's clinical features (developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, inappropriate laughter, microcephaly) are classic for Angelman syndrome.
    - The father's clinical features (obesity, hyperphagia, intellectual disability) are classic for Prader-Willi syndrome.
    - The methylation analysis shows hypermethylation of the paternal allele and hypomethylation of the maternal allele. This pattern is normal.
    - However, the question stem implies a *pathological* mechanism causing the patient's AS. The methylation pattern described (paternal hypermethylation, maternal hypomethylation) is the *normal* pattern. The question is asking for the *cause* of the patient's AS, given this normal methylation pattern *in the context of her symptoms*.
    - The key is that AS requires loss of function of the *maternally* inherited *UBE3A*. The normal methylation pattern (paternal hypermethylation, maternal hypomethylation) means the maternal *UBE3A* is normally expressed. Therefore, the mechanism must be something *other* than a defect in the maternal allele itself or a maternal imprinting defect.
    - The only mechanism listed that fits AS and involves the 15q11-q13 region, given the normal methylation pattern, is paternal UPD. In paternal UPD, the patient inherits two copies of chromosome 15 from the father. Both paternal copies have methylated *UBE3A*, leading to its silencing in the brain, causing AS. The normal methylation pattern (paternal hypermethylation, maternal hypomethylation) is observed because the analysis is performed on the patient's cells, which contain one paternal and one paternal chromosome 15 (in the case of paternal UPD). The *UBE3A* gene on both chromosomes is silenced due to methylation inherited from the father.
    - The father's PWS is likely due to a deletion of the paternal 15q11-q13 region, which is the most common cause. This deletion would be present on the chromosome 15 he passed on to the patient, leading to paternal UPD if the mother did not contribute a chromosome 15.

##### 3. Synthesis
The patient presents with classic Angelman syndrome symptoms. The methylation analysis shows the normal pattern (paternal hypermethylation, maternal hypomethylation). This rules out imprinting defects and defects in the maternal allele itself (like a maternal deletion or mutation). The only remaining mechanism among the choices that causes AS and is consistent with a normal methylation pattern is paternal UPD, where the patient inherits two silenced paternal copies of UBE3A.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** Maternal UPD (15q11-q13) causes Prader-Willi syndrome, not Angelman syndrome. In maternal UPD, the patient inherits two copies of the maternal chromosome 15, both with unmethylated *SNRPN* and *MEST*, leading to their expression and causing PWS. The patient's symptoms are clearly AS.
- (B): **Incorrect.** A deletion of the maternal 15q11-q13 region is the most common cause of Angelman syndrome. However, this would typically be associated with a *loss of the maternal signal* on methylation analysis, not the normal pattern described (paternal hypermethylation, maternal hypomethylation). The vignette describes a normal methylation pattern, ruling out a maternal deletion or mutation as the cause in this specific case.
- (D): **Incorrect.** A deletion of the paternal 15q11-q13 region is the most common cause of Prader-Willi syndrome. This would cause the father's symptoms but not the patient's AS symptoms. Furthermore, the patient's methylation pattern is normal, which is not expected if the patient had a paternal deletion (which would lead to loss of the paternal methylation signal).
- (E): **Incorrect.** Hypermethylation of the maternal UBE3A allele would silence it, causing Angelman syndrome. However, this represents an imprinting defect, which would be detected by methylation analysis as *abnormal* methylation (e.g., hypermethylation of the maternal allele). The vignette explicitly states the methylation pattern is normal (paternal hypermethylation, maternal hypomethylation), ruling out an imprinting defect.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Understand the concept of genomic imprinting at the 15q11-q13 locus and how defects in either the maternal (causing AS) or paternal (causing PWS) contribution lead to distinct neurodevelopmental disorders. Recognize that different molecular mechanisms (deletion, UPD, imprinting defect, mutation) can cause each syndrome, and that methylation analysis is crucial for diagnosis, especially when the mechanism is not obvious from karyotype or clinical presentation alone.
- **Differentials:** Key differential is Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS). AS features include severe developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, inappropriate laughter, microcephaly. PWS features include neonatal hypotonia, hyperphagia leading to obesity, short stature, hypogonadism, mild-moderate intellectual disability. The 15q11-q13 region is involved in both. Methylation analysis is key to differentiating the underlying molecular cause.

<a id='question-143'></a>

### Question 143: Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS) Mechanisms: 70% Paternal 15q11-q13 Deletion, 25% Maternal Uniparental Disomy (UPD), 5% Imprinting Center Defect - Clinical Scenario 3
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Genomic imprinting and its disruption leading to Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS), specifically distinguishing between deletion, uniparental disomy (UPD), and imprinting defects. (基因组印记及其破坏导致Prader-Willi综合征，具体区分缺失、单亲二体和印记缺陷。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy is brought to the pediatrician by his parents due to concerns about excessive eating (hyperphagia) and developmental delay. He was born full-term after an uncomplicated pregnancy. Since infancy, he has exhibited hypotonia and feeding difficulties, requiring specialized formula and feeding support. Over the past year, his parents have noted an insatiable appetite, leading to significant weight gain and obesity. He also displays characteristic facial features, including almond-shaped eyes, a narrow forehead, and a thin upper lip. His developmental milestones are delayed; he is not yet speaking in sentences and has difficulty with fine motor skills. His older sister is developmentally normal. Karyotyping reveals a normal male karyotype (46,XY). Fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH) analysis using probes specific for the 15q11-q13 region is performed on the patient's peripheral blood lymphocytes. The FISH analysis shows that both copies of the paternal chromosome 15 contain the SNRPN gene locus, while both copies of the maternal chromosome 15 lack the SNRPN gene locus.

Which of the following genetic mechanisms is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Paternal deletion of the 15q11-q13 region.
(B) Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15.
(C) Imprinting center defect on the paternal chromosome 15.
(D) Deletion of the maternal 15q11-q13 region.
(E) Mosaicism for paternal deletion of the 15q11-q13 region.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with hyperphagia, developmental delay, hypotonia since infancy, feeding difficulties, characteristic facial features (almond eyes, narrow forehead, thin upper lip), obesity. These are classic symptoms of Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS).
- **Family History:** Older sister is normal, suggesting a de novo event or a mechanism not typically inherited in a Mendelian fashion (like imprinting disorders).
- **Karyotype:** Normal 46,XY rules out aneuploidy.
- **FISH Analysis:** This is the crucial piece of information. FISH probes for the 15q11-q13 region show:
    - Paternal chromosomes 15: Both copies contain the SNRPN gene locus.
    - Maternal chromosomes 15: Both copies lack the SNRPN gene locus.
- **Interpretation of FISH:** The SNRPN gene (Small Nuclear Ribonucleoprotein N) is located within the 15q11-q13 region and is normally expressed only from the *paternal* allele due to genomic imprinting. The FISH results indicate that the patient has two copies of the maternal chromosome 15 (lacking the paternally expressed SNRPN) and no copies of the paternal chromosome 15 (which would normally carry the expressed SNRPN). This pattern is characteristic of maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS):** Caused by the loss of function of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11-q13 region. Key genes include SNRPN, NKX1, and others.
- **Genomic Imprinting:** An epigenetic phenomenon where certain genes are expressed in a parent-of-origin-specific manner. In the 15q11-q13 region, most genes are normally expressed *only* from the paternal chromosome, while others are expressed *only* from the maternal chromosome.
- **Mechanisms of PWS:**
    1.  **Paternal Deletion (approx. 70%):** Deletion of the critical region on the paternal chromosome 15.
    2.  **Maternal Uniparental Disomy (UPD) (approx. 25%):** The individual inherits two copies of chromosome 15 from the mother and no copy from the father. Since the maternal copies lack the paternally expressed genes (like SNRPN), the individual effectively lacks these genes.
    3.  **Imprinting Center Defect (approx. 5%):** A defect in the imprinting control region (ICR) on the paternal chromosome 15, which prevents the proper silencing of the maternal allele, leading to loss of paternal expression.
- **FISH Interpretation:** FISH uses fluorescent probes that bind to specific DNA sequences. In this case, probes targeting the SNRPN locus (within 15q11-q13) are used. The finding that *both* paternal chromosomes show the signal (SNRPN present) and *both* maternal chromosomes lack the signal (SNRPN absent) means the patient has two maternal chromosome 15s and no paternal chromosome 15s. This is the definition of maternal UPD for chromosome 15.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Paternal deletion of the 15q11-q13 region:** This is the most common cause of PWS. However, FISH results would show the SNRPN locus present on both maternal chromosomes and absent on both paternal chromosomes. The vignette explicitly states the opposite FISH pattern (SNRPN present on both paternal, absent on both maternal). Therefore, this is incorrect.
- (C) **Imprinting center defect on the paternal chromosome 15:** This mechanism also leads to PWS because it prevents paternal expression of genes in the region. However, FISH analysis would typically show the SNRPN locus present on both paternal and both maternal chromosomes, but the paternal allele would be silenced (though standard FISH might not directly detect silencing, it wouldn't show the specific pattern described). The described FISH pattern (SNRPN present only on paternal chromosomes) is inconsistent with an imprinting defect. Therefore, this is incorrect.
- (D) **Deletion of the maternal 15q11-q13 region:** This would cause Angelman Syndrome (AS), not PWS. AS results from the loss of the maternally expressed UBE3A gene in this region. FISH would show UBE3A present on both paternal chromosomes and absent on both maternal chromosomes. The vignette describes the SNRPN pattern, and the clinical presentation is PWS, not AS. Therefore, this is incorrect.
- (E) **Mosaicism for paternal deletion of the 15q11-q13 region:** Mosaicism means some cells have the deletion and others do not. FISH analysis on peripheral blood lymphocytes might show a mixed pattern (some cells with SNRPN on both paternal, some with SNRPN absent on paternal), or it might show the pattern consistent with a complete deletion if the proportion of affected cells is high enough. However, the vignette describes a clear, consistent FISH pattern across all analyzed cells (both paternal SNRPN+, both maternal SNRPN-), which is characteristic of UPD, not mosaicism for deletion. Therefore, this is incorrect.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Prader-Willi Syndrome is caused by the lack of expression of paternally inherited genes in the 15q11-q13 region due to genomic imprinting. The three main mechanisms are paternal deletion, maternal uniparental disomy (UPD), and imprinting center defect. FISH analysis is crucial for diagnosis and differentiating these mechanisms. Maternal UPD (two maternal chromosomes 15, no paternal) results in the absence of the paternally expressed SNRPN gene, leading to PWS. Angelman Syndrome results from the loss of the maternally expressed UBE3A gene in the same region and presents differently (severe developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, happy demeanor). Understanding the specific FISH patterns is key to distinguishing between PWS and AS and identifying the underlying genetic mechanism for PWS.

<a id='question-144'></a>

### Question 144: Prader-Willi Syndrome - Clinical Phenotype & Genetic Mechanism
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS) is caused by the loss of function of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11.2-q13 region, typically due to deletion, maternal uniparental disomy (UPD), or imprinting defect. The clinical presentation includes neonatal hypotonia and feeding difficulties, followed by hyperphagia and obesity in childhood. (Prader-Willi 综合征 (PWS) 是由 15q11.2-q13 区域父本表达基因功能丧失引起的，通常是由于缺失、母本单亲二倍体 (UPD) 或印迹缺陷造成的。临床表现包括新生儿低张力肌和喂养困难，随后是儿童时期食欲亢进和肥胖。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-month-old male infant presents to the pediatrician with developmental delay, poor weight gain despite adequate caloric intake, and generalized hypotonia noted since birth. His parents report significant difficulty feeding him, requiring assistance with bottle feeding and frequent breaks due to fatigue. Physical examination reveals hypotonia, almond-shaped eyes, a narrow forehead, and small hands and feet. His developmental milestones are delayed; he does not roll over or sit unsupported. His parents are concerned about his lack of interest in food, which contrasts sharply with his older sibling who was a voracious eater. Laboratory tests show normal electrolytes, glucose, and thyroid function. Karyotype analysis reveals a normal male chromosome complement (46,XY). Further investigation including DNA methylation analysis of the 15q11.2-q13 region is ordered. Based on the clinical presentation and likely genetic findings, which of the following molecular mechanisms is most consistent with this patient's condition?

(A) A mutation in the *MC4R* gene leading to constitutive activation of the melanocortin pathway, resulting in decreased appetite.
(B) A deletion of the paternal chromosome 15q11.2-q13 region, leading to the absence of paternally expressed genes in this region.
(C) A translocation involving chromosome 21, resulting in trisomy 21 (Down syndrome) and associated hypotonia and developmental delay.
(D) A mutation in the *FBN1* gene causing Marfan syndrome, characterized by tall stature, long limbs, and cardiovascular abnormalities.
(E) Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15, resulting in the expression of only maternal alleles in the 15q11.2-q13 region.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Age and Sex:** 6-month-old male infant.
*   **Presenting Symptoms:** Developmental delay, poor weight gain, generalized hypotonia since birth, difficulty feeding (requires assistance, fatigue).
*   **Physical Exam Findings:** Hypotonia, almond-shaped eyes, narrow forehead, small hands and feet.
*   **Developmental Milestones:** Delayed (cannot roll over or sit unsupported).
*   **Feeding History Contrast:** Parents note lack of interest in food, contrasting with a sibling who was a voracious eater (foreshadowing the later hyperphagia phase).
*   **Initial Labs:** Normal electrolytes, glucose, thyroid function (rules out common metabolic/endocrine causes of hypotonia/poor feeding).
*   **Karyotype:** Normal 46,XY (rules out aneuploidies like Trisomy 21, which can cause hypotonia).
*   **Further Investigation:** DNA methylation analysis of 15q11.2-q13 region ordered (suggests suspicion for an imprinting disorder).
*   **Overall Picture:** Neonatal hypotonia, feeding difficulties, developmental delay, characteristic facial features, and the *implication* of later hyperphagia (based on sibling contrast and typical PWS progression) strongly point towards Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS).

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS) is a genetic disorder caused by the loss of function of specific genes located in the 15q11.2-q13 region on the paternal chromosome 15. This region is subject to genomic imprinting, meaning that certain genes are expressed only from the paternal allele, while others are expressed only from the maternal allele. The critical genes for PWS are paternally expressed. The typical mechanisms leading to PWS are:
1.  **Deletion:** Approximately 70% of cases involve a deletion of the paternal 15q11.2-q13 region.
2.  **Maternal Uniparental Disomy (UPD):** About 25% of cases result from inheriting two copies of chromosome 15 from the mother and none from the father (15q11.2-q13 UPD). In this scenario, the individual only has maternal alleles in this region, which lack the paternally expressed genes required for normal development.
3.  **Imprinting Defect:** A small percentage (<5%) of cases are caused by a defect in the imprinting center on the paternal chromosome 15, leading to silencing of the paternal alleles in the critical region.

The clinical phenotype of PWS is characterized by distinct phases:
*   **Infancy:** Severe hypotonia, poor suck, feeding difficulties, failure to thrive, developmental delay.
*   **Childhood:** Development of hyperphagia (insatiable appetite) typically starting between ages 1-6 years, leading to morbid obesity if uncontrolled. Other features include short stature, hypogonadism (small genitalia, delayed/incomplete puberty), characteristic facial features (almond-shaped eyes, narrow forehead, thin upper lip), small hands and feet, intellectual disability, and behavioral problems.

The question asks for the molecular mechanism consistent with the patient's condition. The clinical presentation (neonatal hypotonia, feeding difficulties, developmental delay, characteristic features) is classic for PWS. The normal karyotype rules out common chromosomal abnormalities like Down syndrome. The ordered DNA methylation analysis specifically targets the 15q11.2-q13 region, further supporting the suspicion of PWS, which is an imprinting disorder. Option (B) describes the most common genetic cause of PWS – a deletion of the paternal chromosome 15q11.2-q13 region. This deletion leads to the absence of the necessary paternally expressed genes, causing the characteristic phenotype.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): *MC4R* (Melanocortin 4 Receptor) gene mutations are a common cause of *monogenic obesity*, characterized primarily by hyperphagia and obesity, often starting in childhood. However, *MC4R* mutations do *not* cause the neonatal hypotonia, feeding difficulties, developmental delay, or characteristic facial features seen in PWS. Furthermore, *MC4R* mutations typically lead to *increased* appetite due to constitutive activation of the melanocortin pathway (loss-of-function mutations in *MC4R* lead to decreased satiety signaling). The vignette describes *poor* feeding initially, not increased appetite. This option confuses a cause of obesity with the full PWS phenotype and misrepresents the effect of *MC4R* mutations.
- (C): A translocation involving chromosome 21 typically leads to Trisomy 21 (Down syndrome). While Down syndrome can cause hypotonia and developmental delay, it is associated with distinct facial features (upslanting palpebral fissures, epicanthal folds, flat facial profile, protruding tongue), congenital heart defects, and other systemic abnormalities not described in the vignette (e.g., no mention of characteristic PWS facial features like almond eyes or small hands/feet). The normal karyotype also rules out Trisomy 21.
- (D): *FBN1* (Fibrillin-1) gene mutations cause Marfan syndrome. Marfan syndrome is a connective tissue disorder characterized by tall stature, long limbs (dolichostenomelia), arachnodactyly, joint hypermobility, lens dislocation, and cardiovascular abnormalities (aortic root dilation/dissection). It does not cause hypotonia, feeding difficulties, developmental delay, or the specific facial features described in the vignette.
- (E): Maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15 *is* a cause of PWS (approximately 25% of cases). However, option (B) describes the *most common* cause (deletion, ~70%). While UPD is a valid mechanism for PWS, the question asks for the mechanism *most consistent* with the patient's condition, and statistically, deletion is more likely. Furthermore, the question stem doesn't provide specific information pointing towards UPD over deletion (e.g., family history suggestive of UPD). Option (B) represents the most frequent underlying genetic event leading to the described clinical picture.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Prader-Willi syndrome is a classic example of an imprinting disorder caused by the loss of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11.2-q13 region. Key features include neonatal hypotonia/feeding issues followed by childhood hyperphagia/obesity. Differential diagnoses for neonatal hypotonia include spinal muscular atrophy (SMA), congenital myopathies, metabolic disorders, and chromosomal abnormalities (e.g., Down syndrome). Differential diagnoses for hyperphagia/obesity include Bardet-Biedl syndrome, *MC4R* mutations, and hypothalamic lesions. The combination of neonatal hypotonia *and* later hyperphagia is highly suggestive of PWS.

### Question 145: Angelman Syndrome Mechanisms
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the genetic and epigenetic mechanisms underlying Angelman syndrome (AS), specifically imprinting and its role in the differential expression of the *UBE3A* gene based on parental origin. (理解安吉尔曼综合征 (AS) 的遗传和表观遗传机制，特别是印记及其在 *UBE3A* 基因基于亲本来源的差异表达中的作用。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old girl presents with severe developmental delay, ataxia, seizures (primarily tonic-clonic), and frequent episodes of inappropriate laughter ("happy puppet syndrome"). She has minimal speech and exhibits microcephaly. Her parents report that she was born full-term without complications. Her father has a history of epilepsy, but her mother is healthy. A maternal uncle also exhibits similar developmental and behavioral features. Karyotyping reveals a normal female karyotype (46,XX). Fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH) analysis using a probe specific for the 15q11-q13 region shows a deletion on the maternal chromosome 15. Methylation analysis of the *UBE3A* gene reveals hypermethylation of the maternal allele and hypomethylation of the paternal allele.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Paternal uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 15, resulting in two copies of the paternally inherited *UBE3A* gene, which is normally silenced by methylation.
(B) A point mutation in the paternal *UBE3A* gene that introduces a premature stop codon, leading to a non-functional protein.
(C) A deletion of the paternal 15q11-q13 region, removing the normally expressed *UBE3A* allele.
(D) A deletion of the maternal 15q11-q13 region, removing the normally expressed *UBE3A* allele, coupled with methylation-specific silencing of the paternal *UBE3A* allele.
(E) A translocation involving chromosome 15 and another autosome, leading to loss of the maternal *UBE3A* allele and disruption of the paternal allele's expression.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Clinical Presentation:** Severe developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, inappropriate laughter ("happy puppet"), minimal speech, microcephaly are classic features of Angelman syndrome (AS).
- **Family History:** Affected maternal uncle suggests a possible inherited component, potentially related to the maternal lineage or germline mosaicism. Father's epilepsy is less specific but could indicate a predisposition to neurological disorders.
- **Karyotype:** Normal 46,XX rules out aneuploidy like Down syndrome or Turner syndrome.
- **FISH Analysis:** Deletion in the 15q11-q13 region on the maternal chromosome 15 is a key finding. This region contains the *UBE3A* gene.
- **Methylation Analysis:** Hypermethylation of the maternal *UBE3A* allele and hypomethylation of the paternal *UBE3A* allele are crucial. This pattern is characteristic of genomic imprinting in this region.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Angelman Syndrome Pathophysiology:** AS is caused by the loss of function of the maternally inherited *UBE3A* gene. *UBE3A* encodes an E3 ubiquitin ligase involved in protein degradation.
- **Genomic Imprinting:** The 15q11-q13 region is subject to genomic imprinting. This means that gene expression depends on the parent of origin. In this region, the maternal allele is normally expressed, while the paternal allele is silenced by DNA methylation in most brain neurons.
- **Molecular Mechanisms of AS:**
    - **Deletion (70%):** Deletion of the maternal 15q11-q13 region, as found in this patient by FISH, removes the normally expressed *UBE3A* allele.
    - **Paternal UPD (5%):** Paternal uniparental disomy means the individual inherits two copies of chromosome 15 from the father and none from the mother. Since the paternal *UBE3A* allele is normally silenced by methylation, this results in a lack of functional *UBE3A* protein.
    - **UBE3A Mutation (10%):** A mutation (e.g., nonsense, frameshift, splice site) in the maternal *UBE3A* allele renders it non-functional.
    - **Imprinting Defect (3%):** A defect in the imprinting center (IC1) on the maternal chromosome 15 leads to methylation and silencing of the maternal *UBE3A* allele, similar to the paternal allele.
    - **Unknown (2%):** The cause is not identified in a small percentage of cases.
- **Patient's Case:** The patient has a maternal deletion of 15q11-q13 (removing the normally expressed maternal *UBE3A*) and methylation analysis confirms the typical imprinting pattern (maternal *UBE3A* hypermethylated/silenced, paternal *UBE3A* hypomethylated/expressed). The combination of the maternal deletion and the methylation pattern explains the loss of *UBE3A* function. The paternal allele is hypomethylated and potentially expressed, but it is insufficient to compensate for the loss of the maternal allele in the brain, or it may also be subject to neuron-specific silencing mechanisms.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Paternal UPD (15q11-q13) would result in two copies of the *paternally* inherited *UBE3A* gene. Since the paternal allele is normally silenced by methylation in neurons, this leads to AS. However, the FISH analysis in the vignette specifically shows a *maternal* deletion, not paternal UPD. This option describes a different mechanism for AS.
- (B): A point mutation in the *paternal* *UBE3A* gene would not cause AS. The paternal allele is normally silenced by methylation in neurons. A mutation in the *maternal* *UBE3A* gene could cause AS, but the vignette points to a deletion, not a point mutation, and the methylation pattern is consistent with imprinting, not a mutation affecting the paternal allele.
- (C): A deletion of the *paternal* 15q11-q13 region would remove the normally silenced *UBE3A* allele. This would not cause AS because the functional maternal *UBE3A* allele would still be expressed. This option incorrectly identifies the chromosome involved in the deletion.
- (E): A translocation involving chromosome 15 could lead to loss of the maternal *UBE3A* allele (if the translocation disrupts the maternal chromosome 15). However, the vignette specifically identifies a *deletion* via FISH, not a translocation. Also, the description implies the paternal allele is disrupted, but the key issue in AS is the loss of the *maternally expressed* allele, which is normally active. While a translocation *could* theoretically cause AS, the specific findings (maternal deletion, imprinting pattern) point directly to option D.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Angelman syndrome results from the loss of function of the maternally inherited *UBE3A* gene due to genomic imprinting. The most common mechanism is a maternal deletion of 15q11-q13. Other mechanisms include paternal UPD, maternal *UBE3A* mutation, and imprinting defects. Understanding genomic imprinting and its role in specific syndromes like AS and Prader-Willi syndrome is crucial. Key differentials include Prader-Willi syndrome (paternal 15q11-q13 deletion/UPD/mutation, paternal *SNRPN* silencing), Rett syndrome (MECP2 mutation), and other developmental delay syndromes.
