<a id='question-146'></a>

### Question 146: Angelman Syndrome - Clinical Phenotype and Molecular Mechanism
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the clinical features of Angelman syndrome (AS) and the molecular mechanism underlying its most common cause, specifically the role of maternal deletion of the *UBE3A* gene on chromosome 15q11-q13 and the concept of genomic imprinting. (安吉尔曼综合征的临床特征和分子机制，特别是15号染色体q11-q13区域*UBE3A*基因的母系缺失以及基因组印记的概念。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old girl is brought to the pediatrician by her parents due to significant developmental delay. She has severe intellectual disability, is unable to speak, and exhibits frequent, unprovoked episodes of inappropriate laughter, often described as "happy puppet syndrome." Her gait is described as jerky and ataxic, resembling that of a puppet. Physical examination reveals microcephaly and seizures. Her parents report no significant family history of similar conditions. Karyotyping reveals a normal female karyotype (46,XX). Fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH) analysis using a probe specific for the 15q11-q13 region shows that the maternal allele is absent, while the paternal allele is present. Methylation analysis of the *UBE3A* gene promoter region indicates hypermethylation on the paternal allele and hypomethylation on the maternal allele (which is absent).

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the patient's clinical presentation?

(A) Paternal deletion of the *UBE3A* gene leading to loss of function of the ubiquitination enzyme required for protein degradation.
(B) Maternal deletion of the *UBE3A* gene, coupled with genomic imprinting resulting in the exclusive expression of the paternal *UBE3A* allele in the brain.
(C) A mutation in the *UBE3A* gene on the paternal chromosome that results in a non-functional protein, combined with methylation silencing of the maternal allele.
(D) Uniparental disomy (UPD) of chromosome 15, where the patient inherited two paternal copies of chromosome 15, leading to the absence of the maternal *UBE3A* allele and its silencing.
(E) A mutation in the *SNRPN* gene located within the 15q11-q13 region, which disrupts the function of the small nuclear ribonucleoprotein complex involved in splicing.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old girl with severe intellectual disability, absent speech, inappropriate laughter ("happy puppet"), ataxic gait ("puppet-like"), microcephaly, and seizures. This constellation of symptoms is highly characteristic of Angelman syndrome (AS).
- **Karyotype:** Normal (46,XX), ruling out aneuploidy.
- **FISH Analysis:** Absence of the maternal allele for the 15q11-q13 region, while the paternal allele is present. This indicates a maternal deletion within this specific region.
- **Methylation Analysis:** Hypermethylation of the *UBE3A* promoter on the paternal allele and hypomethylation on the maternal allele (which is absent). This pattern is consistent with genomic imprinting. In normal individuals, the maternal *UBE3A* allele is hypomethylated and expressed primarily in the brain, while the paternal allele is hypermethylated and silenced.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the clinical presentation.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Angelman Syndrome (AS):** A neurodevelopmental disorder characterized by severe intellectual disability, developmental delay, speech impairment, movement/balance disorder (ataxia, jerky movements), and a unique behavioral phenotype (frequent laughter/smiling, happy demeanor, easily excitable).
- **Genetic Basis:** AS is most commonly caused by the loss of function of the maternally inherited *UBE3A* gene located in the 15q11-q13 region. This region is subject to genomic imprinting.
- **Genomic Imprinting:** An epigenetic phenomenon where certain genes are expressed in a parent-of-origin-specific manner. In the 15q11-q13 region, the maternal allele of *UBE3A* is normally expressed in the brain due to hypomethylation of its promoter, while the paternal allele is silenced by hypermethylation.
- **UBE3A Protein:** An E3 ubiquitin ligase involved in protein degradation via the ubiquitin-proteasome system. Its precise function in the brain is not fully understood, but its loss leads to the AS phenotype.
- **Molecular Mechanisms of AS:**
    - **Maternal Deletion (most common, ~70%):** Deletion of the 15q11-q13 region on the maternal chromosome 15. This removes the normally expressed maternal *UBE3A* allele. The paternal allele remains but is silenced by imprinting (hypermethylation).
    - **Paternal Deletion (~5%):** Deletion of the same region on the paternal chromosome 15. This does not cause AS because the maternal *UBE3A* allele is normally expressed. This deletion causes Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS).
    - **Maternal Uniparental Disomy (UPD) (~3-7%):** The individual inherits two copies of chromosome 15 from the mother and none from the father. Both maternal copies of *UBE3A* are normally expressed, but the paternal copy is silenced. However, the imprinting center on the maternal chromosomes is also inherited, leading to silencing of the maternal *UBE3A* allele.
    - **Imprinting Defect (~3-7%):** A defect in the imprinting center on the maternal chromosome 15 leads to hypermethylation and silencing of the maternal *UBE3A* allele, similar to the paternal situation.
    - **UBE3A Mutation (~10%):** A mutation within the *UBE3A* gene itself on the maternal chromosome that results in a non-functional protein.
- **Applying to the Vignette:** The patient has a maternal deletion of the 15q11-q13 region (FISH) and the characteristic methylation pattern of imprinting (maternal allele absent/hypomethylated, paternal allele present/hypermethylated). This means the normally expressed maternal *UBE3A* allele is missing, and the paternal allele is silenced due to imprinting. Therefore, there is no functional UBE3A protein expressed in the brain, leading to the Angelman syndrome phenotype. Option (B) accurately describes this mechanism.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** This describes the mechanism of Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS), not Angelman syndrome. PWS is caused by the loss of function of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11-q13 region (e.g., *SNRPN*), often due to paternal deletion or maternal UPD. The vignette clearly points to a maternal deletion and the loss of the maternal *UBE3A* allele, which is the hallmark of AS.
- (C): **Incorrect.** This describes a potential cause of Angelman syndrome (a mutation in the maternal *UBE3A* gene), but the vignette specifically indicates a *deletion* of the maternal 15q11-q13 region via FISH, not a point mutation within the *UBE3A* gene. While a *UBE3A* mutation can cause AS, it's not the mechanism supported by the FISH data in this case.
- (D): **Incorrect.** This describes maternal uniparental disomy (UPD) for chromosome 15, which *can* cause AS. However, the vignette explicitly states that FISH analysis shows the *maternal allele is absent*. In maternal UPD, both maternal alleles are present (though the *UBE3A* allele is often silenced due to imprinting defects), and the paternal allele is absent. The FISH result directly contradicts maternal UPD.
- (E): **Incorrect.** This describes a potential cause of Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS), not Angelman syndrome. Mutations in the *SNRPN* gene, located in the 15q11-q13 region, are associated with PWS when the paternal allele is affected. The clinical presentation and the specific genetic finding (maternal deletion affecting *UBE3A*) point strongly to AS.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Angelman syndrome is caused by the loss of function of the maternally expressed *UBE3A* gene due to genomic imprinting. The most common cause is a maternal deletion of the 15q11-q13 region. Key clinical features include severe intellectual disability, ataxia, inappropriate laughter, and absent speech. Differentiate AS from Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS), which is caused by the loss of function of paternally expressed genes in the same region (e.g., *SNRPN*) and presents with hypotonia, hyperphagia, and obesity. Remember the concept of genomic imprinting and parent-of-origin effects.

<a id='question-147'></a>

### Question 147: Uniparental Disomy (UPD)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the clinical consequences of uniparental disomy (UPD), specifically imprinting and the resulting phenotypic expression, including Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS) and Angelman syndrome (AS). (理解单亲二体 (UPD) 的临床后果，特别是印记效应和由此产生的表型表达，包括 Prader-Willi 综合征 (PWS) 和 Angelman 综合征 (AS))

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to his pediatrician with developmental delay, hypotonia, and hyperphagia leading to severe obesity. His parents report that he has difficulty feeding as an infant but now exhibits an insatiable appetite. Physical examination reveals short stature, small hands and feet, and characteristic facial features including almond-shaped eyes and a narrow forehead. His older sister is developmentally normal. The parents are of average height and weight, with no significant medical history. Karyotyping reveals a normal 46,XY chromosome complement. However, methylation analysis of the SNRPN gene region on chromosome 15q11-q13 shows that all copies of the SNRPN gene are unmethylated.

(A) A deletion of the paternal 15q11-q13 region.
(B) A deletion of the maternal 15q11-q13 region.
(C) A point mutation in the UBE3A gene on the maternal chromosome 15.
(D) A point mutation in the SNRPN gene on the paternal chromosome 15.
(E) Uniparental disomy for chromosome 21.

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with developmental delay, hypotonia, hyperphagia, obesity, short stature, small hands/feet, characteristic facial features. This constellation of symptoms strongly suggests Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS).
- **Family History:** Older sister is normal, parents are unaffected. This suggests a *de novo* event or an inheritance pattern not typical of autosomal dominant/recessive disorders.
- **Karyotype:** Normal 46,XY rules out large chromosomal abnormalities like trisomy or large deletions/duplications detectable by standard karyotyping.
- **Methylation Analysis:** Unmethylated SNRPN gene copies. The SNRPN gene is located in the 15q11-q13 region. In normal individuals, the paternal copy of this region is methylated, while the maternal copy is unmethylated. The SNRPN gene itself is paternally expressed. The *UBE3A* gene, also in this region, is maternally expressed and normally methylated on both alleles.
- **Diagnosis Deduction:** The clinical presentation (PWS) combined with the methylation pattern (unmethylated SNRPN copies) indicates a loss of function of the *paternally* expressed genes in the 15q11-q13 region. Since the karyotype is normal, a simple deletion is less likely, but a mechanism leading to the loss of paternal contribution and retention of maternal contribution is suspected. The unmethylated state of *all* SNRPN copies points towards the absence of the normally methylated paternal contribution.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Prader-Willi Syndrome (PWS):** Caused by the loss of function of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11-q13 region. The key genes involved are *SNRPN* and *NDN* (Neuropeptide N).
- **Angelman Syndrome (AS):** Caused by the loss of function of the *maternally* expressed *UBE3A* gene in the same 15q11-q13 region.
- **Genomic Imprinting:** A phenomenon where certain genes are expressed in a parent-of-origin-specific manner. This is established during gametogenesis through epigenetic modifications, primarily DNA methylation.
- **15q11-q13 Region:** Contains several imprinted genes. The paternal chromosome 15 normally carries methylated copies of *SNRPN*, *NDN*, and *UBE3A* (though *UBE3A* is silenced). The maternal chromosome 15 normally carries unmethylated copies of these genes, with *UBE3A* being expressed and *SNRPN/NDN* being silenced.
- **Mechanism in this Case:** The patient has PWS symptoms and unmethylated SNRPN copies. This means the paternal contribution of the 15q11-q13 region is missing or non-functional. The normal methylation pattern is paternal methylation (methylated SNRPN) and maternal unmethylation (unmethylated SNRPN). The finding of *all* copies being unmethylated indicates that the patient inherited two copies of chromosome 15 from his mother and zero from his father (UPD 15, maternal). This results in the loss of the paternally expressed *SNRPN* and *NDN* genes, causing PWS.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **A deletion of the paternal 15q11-q13 region.** This is a common cause of PWS (~25% of cases). It would lead to the loss of the paternally expressed genes. However, methylation analysis in a simple paternal deletion would typically show *only* the maternal, unmethylated copies of SNRPN. The vignette states *all* copies are unmethylated, which is inconsistent with a simple paternal deletion where the maternal allele remains unmethylated. While UPD can *result* from an error in meiosis leading to a deletion of one chromosome followed by duplication of the other, the direct finding of unmethylated SNRPN on *all* copies points more specifically to the absence of the paternal contribution altogether, characteristic of UPD. UPD is a more specific explanation for the methylation finding described.
- (B): **A deletion of the maternal 15q11-q13 region.** This would cause Angelman syndrome (AS), characterized by developmental delay, ataxia, seizures, and happy demeanor, not the PWS phenotype described (hypotonia, hyperphagia, obesity). Methylation analysis would show methylated SNRPN copies (from the paternal chromosome).
- (C): **A point mutation in the UBE3A gene on the maternal chromosome 15.** This would also cause Angelman syndrome (AS), as it disrupts the function of the maternally expressed *UBE3A* gene. The clinical presentation is inconsistent with AS, and methylation analysis would likely show methylation patterns consistent with a normal maternal allele (unmethylated SNRPN).
- (D): **A point mutation in the SNRPN gene on the paternal chromosome 15.** While a mutation in *SNRPN* could cause PWS, the key finding here is the methylation pattern. A point mutation wouldn't necessarily alter the methylation status of the SNRPN promoter. Furthermore, the finding of *all* copies being unmethylated strongly suggests a loss of the *entire* paternal contribution (UPD) rather than a specific mutation within a gene on the paternal chromosome.
- (E): **Uniparental disomy for chromosome 21.** This refers to UPD for Down syndrome (Trisomy 21). It would not explain the specific symptoms of PWS or the methylation pattern of the SNRPN gene on chromosome 15.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Understand that Prader-Willi syndrome (PWS) results from the loss of paternally expressed genes in the 15q11-q13 region, while Angelman syndrome (AS) results from the loss of the maternally expressed *UBE3A* gene in the same region. Recognize that genomic imprinting and uniparental disomy (UPD) are key mechanisms underlying these disorders. Methylation analysis is crucial for diagnosis.
- **Differentials:** PWS vs. AS (distinguished by clinical features and methylation patterns). Other causes of developmental delay and hypotonia. Other genetic causes of obesity.

<a id='question-148'></a>

### Question 148: Heterodisomy vs Isodisomy
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating between heterodisomy (resulting from nondisjunction in meiosis I, leading to chromosomes with different parental origins) and isodisomy (resulting from nondisjunction in meiosis II or post-zygotic mitosis, leading to chromosomes with identical parental origins) based on genetic marker analysis.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old girl presents with developmental delay, hypotonia, and characteristic facial features including hypertelorism, epicanthal folds, and a flat nasal bridge. Her parents are unaffected, but her mother is 38 years old at the time of birth. Karyotyping reveals a 46,XX karyotype with an extra copy of chromosome 21, consistent with Down syndrome (Trisomy 21). To determine the mechanism of trisomy, high-resolution single nucleotide polymorphism (SNP) analysis is performed on the patient's chromosomes 21. The analysis reveals that one chromosome 21 is entirely maternal in origin, while the other chromosome 21 is entirely paternal in origin. The patient's mother has a normal karyotype (46,XX). The father has a normal karyotype (46,XY).

Which of the following mechanisms is the most likely cause of this patient's trisomy 21?

(A) Nondisjunction during maternal meiosis II.
(B) Nondisjunction during paternal meiosis I.
(C) Nondisjunction during maternal meiosis I.
(D) Nondisjunction during paternal meiosis II.
(E) Post-zygotic mitosis error.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old girl with developmental delay, hypotonia, and facial features characteristic of Down syndrome (Trisomy 21). This establishes the diagnosis of Trisomy 21.
- **Parental Information:** Mother is 38 years old (advanced maternal age, increased risk for nondisjunction). Both parents have normal karyotypes (46,XX and 46,XY). This rules out balanced translocations or inversions in the parents as the primary cause of the trisomy.
- **Karyotype:** 46,XX with an extra chromosome 21 (Trisomy 21). Confirms the diagnosis.
- **SNP Analysis:** Crucial finding. Shows that one chromosome 21 is entirely maternal and the other is entirely paternal. This indicates that the extra chromosome 21 originated from a single gamete (either sperm or egg) that contained two copies of chromosome 21.
- **Question:** Asks for the mechanism causing this specific type of trisomy 21, where the extra chromosome is derived from a single gamete containing two chromosomes of the same type, but with different parental origins.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Trisomy 21 Mechanisms:** Trisomy 21 can arise from several mechanisms:
    - **Meiosis I Nondisjunction (Maternal or Paternal):** Homologous chromosomes fail to separate during meiosis I. This results in a gamete (egg or sperm) with two copies of chromosome 21 (n+1). If this gamete fuses with a normal gamete (n), the resulting zygote has trisomy 21 (2n+1). Since the two chromosome 21s in the gamete came from different homologous chromosomes (one maternal, one paternal), the resulting trisomy 21 is called **heterodisomy**. This is the most common cause (~90%) of Trisomy 21.
    - **Meiosis II Nondisjunction (Maternal or Paternal):** Sister chromatids fail to separate during meiosis II. This results in a gamete with two copies of chromosome 21 (n+1). If this gamete fuses with a normal gamete (n), the resulting zygote has trisomy 21 (2n+1). Since the two chromosome 21s in the gamete are sister chromatids (identical copies derived from the same parent), the resulting trisomy 21 is called **isodisomy**. This accounts for a small percentage (~4%) of Trisomy 21 cases.
    - **Post-zygotic Mitosis Error:** Nondisjunction occurs during mitosis after fertilization. This leads to mosaicism, where some cells have the normal chromosome number (46,XX) and others have trisomy 21 (47,XX,+21). This is less common.
- **SNP Analysis Interpretation:** The finding that one chromosome 21 is entirely maternal and the other entirely paternal definitively indicates **heterodisomy**. This means the extra chromosome 21 came from a single gamete that contained one maternal chromosome 21 and one paternal chromosome 21.
- **Linking Heterodisomy to Meiosis I:** Heterodisomy results from the failure of homologous chromosomes to separate during **meiosis I**.
- **Evaluating the Options:**
    - (A) Nondisjunction during maternal meiosis II leads to isodisomy (both copies maternal). Incorrect.
    - (B) Nondisjunction during paternal meiosis I leads to heterodisomy (one copy maternal, one copy paternal). This is a possible cause of heterodisomy.
    - (C) Nondisjunction during maternal meiosis I leads to heterodisomy (one copy maternal, one copy paternal). This is a possible cause of heterodisomy.
    - (D) Nondisjunction during paternal meiosis II leads to isodisomy (both copies paternal). Incorrect.
    - (E) Post-zygotic mitosis error leads to mosaicism, not the consistent heterodisomy described. Incorrect.
- **Distinguishing Meiosis I vs Meiosis II:** Meiosis I involves separation of homologous chromosomes (one maternal, one paternal). Meiosis II involves separation of sister chromatids (identical copies). Therefore, nondisjunction in Meiosis I leads to a gamete with two different chromosomes (heterodisomy), while nondisjunction in Meiosis II leads to a gamete with two identical chromosomes (isodisomy).
- **Maternal vs Paternal Origin:** The SNP analysis shows one chromosome is entirely maternal and the other entirely paternal. This confirms heterodisomy. The question asks for the *mechanism*, which could be maternal or paternal meiosis I nondisjunction. However, maternal meiosis I nondisjunction is significantly more common (~90% of Trisomy 21 cases) than paternal meiosis I nondisjunction. Given the mother's advanced age (a known risk factor for maternal meiosis I nondisjunction), this is the most probable cause.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Nondisjunction during maternal meiosis II: This would result in a gamete with two identical maternal copies of chromosome 21 (isodisomy). The SNP analysis shows one maternal and one paternal copy, ruling out isodisomy. Therefore, this option is incorrect.
- (B) Nondisjunction during paternal meiosis I: This would also result in a gamete with two different copies of chromosome 21 (one paternal, one maternal - heterodisomy). While this mechanism *could* produce the observed SNP pattern, maternal meiosis I nondisjunction is far more common, especially given the mother's age. This option is less likely than (C).
- (D) Nondisjunction during paternal meiosis II: This would result in a gamete with two identical paternal copies of chromosome 21 (isodisomy). The SNP analysis shows one maternal and one paternal copy, ruling out isodisomy. Therefore, this option is incorrect.
- (E) Post-zygotic mitosis error: This results in mosaicism, where some cells have trisomy 21 and others are normal. The karyotype analysis (46,XX with an extra 21) and SNP analysis imply a consistent trisomy in all cells analyzed, not mosaicism. Therefore, this option is incorrect.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Understand the difference between heterodisomy (Meiosis I nondisjunction, different parental origins) and isodisomy (Meiosis II nondisjunction or post-zygotic mitosis, identical parental origins) based on genetic marker analysis (like SNP arrays). Recognize that maternal meiosis I nondisjunction is the most common cause of Trisomy 21, especially in older mothers.
- **Differentials:** Distinguish Trisomy 21 caused by Meiosis I nondisjunction (heterodisomy) from Meiosis II nondisjunction (isodisomy) and post-zygotic errors (mosaicism) using molecular genetic techniques. Consider other causes of developmental delay and dysmorphic features, but the specific SNP finding points directly to the mechanism of trisomy 21.

<a id='question-149'></a>

### Question 149: Trinucleotide Repeat Expansion: Dynamic Unstable Repeat Expansions Amplifying in Meiosis Causing Disease Exacerbation - Clinical Scenario 3
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Dynamic mutation (dynamic unstable repeat expansion) in trinucleotide repeat disorders, specifically Huntington's disease, leading to anticipation (earlier onset and/or increased severity in successive generations) due to preferential expansion during gametogenesis. (动态突变/动态不稳定重复序列扩增，在三核苷酸重复疾病中，特别是亨廷顿病，导致预期现象（代际发病年龄提前和/或严重程度增加），这是由于减数分裂期间重复序列的优先扩增。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 42-year-old male presents to a neurology clinic complaining of progressive choreiform movements, cognitive decline, and mood changes over the past 5 years. His father developed similar symptoms at age 35 and died at age 50 from complications of the disease. The patient's paternal grandfather was reportedly affected, exhibiting symptoms in his late 50s. Genetic testing reveals an expanded CAG repeat in the *HTT* gene. The patient's allele has 45 CAG repeats. His father's DNA sample (obtained post-mortem) showed 40 CAG repeats. The patient's daughter, age 12, is asymptomatic but underwent predictive genetic testing, which revealed 38 CAG repeats on the affected allele inherited from her father. The patient's unaffected mother has 15 CAG repeats on both alleles. The patient's unaffected brother has 20 CAG repeats on both alleles.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the observed pattern of disease onset and repeat expansion across these three generations?

(A) Genomic imprinting, where the expression of the *HTT* gene is dependent on the parent of origin, leading to variable penetrance and age of onset.
(B) Meiotic recombination hotspots near the *HTT* gene, causing increased frequency of crossing over and subsequent gene conversion events that alter the CAG repeat number.
(C) DNA methylation patterns at the *HTT* locus, which are unstable and change with each generation, influencing the stability of the CAG repeat.
(D) Slippage of the DNA polymerase during replication in somatic cells, leading to expansion or contraction of the CAG repeat, primarily affecting the severity of symptoms within an individual's lifetime.
(E) Preferential expansion of the CAG repeat during meiosis, particularly in the paternal germline, resulting in anticipation (earlier onset and/or increased severity) in subsequent generations.

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 42-year-old male with chorea, cognitive decline, mood changes – classic symptoms of Huntington's disease (HD).
*   **Family History:** Father affected (onset age 35), paternal grandfather affected (onset age late 50s). This suggests an autosomal dominant inheritance pattern.
*   **Genetic Testing:** Expanded CAG repeat in *HTT* gene. Patient has 45 repeats, father had 40 repeats, daughter has 38 repeats (on the affected allele).
*   **Repeat Expansion:** The number of CAG repeats increased from 40 in the father to 45 in the son (patient).
*   **Anticipation:** The age of onset decreased from the paternal grandfather (late 50s) to the father (35) to the patient (42). This phenomenon, where symptoms appear earlier or are more severe in successive generations, is called anticipation.
*   **Daughter's Result:** The daughter inherited the allele from her father (45 repeats) but has 38 repeats. This indicates a contraction occurred during her father's gametogenesis or her mother's gametogenesis (since she inherited the normal allele from her mother). However, the overall pattern points towards expansion.
*   **Mother & Brother:** Unaffected, with normal repeat numbers (15 and 20 repeats, respectively), confirming the disease is linked to the expanded allele and is autosomal dominant.

The key observation is the increase in CAG repeats from father to son (40 -> 45) coupled with the earlier age of onset in the son compared to his father and grandfather. This pattern is characteristic of dynamic mutation/anticipation in trinucleotide repeat disorders like Huntington's disease.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
Huntington's disease is caused by an expansion of a CAG trinucleotide repeat in the *HTT* gene (located on chromosome 4). The normal allele typically has 10-35 CAG repeats. Alleles with 36-39 repeats are considered to have reduced penetrance, while alleles with ≥40 repeats are fully penetrant and cause HD.

The expanded CAG repeat leads to the production of a mutant huntingtin protein with an elongated polyglutamine tract. This mutant protein misfolds, aggregates, and is toxic to neurons, particularly in the striatum, leading to the characteristic motor, cognitive, and psychiatric symptoms.

The key feature relevant to this question is *anticipation*. Anticipation occurs because the expanded CAG repeat is unstable and tends to change in size during transmission from one generation to the next, particularly during meiosis (gamete formation). This instability is primarily due to slippage of the DNA polymerase during replication within the germline cells.

*   **Mechanism of Expansion:** During DNA replication, the polymerase can "slip" on the repetitive sequence, either adding extra repeats (expansion) or deleting repeats (contraction). This slippage is more pronounced with longer repeat tracts.
*   **Gametogenesis:** The expansion/contraction events occur primarily during meiosis in the formation of sperm and eggs.
*   **Paternal Bias:** In Huntington's disease, expansions tend to occur more frequently and to a greater extent during spermatogenesis (paternal transmission) compared to oogenesis (maternal transmission). This is why anticipation is often more pronounced when the disease is inherited from an affected father.
*   **Patient's Case:** The father had 40 repeats and the son has 45 repeats (expansion). The age of onset decreased (father 35, son 42). This fits the pattern of anticipation due to dynamic mutation expansion during paternal meiosis. The daughter's result (38 repeats) shows that contraction can also occur, but the overall trend in the paternal line is expansion and earlier onset.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Genomic imprinting** involves differential expression of genes depending on the parent of origin. While imprinting affects some genetic disorders (e.g., Prader-Willi/Angelman syndromes), Huntington's disease is not an imprinted disorder. The *HTT* gene is expressed from both maternal and paternal alleles, and the disease severity is related to the number of repeats on *both* alleles (though one expanded allele is usually sufficient for full penetrance). Imprinting does not explain the repeat expansion or anticipation.
- (B): **Meiotic recombination hotspots** can increase the frequency of crossing over. While recombination occurs near genes, it doesn't directly cause the expansion or contraction of microsatellite repeats like CAG repeats. Gene conversion, a process associated with recombination, *can* sometimes alter repeat numbers, but the primary mechanism for instability in trinucleotide repeat disorders is polymerase slippage during replication, not recombination hotspots.
- (C): **DNA methylation patterns** are epigenetic modifications that can influence gene expression. While changes in methylation can occur in some genetic disorders or cancers, they are not the primary mechanism driving the dynamic instability (expansion/contraction) of CAG repeats in Huntington's disease. The instability is primarily due to the inherent properties of the repetitive DNA sequence during replication.
- (D): **Somatic cell replication slippage** can occur, potentially leading to mosaicism (different repeat lengths in different tissues). However, the phenomenon of anticipation (changes in repeat number and age of onset across *generations*) is primarily driven by events occurring during *meiosis* in the germline, not somatic cells. Somatic instability might influence disease progression within an individual, but it doesn't explain the intergenerational pattern.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key takeaway is understanding dynamic mutations (trinucleotide repeat expansions) and the phenomenon of anticipation. This occurs due to instability of the repeat sequence during DNA replication, particularly in the germline (meiosis), leading to changes in repeat number and often earlier onset/increased severity in subsequent generations. Huntington's disease is the classic example, often showing paternal transmission bias.

**Differentials:**
*   **Fragile X syndrome:** Another trinucleotide repeat disorder (CGG repeat in *FMR1* gene), also exhibits anticipation, but typically shows maternal transmission bias and is associated with intellectual disability.
*   **Myotonic dystrophy:** Trinucleotide repeat disorder (CTG repeat in *DMPK* gene), exhibits anticipation.
*   **Spinocerebellar ataxias (SCAs):** Several types are caused by trinucleotide repeat expansions (e.g., SCA1, SCA2, SCA3), often showing anticipation.
*   **Non-trinucleotide repeat disorders:** Many other genetic disorders exist, but anticipation is characteristic of dynamic mutations involving trinucleotide repeats.

<a id='question-150'></a>

### Question 150: Genetic Anticipation: Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1 (DM1)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Genetic anticipation, specifically in the context of trinucleotide repeat expansion disorders like Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1 (DM1), characterized by progressive worsening of symptoms and earlier age of onset in successive generations due to unstable repeat expansion during gametogenesis. (遗传性预期，特别是甲型肌营养不良症 (DM1) 这种三核苷酸重复序列扩张疾病，其特征是由于生殖细胞中不稳定的重复序列扩张，导致后代症状逐渐加重、发病年龄提前。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male presents to his physician complaining of progressive muscle weakness, myotonia (difficulty relaxing muscles after contraction), cataracts, and cardiac conduction abnormalities. His symptoms began in his late 40s. His father, who passed away at age 75, had similar symptoms that started in his late 50s. However, his paternal grandfather had symptoms that began in his early 60s. The patient's 35-year-old son has recently developed mild muscle weakness and myotonia, with symptoms starting in his late 20s. Genetic testing reveals an expansion of a CTG trinucleotide repeat in the *DMPK* gene. The patient's repeat count is 1500 copies, his father's was 1000 copies, his paternal grandfather's was 700 copies, and his son's is 2000 copies.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the observed pattern of disease presentation in this family?

(A) Genomic imprinting, where the expression of the mutated allele is dependent on its parental origin, leading to variable expressivity.
(B) Uniparental disomy, resulting in the inheritance of two copies of the chromosome carrying the normal allele, masking the effect of the mutated allele.
(C) Trinucleotide repeat expansion instability during meiosis, leading to an increased number of repeats in subsequent generations, causing earlier onset and increased severity.
(D) Somatic mosaicism, where the mutation arises post-zygotically, leading to variable expression depending on the proportion of affected cells.
(E) X-chromosome inactivation, resulting in skewed lyonization, where one X chromosome is preferentially inactivated, leading to variable expression in females.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 62-year-old male with myotonia, muscle weakness, cataracts, cardiac issues, onset in late 40s. This constellation of symptoms is highly suggestive of Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1 (DM1).
- **Family History:** Father had similar symptoms, onset in late 50s. Paternal grandfather had symptoms, onset in early 60s. Son has symptoms, onset in late 20s. This pattern shows a clear trend: symptoms are appearing at progressively earlier ages in successive generations. This is the hallmark of genetic anticipation.
- **Genetic Testing:** Expansion of a CTG trinucleotide repeat in the *DMPK* gene. This confirms the diagnosis of DM1, which is caused by a CTG repeat expansion in the 3' untranslated region of the *DMPK* gene.
- **Repeat Counts:** Patient (1500), Father (1000), Grandfather (700), Son (2000). The repeat count has increased from grandfather to father, decreased from father to patient, but dramatically increased from father to son. The key observation is the correlation between repeat size and age of onset (larger repeat = earlier onset) and the intergenerational change in repeat size.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the pattern. The pattern is genetic anticipation.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **DM1 Pathophysiology:** DM1 is an autosomal dominant disorder caused by an expansion of a CTG trinucleotide repeat in the 3' untranslated region (UTR) of the *DMPK* gene on chromosome 19.
- **Normal vs. Mutant Alleles:** Normal individuals have <37 CTG repeats. Individuals with DM1 have >50 repeats. The number of repeats correlates inversely with the age of onset (more repeats = earlier onset) and directly with disease severity.
- **Genetic Anticipation Mechanism:** The CTG repeats are prone to expansion during DNA replication, particularly during meiosis (gametogenesis). The enzyme responsible for replication slippage is DNA polymerase. The G-rich repeat sequence can form secondary structures (hairpins) that stall the polymerase, leading to misincorporation of nucleotides and expansion of the repeat tract.
- **Inheritance Pattern:** The expanded allele is passed from parent to offspring. During gamete formation (meiosis), the repeat sequence can expand further, especially when inherited from the father (anticipation is often more pronounced with paternal transmission, although it can occur with maternal transmission too). The son inherited the allele from his father, which had 1000 repeats. It expanded to 2000 repeats in the son's germline, leading to an earlier age of onset (late 20s) compared to his father (late 40s) and grandfather (early 60s). The patient's repeat count (1500) is larger than his father's (1000), but his onset was later (late 40s vs late 50s), suggesting that the relationship between repeat size and age of onset is not perfectly linear and other factors might influence penetrance and expressivity. However, the overall trend across three generations clearly demonstrates genetic anticipation due to trinucleotide repeat expansion instability.
- **Molecular Basis:** The expansion occurs due to slippage of the DNA polymerase during replication, particularly in regions with repetitive sequences. This is a known mechanism for several neurodegenerative disorders caused by trinucleotide repeat expansions (e.g., Huntington's disease, Fragile X syndrome, DM1, Friedreich's ataxia).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Genomic imprinting:** This is an epigenetic phenomenon where gene expression depends on the parent of origin. While some disorders show imprinting (e.g., Prader-Willi/Angelman syndrome), DM1 is not primarily explained by imprinting. The CTG repeat expansion itself is the cause, and anticipation occurs regardless of parental origin, although the *degree* of anticipation can sometimes differ between paternal and maternal transmission. Imprinting would lead to different phenotypes based on parent of origin, not necessarily earlier onset in successive generations.
- (B) **Uniparental disomy (UPD):** This occurs when an individual inherits two copies of a chromosome (or part of a chromosome) from one parent and no copies from the other parent. UPD can cause disease if it involves imprinted genes or if it leads to homozygosity for a recessive mutation. It does not explain the progressive expansion of a trinucleotide repeat and earlier onset seen in DM1.
- (D) **Somatic mosaicism:** This refers to the presence of two or more cell lines with different genotypes within a single individual, arising from a mutation occurring after fertilization. While somatic mosaicism can affect disease expression, it does not explain the pattern of genetic anticipation observed across multiple generations, which is a germline phenomenon (occurring in sperm or egg cells).
- (E) **X-chromosome inactivation (Lyonization):** This process occurs in females (XX) where one X chromosome is randomly inactivated in each cell. Skewed X-inactivation can lead to variable expression of X-linked disorders. DM1 is an autosomal dominant disorder (chromosome 19), not X-linked, so X-inactivation is irrelevant to its inheritance pattern or the mechanism of anticipation.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Genetic anticipation is a key feature of trinucleotide repeat expansion disorders. The mechanism involves instability of the repeat sequence during DNA replication in germ cells, leading to expansion in subsequent generations, correlating with earlier age of onset and increased severity. Key examples include Myotonic Dystrophy Type 1 (CTG repeat in *DMPK*), Huntington's disease (CAG repeat in *HTT*), Fragile X syndrome (CGG repeat in *FMR1*), and Friedreich's ataxia (GAA repeat in *FXN*). Remember that anticipation is most pronounced with large repeat expansions and often shows parent-of-origin effects (e.g., more severe with paternal transmission in Huntington's and DM1).
